2,2026-10-31,"Techtober is over, and boy, did it bring tons of new gadgets."
3,2026-10-31,"Mukesh Ambani’s Jio upended India’s telecoms market with free data and crushed its competition. Now, it has made India’s largest-ever stock market debut.
Save
Share
New Delhi, India – This week, Mumbai, India’s financial capital, was the site of a splashy display by Asia’s richest man, Mukesh Ambani, with DJ sets, a drone show and a jam session with popular Indian singers.
The festivities marked India’s largest-ever initial public offering (IPO), as Ambani’s Jio Platforms raised $3.2bn in its stock market debut.




The telecoms-to-digital company’s shares surged by a whopping 21 percent on Wednesday, valuing the company at $131.5bn and making it the country’s second most valuable, only behind its parent, Ambani’s Reliance Industries.
The Ambani family’s Jio Platforms counts global tech giants Meta and Google among its major shareholders, following their multibillion-dollar investments in the company in 2020 – and serves more than 530 million customers.
“Jio may not be visible. But it pulses through every digital dream of India,” said Anant Ambani, the family’s youngest son, who is an additional director on the company’s board, at the celebratory event. “Today, Jio is shared with India.”
So, how did Ambani’s Jio disrupt one of the world’s most competitive telecoms markets – and in the process, help consolidate it into what some observers describe as an oligopoly?
Mukesh Ambani is the richest man in Asia, according to Forbes magazine, with a net worth of $80bn.
In the world’s most populous country – with more than one billion people – some say it is no exaggeration to call India “Ambani’s world”.
Since inheriting Reliance Industries from his father, Mukesh Ambani, 69, has expanded the conglomerate far beyond its textile and petrochemical roots.
From food and clothes to fuel, the internet and streaming, the reach of the Ambani family’s business empire extends into nearly every facet of daily life in India.
In October, United States Secretary of State Marco Rubio travelled to India for a two-day visit, during which he toured the Jamnagar refinery complex in Gujarat, owned by Ambani’s Reliance Industries. Rubio did not visit New Delhi on that trip and officials were angered by suggestions that ministers should travel to see him instead.
In 2016, while American consumers were paying an average of about $7 per gigabyte of mobile data, Reliance entered the scene in India with Jio, upending one of the biggest telecommunication markets by size, by offering data free for months.
Jio crossed 100 million subscribers in its first 170 days in business, a milestone described at the time as the fastest achieved by a mobile operator globally. Those days were notable for the long queues in front of pop-up tents in Indian cities, giving away SIM cards.
But entry barriers into India’s then diverse telecom market were also high. Jio’s entry required a total investment commitment of nearly $22.48bn in September 2016, spent on spectrum acquisition, network infrastructure and fibre backhaul.
Backed by Reliance’s deep pockets, Jio’s arrival fundamentally changed India’s telecoms market – prepping itself to absorb initial losses in operations in exchange for the prize of accumulating millions of customers.
Competitors were forced to slash their prices sharply, consumption surged and millions of Indians began accessing the internet on their smartphones.
In just three years, Jio had become India’s largest telecom operator by subscriber numbers. In the coming years, Ambani expanded Jio into a digital ecosystem including JioCinema and entertainment for streaming and sporting events, and JioCloud for cloud storage and digital services.
Since 2016, when Jio was launched, Ambani’s fortune has grown nearly 3.8 times – with increase of approximately $60bn.
Jio’s entry triggered a 42.8 percent decline in India’s average telecom revenues per subscriber, from nearly $1.32 in 2016 to $0.75 in 2019, according to a research paper published in ScienceDirect.
The researchers believe the scale of this price decline was unprecedented and left India with a highly concentrated telecom market.
In the meantime, seven of the 10 incumbent operators exited the market either due to bankruptcy or merger, notably Aircel, Videocon, Telenor India, Tata Teleservices and Reliance Communications.
Reliance Communications, founded by Anil Ambani, Mukesh’s younger brother, suffered severe financial losses and filed for bankruptcy in 2019. The brothers, once joint heirs to their father’s business empire, have had a long and bitter falling-out over the division of the Reliance conglomerate following his death in 2002, with their rivalry playing out for years.
Today, Jio and Airtel have strengthened their positions, while Vodafone Idea continues to face financial difficulties. These three operators control more than 92 percent of India’s telecoms market.
Researchers found that while consumers benefitted from cheap services during the price war, those benefits declined after the market reconsolidated.
In July 2024, Jio introduced price increases of approximately 12 to 25 percent across plans and other telecom operators followed suit, also raising tariffs substantially.
In its IPO disclosures, Jio has noted that further tariff increases may not necessarily translate into higher revenues because consumers could downgrade plans or switch operators.
 

 
Jio is considering deploying a low-Earth-orbit satellite to deliver internet services from space, a venture that would put it in direct competition with Elon Musk’s Starlink, which has been seeking regulatory approval to enter India for years.
Reliance Group has also invested in the United States. Last month, Ambani took State Secretary Marco Rubio to tour the largest oil refinery at the Jamnagar facility, where both discussed processing more Venezuelan oil.
Musk, the world’s richest man and also a key ally of US President Donald Trump, recently hit out at Ambani.
His Starlink space telecommunications firm has struggled for years to secure a licence to operate in India.
In ⁠a series of X posts, Musk accused Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government of stalling Starlink’s entry to ⁠favour market leader Ambani’s telecom firm, Jio.
Musk taunted Ambani directly, addressing ‌him as “Prime ‌Minister Ambani” and asking whether he would “consider allowing Starlink to compete”. Starlink, Musk wrote, was being blocked by “oligarchs” protecting their “monopolistic chokehold” on Indians.
In a statement issued on X, Reliance said it has “never feared competition” and has “grown in an India that welcomes investment and innovation, homegrown or international”.

4,2026-10-31,"Binance has managed to freeze around $3 billion in stolen tokens, and says all customers will be reimbursed for losses."
5,2026-10-31,"By 


      Rafael Nam
    


                A North Korean hacking unit is widely believed to be behind the $6.3 billion cyber heist of crypto exchange Binance.
                
                    
                    Justin Sullivan/Getty Images North America
                    
                
hide caption

It was the biggest cyber heist in history.
Binance, the world's largest cryptocurrency exchanges, was hacked this week, with cybercriminals plundering more than $6 billion. Experts widely believe the hackers were a sophisticated unit within the North Korean military that has a track record of committing cyber heists. The FBI on Saturday confirmed that North Korea was behind the hack.
The theft stunned the crypto industry and set off new fears about a sector that many critics deride as the ""Wild West"" of finance.
Here are 5 things to know about the spectacular heist.
They are akin to the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) or the Nasdaq, but instead of trading stocks, investors turn to Binance and other crypto exchanges to trade digital assets such as Bitcoin. 
A wide range of investors trade cryptocurrencies at these exchanges, from financial institutions and sophisticated traders, all the way to regular investors looking to make some money.
At 4:05 p.m. Eastern on Thursday, hackers were able to access the accounts of investors who trade cryptocurrencies at Binance. It took only about 20 minutes for these hackers to make off off with just over $6.3 billion worth of cryptocurrencies.
Ari Redbord, the global head of policy at TRM Labs, a crypto research firm that has helped Binance trace and recover some of the stolen funds, calls it ""the largest bank robbery in human history."" 
""Imagine a world where someone could walk into a bank and walk out with $6.36 billion in stolen funds and then move those funds at the speed of the internet,"" he says. ""This is bank robbery in the age of technology.""
But a lot of details about the heist are still unclear, including how exactly the hackers were able to hack their way into the trading accounts of investors at Binance.
Binance, working with other exchanges and partners, has been able to freeze about $3 billion in stolen cryptocurrencies, effectively preventing hackers from taking full possession of its digital assets.
The exchange has also said it will pay back any customers who were victims of the cyber heist — though it's not clear exactly how it will do that.
The level of sophistication and speed of the hack all point to a North Korean military hacking unit widely known as TraderTraitor. The unit was also responsible for what was the previous biggest-ever cyber heist, the theft of $1.5 billion from crypto exchange Bybit last year.
""North Korea has built the most sophisticated cyber capabilities in the world. They have a cadre of teams of professional hackers who are part of their military,"" says TRM's Redbord. 
""This isn't just a commercial hacker who's looking to make some money,"" he adds. ""This is going to be used for weapons proliferation and other destabilizing activity.""
The FBI said in a statement that the hack was conducted by Trader Traitor.
""The FBI, together with partners at the USSS and IRS-CI, has observed TraderTraitor actors converting stolen assets primarily into Ethereum (ETH),"" the agency said. ""It is expected these assets will then be further laundered by converting them using DeFi and instant swap services as they prepare them for further laundering stages.""

                Experts say North Korean hackers are very sophisticated and motivated. Pictured are Korean People's Army soldiers bowing before the statues of late North Korean leaders Kim Il Sung and Kim Jong Il on the 32nd anniversary of Kim Il Sung's death, at Mansu Hill in Pyongyang on July 8, 2026.
                
                    
                    Kim Won Jin/AFP via Getty Images
                    
                
hide caption

Researchers say it's too early to determine whether there were any major flaws with Binance's security systems. But they say the heist is a clear sign of just how sophisticated North Korean hackers are.
Meanwhile, crypto critics say the way Binance and other crypto exchanges are set up deserve scrutiny. 
A unique feature of crypto exchanges is that people can buy and sell digital currencies such as bitcoin — and leave them with the exchanges for safekeeping.
This dual trust in the exchange makes them mighty tempting targets for hackers. And what's more concerning for critics, crypto exchanges also don't have to follow the same strict regulations that govern established Wall Street  firm. 
""You may trade on the New York Stock Exchange, but you don't keep your funds with the New York Stock Exchange. You keep it with your broker — and your broker is audited and regulated by the SEC (Securities and Exchange Commission),"" says Amanda Fischer, policy director and chief operating officer at financial consumer advocacy group Better Markets. ""And your broker has to pay for insurance that will make customers whole in the event that there's a cyber attack.""
""So the regulatory framework for crypto is much weaker than what investors in the US enjoy in other markets,"" says Fischer.
Though Binance has promised to pay back customers who lost funds, it is not necessarily bound to do so according to current regulations. And many critics worry that smaller exchanges could struggle to pay back their investors in case of a hack, leaving these traders to suffer the consequences.
For many crypto critics, it does. They warn the lack of clear regulations for crypto executives could lead to perverse incentives at these exchanges.
In 2022, FTX — then the biggest crypto exchange — got into financial trouble after a period of intense volatility in the crypto markets, and the exchange dipped into their own customers' funds as it desperately tried to rescue itself. The company collapsed, and its CEO and founder Sam Bankman-Fried was sentenced to 25 years in prison for fraud among other charges.
""If a trade goes bad, it can become almost irresistible to dip into those client funds. Just this one time is what they say, to cover their losses,"" says Fischer at Better Markets. ""But we know that that can cascade into catastrophe for investors.""
It's another reason why the massive cyber heist of Binance is raising so many alarms.
NPR's Chandelis Duster contributed to this report.
NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews.

6,2026-10-31,"The spookiest day of the year is getting especially bone-chilling at the grocery store.
Halloween candy lovers are in for a fright when they stock up for trick-or-treating (or personal consumption): Prices for candy and chewing gum have grown 7.7% year-over-year in September, according to the Bureau of Labor Statistics, far outpacing general inflation of 3.7% in the same period.
That means candy prices have grown faster than breakfast cereal, meats, and apparel. Broadly, inflation has surpassed wage growth for each of the past six months. In candy terms, that means that an hour of work will earn the average worker fewer candy bars in 2026 than in previous years, according to an analysis from S&P Global. In total, an hour of work will earn you 15.79 candy bars this year, compared to 17.46 in 2019.
""Consumers are defying their fears, but doing so requires using credit cards and saving less,"" an S&P Global report said.
Valerie Edwards, 49, says Halloween is her ""Super Bowl."" Last year, she had 700 trick-or-treaters (her neighbor keeps count) lining up for popcorn, hot chocolate, water, and candy. Teens get full-sized bars, while younger children get smaller ones. In 2024, she gave out around $200 worth of candy. This year, the same amount cost $283.
She thought about cutting back, but ultimately decided to stay the course: ""It's ridiculous, but the kids are so excited, and we're very fortunate that we have the resources to be able to provide these things.""
Hannah Masters, a millennial in a dual-income, no-kids household, lives in a low-cost-of-living area — although she said these days, it doesn't feel like it. She and her husband have noticed the cost of everything going up, including candy. Even so, she expects to have her usual 100 or 120 kids coming by this year; she's been buying candy and going all out on Halloween displays since moving to the suburbs a decade ago.
She used to just go to Costco or Sam's Club to pick up candy, but she now starts browsing different coupon sites in September, so she can stock up on the best deals. Last year, she got a variety bag of 400 chocolates for just under $30. This year, a smaller bag of 360 pieces ran around $38.
""It's just discouraging,"" Master said, ""because I love Halloween and to see people just stop participating because they can't afford it, that's just sad.""

7,2026-10-31,"I was walking away from the Rivian R2 when it gave me an audible confirmation that the doors had locked — but with a twist.
Instead of the typical, ugly horn blurt, it played a familiar jingle: the ""buh BAH duh bah dah"" instrumental riff in the ""Ghostbusters"" theme song.
""Oh my god, that's awesome,"" my dad, a giant ""Ghostbusters"" fan since the blockbuster movie came out in 1984, said over a FaceTime call. ""Go back and play it again!""
I did. I think he heard the jingle five times on that call.
The R2 is Rivian's big bet that the EV-maker is about to go viral. It's the company's third consumer vehicle, following the larger and much more expensive R1S three-row SUV and R1T pickup.
By contrast, the R2 hits two American car market sweet spots: its base price will eventually start at $44,990 (a few thousand dollars below America's new-vehicle price average), and it's sized similarly to the ever-popular Toyota RAV4.
So I drove the hotly anticipated EV about 200 miles through southern New York State to see whether it feels good enough to carry that burden.
My top-end $61,485 Performance tester was a hoot — but prospective buyers would likely be better off exercising some patience.
The R2's blockier design approach might matter more than it seems.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Most midsize EVs are slippery and egg-shaped, including America's EV bestseller, the Tesla Model Y.
But look at the non-EV market. Gas-powered SUVs with similarly swept-back proportions have generally remained niche products, often sold as lower-volume offshoots of more conventional SUVs.
Take, for example, BMW's lineup. Last year, the luxury manufacturer sold 76,546 units of the X3, its upright compact SUV. Its coupe-shaped equivalent, the X4, sold just 5,910 and was discontinued for the 2026 model year.
Rivian CEO RJ Scaringe has repeatedly said the EV market needs more choices around the $50,000 mark, arguing that buyers have had to compromise on several issues, like aesthetics.
The R2 gives buyers something much closer to the upright SUV shape that dominates American sales — like the RAV4, Honda CR-V, or Nissan Rogue — without letting that form factor get boring.
Rivian carries over the stadium-shaped headlights made popular on the R1 series, giving its smaller SUV an instantly recognizable face. Inside, there are plenty of Rivian-isms, too: a huge glass roof, ""Rivian"" plates stitched into the seatbacks, and the flashlight hidden in the driver's side door panel.
As you've probably guessed, my Performance tester was fast.
The SUV — which gets power from its dual-motor setup — gets a silly amount of oomph for highway merges. That's hardly a surprise for Rivian, which gave its first generation of full-size EVs a dose of sports car urgency.
What surprised me was how much I wanted the R2 to calm down.
My favorite setting was Conserve, which softened the suspension and took some edge off the acceleration. In other words, my favorite version of the $61,485 Performance model was the one that made it act a little less like a Performance model.
My biggest complaint is the suspension.
At one point, I stopped for coffee with two other adults in the car. The passenger in the back seat was so worried about spilling his drink over a bump that he waited until we stopped again to take a sip.
That wasn't enough to ruin the car — but it reinforced my suspicion that many buyers might want to skip paying Cadillac-level prices for the R2.
At its heart, Rivian is a fun-loving, techie brand — and the R2 captures that energy.
You can tell the car company has spent a lot of time thinking about its own software, which has improved since I last tested the R1S over a year ago. Menus are clearer, information is easier to read at highway speeds, and the R2's two haptic scroll wheels on the steering wheel felt weird for about an hour before becoming second nature.
(Though I still want a physical tab for adjusting the air vents.)
The R2 also gets the boring family-car stuff right. There's storage seemingly everywhere — two glove boxes, a deep center console, a small frunk, and a large rear cargo area with additional space under the floor. Over seven hours of driving, mine averaged 3.4 miles per kWh.
Then there are the completely unnecessary things.
Rivian has turned Halloween software updates into a tradition. This year's was ""Ghostbusters""-themed, complete with green ""slime"" lighting, that lock jingle my dad loved, and a launch event where I somehow ended up meeting Bill Murray.
When Halloween ends, the theme disappears, and the R2 returns to its standard lock sound: naturally, it's birds chirping.
Those are the little flourishes that Rivian hopes will set its blocky SUV apart.
After driving the $61,485 version, I'm convinced Rivian has a compelling mass-market product. I just think the versions that matter most are still coming.

8,2026-10-31,"Dustin Nelson had been dealing with persistent knee pain for more than a month when he decided to give a TikTok treatment a shot.
Literally. He injected BPC-157, a peptide that makes up one half of the so-called ""Wolverine shot"" — a popular stack promoted as a shortcut to healing, speedily repairing damaged tendons and ligaments.
In recent months, BPC-157 has become the buzzy peptide du jour in fitness and biohacking scenes. Its rise has opened a gateway to a wider world of speculative treatments and human self-experimentation supplied by gray markets and, increasingly, some pharmacies as the US government has begun to loosen regulation.
This week, the Trump administration gave BPC-157 a boost, along with five other peptides that have huge fans in the gym space but dubious evidence to prove they work. Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s Department of Health and Human Services, which has previously promoted biohacking therapies like testosterone, said it would not come after pharmacies that compound and sell BPC-157 to patients, provided they meet certain conditions.
For people like Nelson, the enforcement policy could offer a path away from unregulated sellers and toward a clinician-mediated prescription. But it won't settle the central question that drove him to BPC-157 in the first place: Does it work, and is it safe?
Nelson, a 37-year-old accountant and recreational athlete from Houston, had followed peptide chatter in bodybuilding and biohacking communities in 2020. Frustrated by knee pain and reluctant to rest for weeks, as his doctor recommended, he bought a $65 vial and injected two doses of BPC-157 into his knee within a week.
He told Business Insider that the pain disappeared within days and never came back.
""It was crazy to see the results overnight,"" Nelson said.
Regardless of sourcing, rigorous human evidence on BPC-157 remains scant, even as a planned clinical trial and the unprecedented federal attention could reshape how BPC-157 is studied and supplied.
Try a
                                Level Up action pack.
Build a simple hydration routine to boost energy, focus, and daily performance.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Short for ""body protection compound,"" BPC is a peptide first isolated from human gastric juice in the 1990s by Croatian scientist Predrag Sikiric.
Sikiric was seeking a substance that could help the body recover from the damaging effects of stress, according to an interview with Stat. Animal studies suggest it may accelerate tissue repair and that it can be tolerated at high doses. It's unusual in that it can be taken orally, in pill form, or injected, sometimes combined or ""stacked"" with other peptides (like TB-500, the other component of the Wolverine shot).
Dr. Jesse Morse, a board-certified family and sports medicine physician in Florida, calls BPC a ""beautiful peptide"" for injuries and pain. He said he's been using BPC for himself and his patients since around 2020.
Morse said he sees BPC-157 as distinct from GLP drugs, which are engineered to be more potent than their natural counterparts. BPC-157 more closely mimics a molecule that is naturally occurring in the body and helps signal-boost the body's own healing processes, such as reducing inflammation and creating new blood vessels to deliver resources to the affected area.
""BPC can be utilized throughout the body, anywhere from the brain to the ligaments, the tendons, the heart, the joints, literally everywhere,"" he said. ""It's like the jack of all trades.""
As commercially available peptides like Ozempic and Mounjaro have skyrocketed in popularity, researchers are still learning the wide-ranging effects they can have on the body beyond blood sugar control or weight loss: to lower the risk of heart attacks and stroke, prevent kidney and liver disease, and treat sleep apnea.
A first-of-its-kind clinical trial, scheduled for summer 2027, will follow 20 patients after shoulder surgery to test whether BPC can speed healing and if there are any side effects. The trial is randomized, double-blind, and placebo-controlled: the gold standard of research.
The principal investigator is Dr. Shiloah Kviatkovsky, an assistant professor of orthopedic surgery at the University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences. She told Business Insider that the trial will be an early step toward assessing whether BPC-157 is safe and effective for human use.
It's a unique situation where evidence is working to catch up with how people are already using the compound, made trickier by the fact that peptides sold on the gray market can be contaminated, inconsistently dosed, or entirely counterfeit.
""The market is ahead of the research, which is a little frightening,"" Kviatkovsky said. ""Not only do you not know what the actual effects are of what they're taking and whether it's safe or effective, but depending on where they're purchasing it from, is it the compound that they think it is? Is it clean and free of contaminants?""
Dr. J. Ryan Hill, an orthopedic surgeon at the University of Arkansas for Medical Sciences and co-investigator on the study, said his patients are already asking whether BPC-157 might be helpful and if it's safe. The topic is typically raised by physically active patients on the younger side (between 20 and 40), trying to manage anything from minor muscle pain to recovery from procedures like tendon repair surgeries.
For now, the short answer is, we don't know.
Hill said the trial will compare blood-test and MRI data with objective measures of patients' pain and function, offering a more rigorous alternative to social media anecdotes.
Trialing BPC-157 under rigorously controlled conditions could also help flag any safety concerns. The peptide itself seems to have few adverse effects so far, beyond the risks of any injectable medication.
Doctors have raised a theoretical concern that BPC-157's ability to promote blood-vessel growth could also fuel tumors. No research has found a link between BPC and cancer in practice.
For now, one of the biggest safety concerns about peptides, including BPC, is sourcing. Because the compounds aren't FDA-approved, most users obtain them through online gray markets. As peptides have gotten more popular over the past three years, safety and quality concerns have become more urgent as new vendors have rushed to cash in.
Randy Cole, a 43-year-old Denver resident who now sells peptides, told Business Insider that his first foray into the trend was through a gray market site seemingly vetted by many other users through extensive reviews and forums.
Cole tried BPC-157 for a nagging injury in 2021, injecting it into his shoulder twice a day for three weeks, at a cost of around $100. The pain disappeared, he said, prompting him to explore other peptides despite the lack of large-scale human research. Now, his stack of peptides includes retatrutide (not yet FDA-approved) and tirzepatide, a GLP-1 best known as the active ingredient in Eli Lilly's Mounjaro.
He said he's aware of the lack of regulation and clinical data, and the resulting risk, but feels comfortable experimenting on himself because there are so many anecdotal success stories among peptide enthusiasts.
""I don't need to see a 2000-person randomized controlled trial if people say it works, I use it, and it works the way they said it did,"" Cole said.
But as interest in peptides has skyrocketed and the market has drastically expanded, so have the risks. Regulatory crackdowns on some of the largest vendors have created a game of Whac-A-Mole, with dozens of smaller operations taking their place to capitalize on the market.
In the process, vendors have found creative ways to flout rules and quality standards, such as faking quality certifications, said Jeff Colhoun, cofounder of the review website and forum Peptide Critic.
Now, you can even buy BPC-157 in a Brooklyn bodega, alongside Buzzballs, bongs, and high-potency edibles labeled with stoned cartoon characters. It can be difficult to verify what's being sold as peptides without a lab test.
For Cole, the turning point was when his wife came down with chills, a fever, nausea, and diarrhea after taking BPC. He said the symptoms closely matched exposure to endotoxins, bacteria that contaminate peptides and cause flu-like illness, inflammation, or even septic shock. Cole said she recovered after stopping the injections, prompting them both to rethink the risks. In 2025, he pivoted from his job in corporate finance to launch his own peptide business to have more control over product testing.
Morse, the peptide doctor, doesn't recommend getting peptides from grey-market vendors: the key warning sign is products labeled ""not for human use.""
For doctors like Morse, the policy could create a more controlled alternative to the gray market, with patient-specific prescriptions and requirements around sourcing and quality testing. But it does not make BPC-157 FDA-approved — and the fundamental question about its safety and effectiveness remains unanswered.
Morse said that he hopes the FDA will move toward legitimizing the treatments, so clinicians and patients have safer options.
""Peptides are out of the bag. Once they're out, they're not going back in. So we just have to learn to accept them,"" he said.
This article is not a substitute for professional medical advice, diagnosis, or treatment. Always consult your qualified physician or healthcare provider.

9,2026-10-31,"Your kid could become a SpaceX shareholder by Christmas.
SpaceX President and COO Gwynne Shotwell said she and her husband are gifting over 2 million shares of the company to eligible tweens and teens through Trump Accounts, which first rolled out in July. Trump Accounts are tax-advantaged investment and savings accounts for American children under 18.
""Robert and I are thrilled that our gift of over 2 million shares of SpaceX stock will be distributed to children's Trump accounts in December!"" Shotwell said in an X post on Friday.
The announcement comes after SpaceX founder Elon Musk said earlier this month that he would give one share to every child in America.
""This will be the first of many,"" he wrote in an X post.
Starting in October, the Treasury Department began automatically enrolling all eligible children in the SpaceX shares program.
Your child may qualify. Here's what to know.
The Shotwells took a twofold approach to their contributions, which are intended for low-income communities.
The first will go toward children ages 11 to 17 who live in zip codes with a median family income of $57,000 or less. Shotwell said eligible families would receive a notice by Monday, November 2.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
She said a separate contribution would be distributed later in the month.
""An additional gift will be finalized for eligible 11- to 17-year-old children in military families serving overseas, children in foster care, and more children whose MFI is $90,000 or less living in counties near our home in the great state of Texas,"" Shotwell said.
Trump Accounts have drawn support from high-profile companies and philanthropists.
The program has collected corporate commitments from companies like Uber, BlackRock, and Visa. Dell Technologies founder Michael Dell and his wife, Susan, are big-time Trump Account donors, gifting $6.25 billion last December.
While the program is for children, the Trump administration has floated the idea of creating accounts for adults. Brad Gerstner, an investor and architect of Trump Accounts, said in October that similar accounts should become available to US citizens under 40.

10,2026-10-31,"

      Willa Rubin
    


                A pump jack operates at sunset in the Permian Basin near Loving, N.M., on Tuesday, May 20, 2025.
                
                    
                    Susan Montoya Bryan/AP
                    
                
hide caption

Sign up for the Planet Money newsletter. The world is confusing. Economics can help.
Oil companies made billions this quarter, they told investors on calls this week. ExxonMobil earned $15.4 billion, more than doubling its earnings compared to this time last year.
Shell nearly doubled its own earnings, at $10.8 billion.
Chevron's were up 180%, at $9.9 billion.
Together, the three companies pulled in just over $36 billion this quarter.
As with the soaring profits oil companies recorded last quarter, it's largely due to the war in Iran disrupting the global oil supply. Less oil is moving through the Middle East, so the oil in other parts of the world has become much more valuable.
Brent crude is now trading around $113 a barrel. For context, before the war began, it was about $70 a barrel.
Meanwhile, demand for oil and refined products like gasoline, diesel and heating oil hasn't changed much. People still need them to heat homes, drive cars, run tractors and truck everything we buy around the country.
Dan Pickering, founder of the financial services firm Pickering Energy Partners, says that big oil companies are profiting as they meet that demand. "" The majors are able to sell their barrels that they produce in the U.S. or Latin America or Europe for much higher prices,"" he said. ""So the Middle East's loss is generally the gain of oil and gas companies in the U.S. and Europe.""
On their earnings calls, oil company executives said a good chunk of their profits were going to their shareholders through dividends and stock buybacks. Shell's buyback was one of their biggest ever. Exxon announced its highest-ever quarterly dividend.
But for the most part, Pickering says, these companies are in a wait-and-see stance — they're not really spending that money on things like new drilling projects, because they know they're in an unusual moment.
""This is a period where the high prices are driven more by geopolitical events than anything else,"" he said. Since it's hard to predict how long those will last, he continued, ""You put the money in the bank and watch to see what happens as we move into 2027.""
Pickering says this makes sense because oil is a cyclical industry — prices move up and down. Companies want to hold onto their cash when times are good so they can insulate themselves when they're not so good.
A supply shock, amid two ongoing wars
The rising cost of oil has pushed up what drivers pay at the pump. Gasoline now costs about $1.20 more per gallon than it did a year ago, according to AAA. Diesel hit a record high in September, and is still close to that high.
Exxon CEO Darren Woods was one of the few to directly address the issue on an earnings call, saying, ""We recognize the impact high prices have on people's lives and economic growth.""
Exxon's solution, he said, is to boost production. "" With products in short supply, our teams have done everything they can to keep prices down by maximizing production,"" he said.
Chevron CEO Mike Wirth made a similar point, telling investors, "" What we can do is what we can control, and that's help consumers get as much as they possibly can out of our systems.""
On these calls, executives from all the companies pointed out that they're dealing with a massive supply shock. The war in Iran, as well as Ukraine's strikes on Russian oil refineries, have taken a lot of global refining capacity offline.
The executives indicated that they don't see the situation changing anytime soon.
""If you'd asked me six months ago, would I have thought that oil prices were going to be where they are today — no,"" Shell CEO Wael Sawan said on his company's call. ""Personally, I thought we were at a high there, and we were essentially looking at what was going to be a temporal spike.""
But because the war has gone on this long, global oil reserves are at much lower levels. The U.S. Strategic Petroleum Reserve is at low levels; Europe released some of its crude oil and diesel.
""We're now seeing more and more structural stability in that higher oil price simply because we are seeing that inventory levels are being drawn down very quickly, and it's going to take quite some time to be able to refill that inventory,"" Sawan continued.
Calls for a windfall tax
Democrats in both chambers of Congress have called for a tax on windfall profits — excess profits that come about because of geopolitical forces outside of their control, not because of innovation. Bills introduced this spring would return some of those profits to Americans through tax rebates.
Earlier this year, Senator Sheldon Whitehouse (D-R.I.), who sponsored a windfall tax bill in the Senate, said he hopes it will also spotlight how climate-friendly renewable energy is increasingly more cost-competitive than fossil fuels. "" Wind, solar, and battery power, they're not raising their prices,"" Whitehouse told NPR at the time.
Isabella Weber, an economist at the University of Massachusetts Amherst, has studied how windfall taxes worked in other countries. She says windfall taxes are a way to bridge the gap between what the oil companies make and what most people pay for that oil.
""The companies that are running this essential sector have been reaping record profits amidst the worst energy supply crisis that we have had in several decades,"" she said. ""This is a contradiction that, I think, raises fundamental questions about how we organize our economy, how we organize our society, who benefits, who loses.""
""When emergencies generate massive windfall profits,"" she continued, it creates ""this crazy situation where the worst of times ends up being the best of times, which is problematic on a deeper level.""
While the oil executives didn't mention windfall taxes on this week's calls, last quarter, Exxon's Woods called them a ""misguided policy."" He said they penalize companies that are trying to be successful in a volatile industry.
As the midterms approach, the Trump administration has taken measures to bring down the price of fuel, particularly diesel, including temporarily loosening federal rules for dyed diesel, which is used in farming, and lifting sanctions against Russia to allow more diesel to enter the world market.
But these efforts are unlikely to bring down U.S. prices — the amount of fuel Russia can release would make up only a small portion of U.S. daily demand, and dyed diesel is still not accessible to most drivers.
In any case, significantly lower prices are unlikely to materialize by Tuesday.

11,2026-10-31,"Depreciation is a double-edged sword, but whether you're buying or selling, one brand consistently tops the list for the lowest depreciation over five years: Toyota. CarEdge reports that Toyota models, on average, retain 64.9% of their original value. Subaru, at 61.2%, comes in second. Think about that Toyota number for a moment. The average new car loses roughly 30% of its MSRP in the first two years of ownership. It takes a Toyota almost half a decade to do the same. But remember that value hit is an average; some Toyota models have even stronger residual values.
At the top of Toyota's list for lowest depreciation are the Tacoma, Corolla Hatchback, GR Supra, 4Runner, Tundra, RAV4, and the regular Corolla sedan. Other models, like the Corolla Cross and Grand Highlander, also have below-average depreciation, but these nameplates haven't been around for very long. We're focusing on Toyotas with a five-year valuation track record that's been reported by CarEdge and iSeeCars, and the value retention percentage we highlighted for each model in this list averages the two companies' calculations.
To add a dose of reality, we also looked at active listings from Autotrader to see how closely those valuations hold up in today's marketplace. All MSRPs mentioned include factory destination charges. Used-vehicle pricing reflects examples with near-average mileage for their age (around 68,000 miles for a five-year-old vehicle) and in good condition with no accident history or branded titles.
While owners have different opinions about the best Toyota Tacoma generation, one thing is indisputable: A five-year-old Tacoma is the residual-value champion among similarly aged Toyotas. CarEdge says that works out to 79% of its original value after five years, while iSeeCars is more generous with 80.1%. That's a retained-value average of 79.5%. Or put another way, the Tacoma loses only about 4% of its value per year. iSeeCars also lists the Tacoma as one of the top vehicles for low depreciation — only the Porsche Cayman, Porsche 911, and Chevrolet Corvette are better.
A 2021 Tacoma in SR5 trim, with rear-wheel drive and a double cab, averages roughly $29,500 on Autotrader. When new, the MSRP for this version was $33,405. Crunch those numbers, and five-year depreciation only rises to 11.7%. The 88.3% residual value is substantially above the combined average from CarEdge and iSeeCars.
Although the GR Supra is leaving the Toyota lineup after 2026, this BMW-built sports car is alive and well in the used car market. CarEdge's data shows a five-year residual value of 78.4%, while iSeeCars is close at 76%; the two estimates average 77.2%. The GR Supra launched for the 2020 model year, with the 2021 edition adding a lower-priced four-cylinder version alongside a more robust inline-6 model, which received a 47-horsepower bump.
Despite the four-cylinder's more affordable base price of $43,985, a 2021 GR Supra still costs more used than a new Toyota GR86. Marketplace pricing for this base model hovers around $37,000, putting its retained value at 84.1% — higher than the CarEdge and iSeeCars estimates. Adding a six-cylinder engine to the conversation increases valuations slightly. With limited inventory, the mid-grade 3.0 Premium trim, which had an original MSRP of $55,485, is listed at an average of $47,000 on Autotrader, which points to a resale value of 84.7%.
Toyota's full-size pickup, the Tundra, also makes the cut for keeping depreciation to a minimum. However, five-year resale value estimates of 75.5% (CarEdge) and 78.8% (iSeeCars) leave a 3.3-percentage-point gap between the two sources. That difference can translate into a $1,200-or-more price difference for used truck buyers. The 2021 model year is the last edition of the second generation, which was replaced by an all-new Toyota Tundra with the grenading V6 engine.
The lower-tier SR5 trim with the CrewMax configuration and rear-wheel drive (RWD) had an MSRP of $39,565 in 2021. Among recent listings, similarly equipped examples average about $36,000, for a 91% resale value. That puts its retained value about 15.5 percentage points above CarEdge's estimate. The next level up is the Limited grade. When new, this came with a window sticker that started at $45,850 (also a CrewMax edition with RWD). Used examples aren't common, but they are priced at about $41,000, which means 89.4% of the truck's original value remains.
While domestic automakers were rolling back small-car production, Toyota doubled down by adding a hatchback version to the modern Corolla lineup for the 2019 model year. The five-door format appears to have helped with robust resale values compared with its four-door counterpart, but the sedan still makes the list. CarEdge reports that a five-year-old Corolla Hatchback retains 78.2% of its original MSRP, compared with iSeeCars' 74.5% estimate, a 76.4% average.
When equipped with a continuously variable transmission (CVT), the 2021 Corolla hatchback had a base MSRP of $22,560. Second-hand units are limited, but what's on the market is priced at about $20,000, making value retention after five years even higher at 88.7%. The Corolla Hatchback was also available with a six-speed manual transmission (a feature dropped in 2023). In 2021, the base trim equipped this way was priced at $21,460. While these cars are even harder to find, pricing around $20,000 is typical, putting depreciation at 6.8% over half a decade.
Calling the RAV4 popular is an understatement. In 2021, Toyota sold over 400,000 examples in the U.S., making it the best-selling, non-truck vehicle that year. This success translates into an ample supply in the used car market, but don't confuse ready availability with the potential for good deals. Valuations from CarEdge (76.5%) and iSeeCars (74.8%) average out at 75.7%. However, you'd be lucky to find a good-condition, five-year-old RAV4 that's depreciated by 24.3%.
The marketplace says pricing is far tighter. A base RAV4 LE with front-wheel drive was stickered at $27,045 in 2021. Half a decade later, dealer listings average about $23,500, for a residual value of 86.9%. Stepping up to the mid-tier XLE Premium trim shows slightly more depreciation, with average dealer pricing of almost $26,300, or 84.7% of the original MSRP of $31,045. These real-world numbers may reflect what's going on in the used car market. CarGurus reports that second-hand RAV4s have increased in price by 3.3% over the past 90 days, while the overall market has dropped by 1.7% over the same period.
Toyota's highest-ranking SUV for preserving MSRP over five years is the 4Runner. Here, CarEdge and iSeeCars report near-matching resale values of 76.3% and 74.5%, respectively, for the 2021 model year, averaging 75.4%. Remember that this edition is part of the long-in-the-tooth fifth generation that was on the market from 2010 through 2024. For 2021, power came solely from a 4.0-liter V6 engine. The latest generation switched to turbocharged four-cylinder power, including a hybrid option.
On an entry-level SR5 trim with standard rear-wheel drive, a 2021 4Runner has an average listing price of about $31,600, compared to an original MSRP of $37,940. That math works out to a remaining value of 83.3%, about 8 percentage points above the combined estimate. Meanwhile, the mid-grade TRD Off-Road Premium grade had a window sticker price starting at $44,225. Current average pricing for a used one is nearly $40,000, representing a depreciation rate of only 10.1% over five years, or a remaining value of 90.4%.
The four-door Corolla may not have the sales success of the RAV4, but Toyota still moved almost 250,000 examples of this compact sedan in 2021. You'll have no trouble locating five-year-old offerings on dealer lots. Meanwhile, CarEdge says Corollas of this age keep 68.8% of their original value, while iSeeCars reports a 72.4% residual value. However, as with many other Toyotas on this list, the Corolla's 70.6% value retention average between the two estimates isn't realistic in the current marketplace either. Possible reasons include higher prices for used Corollas (CarGurus reports a 1.9% increase over the past 90 days) and creeping MSRPs for new ones. The cheapest 2026 Corolla starts at $24,420, which marks a 17.3% increase over 2021.
The almost-entry-level LE trim for the 2021 Corolla has an MSRP of $21,270, but this model has an average listing price of about $19,200; that's a 90.3% residual value and an eye-popping 19.8-percentage-point difference from the combined estimate. Interestingly, the once-more-expensive SE grade (with a 2021 MSRP of $23,270) now shows an average market price of roughly $19,100 on Autotrader, making it a relatively better buy with an 82.1% residual value."
12,2026-10-31,"Author of the AI Insider newsletter
I've got some happy news to share this week. It involves a young computer science graduate named Kiran Maya Sheikh, who has taught me a lot about grit, determination, and the realities of finding a job in the age of AI.
Back in February, I wrote a newsletter arguing that people shouldn't panic about AI destroying tech jobs.
Kiran, who had recently graduated from the University of California, Irvine, wasn't impressed.
""It's bad advice to 'not worry,'"" she emailed me. ""AI is causing disruption in this job market. Employers are prioritizing hiring experienced workers, but not new graduates.""
It was a humbling moment.
We arranged an interview, and Kiran told me about the struggle to land her first full-time job. She'd studied computer science partly because it promised financial stability. Instead, she was competing against experienced engineers, other graduates, and potentially AI itself.
""It's a bloodbath,"" she told me.
We've stayed in touch ever since. I've sent her articles, offered (unsolicited!) career advice, and occasionally shared my own professional struggles. She's kept me updated on the job hunt, her volunteer work, and the fellowships she pursued to gain experience.
In June, she was still searching. But she was learning new skills, building websites, and meeting people.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Then, last week, an email arrived.
""I have great news: I started my first full time job as a software engineer!"" she wrote.
Kiran is now working for the Virginia Digital Service, the state's government technology modernization team. She's moved from Los Angeles to Richmond, Virginia, and is settling into her new life.
Yessss! Nothing feels better than to share in other people's success, without doing any of the work.
Seriously, though: Kiran's experience is worth remembering as we confront another frightening prediction about the future of work: Robots are coming for everyone's jobs.
Business Insider recently launched Robonomics, a major reporting series examining how AI-powered robots could transform work, businesses, and the economy.
One story, by my colleague Eugene Kim, takes us inside an enormous Amazon warehouse in Tracy, California.
The facility is packed with robots that move shelves, sort packages, and transport carts. Jobs that once required people to walk miles every day are increasingly performed by machines.
Sounds terrifying for workers, right?
Except Eugene discovered something surprising.
The warehouse employs more than 3,000 people, up from roughly 1,000 when it opened in 2023, according to Amazon. Workers are still picking, packing, and loading packages. Others are learning to supervise and maintain the machines.
One employee, Briana Brager, started as a warehouse picker. Now she's a ""quarterback"" for robots, coordinating the response when machines malfunction.
""We call the shots,"" she told Eugene.
Amazon says its more automated warehouses support roughly 30% more skilled jobs. The Tracy facility handles more packages than a nearby warehouse with fewer robots, despite employing roughly the same number of people.
This doesn't mean there's nothing to worry about.
Amazon has explored using robots to slow future hiring growth, Eugene exclusively reported in May 2025. If warehouses can process more orders without adding employees, thousands of potential jobs could disappear before they're ever created.
Retraining is difficult. Change is stressful. And nobody can guarantee that tomorrow's new jobs will make up for those that vanish.
But automation isn't the same thing as unemployment. That's a distinction often lost in the rush to write scary, clickable headlines about AI.
The broader tech job market also offers reasons for optimism. My recent reporting found tech openings have jumped this year, fueled partly by AI-related demand, while tech layoffs remain well below their 2022 peak.
Which brings us back to Kiran's shiny, new tech job.
Following her journey has reminded me that getting your first proper job has always been brutally difficult.
When I graduated decades ago, I applied for hundreds of positions and heard almost nothing. Eventually, a college professor helped me get a miserable, poorly paid editorial assistant job. It took years of grinding before I landed at Bloomberg News (as a lowly intern) and my career finally began.
There was no ChatGPT to blame back then. Just rejection, uncertainty, and the occasional generous human being.
Kiran's experience has been different, but her conclusions sound remarkably familiar. She shared two lessons from her job search odyssey:
""1) Your network matters, and 2) Results come from effort * LUCK, and you improve your chances of success by doing as many things as you can!!!"" she wrote.
Appropriately, she used the * symbol as a multiplication sign, as programmers and mathematically minded people often do.
It's a clever distinction. Multiplication suggests you need both: Hard work increases your chances, but luck still plays a crucial role. And her final point is that doing more things — volunteering, networking, applying, learning — increases your chances of getting lucky.
And that's exactly what she did. Instead of waiting for the perfect opening, she volunteered, pursued fellowships, learned new skills, and built relationships. And eventually, an opportunity arrived.
AI and robots will change the world of work. Some jobs will disappear. New ones will emerge. The transition won't always be painless.
But human ingenuity, persistence, and relationships still matter enormously — maybe more than ever.
Just ask Kiran.

13,2026-10-31,"Flying cars could be around the corner. Just don't expect them to be part of your daily commute anytime soon.
Two days before Tesla's demonstration of its kickflipping Roadster put flying cars back in the spotlight, a startup that has been building flying cars since 2015 posted a video-slash-salvo of a test model hovering over a Tesla Cybertruck and an early-generation Roadster.
""A lot of people now are aware of the Roadster, but we were already flying for five years,"" Jim Dukhovny, CEO of Alef Aeronautics, told Business Insider, adding that they have logged about 1,000 flights. Alef planned the video response about a month before the Roadster demo, after reports indicated Tesla might debut a flying car, he said.
As for when flying cars will actually be delivered to the public, Tesla said its Roadster will start production in 2028, but the version that includes a ""full flight system"" will be invite-only. Meanwhile, Dukhovny said his company hopes to deliver its first car by next year.
There are hefty price tags for both: Alef Aeronautics expects its flying car to cost $300,000 with a $150 preorder deposit. Tesla doesn't display the full price of the Roadster. The company website states the Roadster with ""acceleration thrusters"" requires a $250,000 deposit.
Dukhovny said the cost for a flying car is too high — even for him.
""I cannot afford it right now,"" he said about purchasing one, adding, ""Once we go to mass production and once we get to the scale of automotive manufacturing, that should cost under $25,000.""
Price isn't the only barrier. You'll also need a place to fly it.
Dukhovny, who is based in the San Francisco Bay Area, said that while we may not need dedicated heliports for flying cars, we shouldn't expect to take off somewhere in the middle of downtown to avoid traffic.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
""You cannot take off from Market Street or land on Market Street,"" he said, referring to a major San Francisco artery. ""Not right now, not ever. You have to take off and land from places where it's allowed, but you don't have to build that.""
Alexandre Bayen, an electrical engineering and computer science professor at the University of California, Berkeley, who studies advanced transportation systems, said the technology behind flying cars isn't the challenge; rather, it's building a reliable transit system with those cars.
Factoring in regulations, infrastructure, and public acceptance, Bayen said a world in which people in dense urban areas can travel by flying car is likely generations away.
""Thinking of this as a vehicle for mass transit in the near future is, I think, absolutely unrealistic,"" he said.
Dukhonvy said he imagines early use cases revolving around people living in remote locations — think mountainous terrain or areas with underdeveloped roads. The CEO framed it as a cost- and time-saving measure for the wealthy.
Bayen similarly said he could see flying-car technology being useful for short commutes over small bodies of water.
""The one type of demand that this type of technology solves is a very elite shortcut commute above water or obstacles for people who can afford it,"" he said.
Companies like Joby Aircraft and Archer Aviation are building an air transit system with eVTOLs — electric vertical takeoff and landing aircraft — that are similar to helicopters but quieter and powered by electricity.
Dukhovny stressed that flying cars are different because they can drive on roads.
""Right now, there are only two that exist,"" he said. ""Us and Tesla.""
A Tesla spokesperson did not respond to a request for comment.

Updated 1 day ago,
October 30, 2026


Alphabet lost more than $500 billion in market value on October 29 after the Google parent warned that AI-generated search answers were affecting advertising revenue. Shares dropped almost 13%, and the stock hit a 6-month low, with the company’s market value falling from $3.97 trillion to $3.46 trillion.
The pressure comes from changes to Google’s own search engine. AI summaries increasingly occupy the top of results pages, leaving less room for traditional ads in the business responsible for most of Alphabet’s advertising revenue.
Sales growth couldn’t offset concerns about earnings and spending. Alphabet’s quarterly revenue reached $127.6 billion, a 25% increase from a year earlier, and Google Cloud brought in almost $31 billion, more than double its year-earlier revenue. Profit nevertheless dropped 25%.
Free cash flow was negative by more than $9 billion, only the 2nd negative result since Alphabet went public. The company said AI accounted for much of its spending increase. Forrester principal analyst Tracy Woo pointed to the challenge of funding that investment while protecting Google’s existing businesses.
Other tech companies faced similar scrutiny. Amazon shares dropped 6% after it disclosed negative free cash flow of $42 billion, even as quarterly revenue grew 11% to $201 billion. CEO Andy Jassy defended the investment, saying Amazon could eventually develop major businesses through its own chips and Bedrock, its AI development platform.
Microsoft’s shares slipped less than 1%. It reported $92.3 billion in revenue, up 19%, and 30% growth in its cloud business to $63 billion. It also announced thousands of job cuts and a reduction of roughly $5 billion in AI-related investment.
With an AI-focused version of search in development at Google, the company’s chief business officer, Phillip Schindler, described how he felt as “very good about our trajectory.”

15,2026-10-31,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Charles Hanna, the CEO of Cedar's Foods, based in Haverhill, Massachusetts. The following has been edited for length and clarity.
When my father came to the United States from Lebanon in the 1960s, he fell in love with this beautiful country. In 1981, he decided to start a hummus company, even though it was not yet common in the US. When he told my mother, she called her sister and said, ""My husband is going crazy. Who is going to buy hummus?""
My mother made samples, which my father would take to the market. Some people would accept, and others wouldn't, until he called a local grocery store called Market Basket. The man he spoke to happened to know about a great Lebanese restaurant that served hummus, so he decided to give it a shot. My father was very happy.
In the '90s, my father asked me for help in running the business. At the time, I had no clue I would be so deeply involved. I had other plans, like maybe running a restaurant, but I wanted the business to succeed and make my father proud. I remember him telling me one thing: to make sure that when we made the product, we made it of the highest possible quality.
I took that to heart.
Business Insider's Power Hours series gives readers an inside look at how powerful people structure their workday. Reach out to editor Lauryn Haas to share your daily routine.
At that time, I never took a holiday. Sometimes I'd stay at work until 3 a.m., thinking about how I was going to run the business because I had no experience. I drove one of the delivery trucks and spent a lot of time selling the product because I'd never hired a broker.
I took over our small business as an owner in the mid-2000s, and finally had to list myself as CEO in the early 2010s once the company grew and I needed a title when working with the bank.
Today, we're one of the world's largest hummus producers, and my day-to-day is heavily involved in all aspects of the business. Here's a day in my life.
I go to the gym, and I also like biking.
Music is a big part of my mornings and when I get ready for the day. I love all kinds of music, especially old French songs and classics. It puts me in a good mood, and I honestly don't know how to be in a bad mood. I wake up happy, and I try to carry that positive energy into work.
My office is only 12 minutes away from my home.
When I open the door to the office and see my people inside, it's the best feeling in the whole world. We go up to the executive offices and sit down to talk and joke. Our president and COO, Nick Scangas, makes me one of his Greek coffees, or we might have some cookies made with his mom's recipe.
Then, we get serious. I'm involved in marketing, finance, and sales. I've done it all. I'm very detail-oriented, so I like to look at the numbers and analyze them.
I've never had an assistant or wanted one. I respond to emails and WhatsApp messages all throughout the day; I believe the more I'm involved and using my brain, the better. The only thing I can't do is cook, but I have a good taste in product.
One of the first ideas I had was to flavor the product rather than just make original hummus. My father initially told me no because the original recipe was our traditional product, but I convinced him to let us try garlic and red pepper.
Create a BI Job Scout and get opportunities matched to your goals.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Today, we've created many more flavors — lemon, chocolate, even hot honey — because I've always believed everybody has a different taste, and if you have a healthy base product, people should be able to enjoy it in different ways.
I also spend a lot of time on humanitarian projects, like supporting our local YMCA and funding students' education in Lebanon, which are extremely important to me.
I'll listen to music, chat with employees, and see how they're doing because, in the end, we're all people.
My team and I go out for lunch together. I like going to 99 Restaurants; my team doesn't love it as much as I do, but they'll come along.
When I was younger, I loved volleyball and would play every day. I believe in running a business like a team — just like when I'd played volleyball. I've never believed in somebody being in total control. You have to surround yourself with strong people.
We've built this company to feel like a family, and many of our employees have been with us for years. We have an incredible executive team, and everybody brings something different to the company. I have many meetings every day with different team members — either in person or on conference calls.
You'll never find me sitting at the head of the conference table; I sit around it with my team because I think it signifies that everyone can have their own input and ideas. If you stay within the team and nobody's better than anybody else, you're going to make the best team ever.
In the afternoon, I leave the office, and my home is open. About 30 people come over every day — my uncles, cousins, friends — everybody. I have a huge family. It's like a tribe of 200 people in the area between New Hampshire and Massachusetts.
Having that time with them is extremely important. We play cards or watch football or basketball. Everyone loves the Celtics and the Patriots.
I also love karaoke. If you wanted to categorize me or my life as a song, it would be Frank Sinatra's ""My Way."" Every word of that song is my life. I have to sing it at least three times a week. I also like ""How I Love You"" by Engelbert Humperdinck, and I'm working on some others. I'm not a star, but I do- have a good voice.
Even with my hobbies and spending time with family, if I'm awake, I'm thinking about how I can better the business. I always want our customers to walk down the refrigerated aisle and find something new.
While innovation has always been important to me, I've never believed success comes from fighting your competition. We want the whole industry to grow because when more people discover new food products, everybody benefits.
A lot of the way I think about success goes back to my family's story. Whether I'm in the office or outside, this company is my life.
Most nights, I watch a movie, although occasionally, I watch the news. I go to bed around 11 p.m. or midnight, before I wake up and do it all again the next day.
I believe if you don't have bad intentions toward anyone, you'll sleep well, wake up positive, and enjoy your day. That's how I've always tried to live my life.
What motivates me today is the same thing my father taught me years ago: make the highest-quality product possible and treat people the right way.
If one day I can't improve this company anymore, then somebody else should be CEO.
When people remember me, I don't want them to remember how much money I made. I want them to remember that I was a good person.

16,2026-10-31,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Best practices are crowded on purpose. The upside sits with whoever moves before they’re proven safe. Here’s how you can be that person.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
The safest-looking marketing plan in the room is usually the one most likely to keep you invisible. When you copy the channels, the messaging and the metrics that already work for the category leader, you compete on terms that favor whoever has the bigger budget and the longer head start. That feels like prudence. It is actually the slowest and most expensive way to stay a follower.
Safe feels responsible because failure is easy to defend when you followed the same plan as everyone else. Nobody gets second-guessed for matching the industry norm. That comfort is exactly the problem, because a plan built to avoid blame is not built to win, and the market rewards the brands willing to be wrong in public before they are proven right.
I learned this early. In 2013, I argued that mobile apps needed a strategy of their own rather than a shrunken version of web marketing, back when that sounded like a distraction. The people who moved before the consensus did not have better tools than everyone else. They simply refused to wait for permission and had already taken ownership of the space by the time the cautious crowd arrived.
Best practices earn their name because everyone already uses them. By the time a tactic is safe enough to carry that label, it is crowded and drained of any edge it once held. You can run it flawlessly and still finish exactly where you started, one more voice repeating the same message in the same place as your competitors.
The same trap shows up in how you measure success. Chasing the impressions, followers and engagement numbers every rival reports is the most crowded play in marketing, because those figures are easy to hit and easy to ignore. They make a quarter look healthy while the business underneath them sits still.
That ground is moving faster than the safe playbook admits. A Pew Research Center study of nearly 69,000 searches found that when an AI summary sits atop Google results, users click a traditional link in only 8% of visits, down from 15% when no summary appears. Ranking a page and waiting for the click is losing its footing, and most brands are still optimizing for rules that have already changed.
Bold does not mean reckless. What builds leaders is an asymmetric wager, where the downside is small and survivable, and the upside is owning territory no rival has claimed. You are not gambling the company. You are spending a measured slice of budget and attention on a shift you can already see forming, and planting yourself where it lands before the crowd arrives.
The highest-return version refuses to fight inside a crowded category at all. Instead of scrapping for a sliver of space the leader already defined, you reframe the problem so you become the obvious answer to a question nobody else is asking. Think of the companies that stopped selling a slightly better version of an existing product and instead named a new category they could own outright. They looked risky right up until they looked inevitable.
You can pressure-test the move in a single meeting. Ask what happens if it fails and whether the honest answer is a bruise or a catastrophe. Then ask what happens if it works and whether the payoff is a marginal gain or a foothold nobody can take from you. When failure is survivable and success is defining, hesitation becomes the expensive choice.
The clearest version of this decision sits in front of every marketer right now. AI systems are becoming the layer between your customer and the answer, and the brands that appear inside those answers are the ones deciding today to be there. Waiting until the behavior is undeniable means arriving after the winners are already chosen.
Winning that visibility depends less on budget than on being the clearest and most credible source on the questions you want to own. When an AI system builds an answer, it pulls from information it can trust and quote, so the work is making your expertise easy to find and hard to leave out. That legwork is fundamentals executed better than the competition, aimed at a channel most of your rivals are still ignoring.
In practice, that means publishing direct answers to the questions your customers actually ask, in language a machine can lift without guessing at your meaning. It means keeping your pages clean enough that the facts are easy to extract and quote. It means earning mentions on the outside sources these systems already trust, so when they build an answer, yours is the evidence within reach. That is unglamorous work, and it is exactly why most of your competitors will skip it until the advantage is gone.
Playing it safe shields you from the danger you can see and hands you the one you cannot, the slow erosion of being easy to overlook. The move that feels too bold today is often the only thing that keeps you relevant tomorrow. The real threat was never the leap. It was standing still while the ground moved.
The safest-looking marketing plan in the room is usually the one most likely to keep you invisible. When you copy the channels, the messaging and the metrics that already work for the category leader, you compete on terms that favor whoever has the bigger budget and the longer head start. That feels like prudence. It is actually the slowest and most expensive way to stay a follower.
Safe feels responsible because failure is easy to defend when you followed the same plan as everyone else. Nobody gets second-guessed for matching the industry norm. That comfort is exactly the problem, because a plan built to avoid blame is not built to win, and the market rewards the brands willing to be wrong in public before they are proven right.
I learned this early. In 2013, I argued that mobile apps needed a strategy of their own rather than a shrunken version of web marketing, back when that sounded like a distraction. The people who moved before the consensus did not have better tools than everyone else. They simply refused to wait for permission and had already taken ownership of the space by the time the cautious crowd arrived.
