17,2026-10-31,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
A couple of years ago, BGR reviewed the Roborock Qrevo Curv, the company's first robot vacuum that could climb while also offering a higher suction power, mop capabilities, and an igloo-like design that made it stand out from the competition. Roborock has since introduced its Saros lineup, grown the Qrevo line, and even unveiled a robot vacuum with a robotic arm, which we also reviewed. A couple of months ago, when the company introduced the Roborock Qrevo 2 Pro robot vacuum, I was eager to try a new iteration of the Qrevo lineup, which continues to offer a unique white cleaning station, flagship features, but costs just $549.99 at the time of writing.
Roborock initially launched the Qrevo 2 Pro robot vacuum for $799,99, but discounts have been easy to find, and with Black Friday just around the corner, the company says it wants to get this vacuum into the homes of as many customers as possible. In fact, you might find discounts even larger than what the company is currently offering.
Keeping in mind the relatively affordable price point in this products category, here's what we found after spending the past month reviewing this vacuum. It not takes some of the best elements of the Qrevo Curv model — it also reminds us of everything great that Roborock introduced recently. Here's what you need to know about Roborock's latest release.
At this point, most people know what the average flagship robot vacuum has to offer: A cleaning dock and a round robot with brushes and potentially mops. Still, after so many years making robots, it still feels like Roborock finding new ways to give its releases a standout design. While the Qrevo 2 Pro's dock isn't quite as space-agey as the model we reviewed in 2024, it's still sleek and stylish. The white model, which is the review unit the company sent BGR, blends pretty seamlessly into the corner of a living room.
The robot's dimensions are 13.86 x 13.78 x 3.80 inches, which means it doesn't feature the ultra-slim design of the Saros 20, which measured in at 3.14 inches tall. But it does something clever whenever you're not using the mopping capabilities, which is removing the mop when it's not in use.
Among the top features of this model, Roborock has included multiple carpet-cleaning options, dual spinning mops, a FlexiArm edge cleaner to clean along walls and furniture legs, and dual anti-tangle brush system. That last feature has cut down significantly on the maintenance requiring to maintain robot vacuums. As with other Roborock vacuums, the Qrevo 2 Pro uses LiDAR to navigate, creating a 3D map of the environment to avoid household obstacles and adjust in real time.
Over the past month, Roborock's Qrevo 2 Pro robot vacuum has been relentlessly cleaning two living rooms (one with a large rug), a kitchen, a service area, a bathroom, and a cement garden. Even with a dog in the house, 20,000 Pa of suction power is more than enough to ensure everything gets clean. The robot is silent when it works as well, meaning it won't be a major distraction while you're at work or trying to have a conversation in your home.
The Qrevo 2 Pro is great at gathering dust, crumbs, and dirt from both hard floors and carpets. With its DuoDivide main brush and anti-tangle side brush that automatically reverses direction to optimize edge coverage and prevent hair wrapping, this robot excels at cleaning up corners. It's also a decent mopping robot, but it's clear that the robot is more capable when it comes to cleaning a house that is consistently cleaned rather than one that could use a deep clean. That said, if you do need to take a second pass, there's a Target Area Re-mop feature for high-traffic zones.
Like the Qrevo Curv, this model can also pass thresholds of up to 4cm. Newer models already achieve 8cm in two phases, but this is great if you have small thresholds, as you don't need to keep raising the robot to continue cleaning the space.
Long gone are the days when setting up a robot vacuum would take ages. Now, once you log in with your Roborock account and start the pairing process, the experience is refreshingly straightforward — even removing the frustration of having to set up your home Wi-Fi on the robot. Once you have your robot synced, it takes a quick tour around your home, and the app will map each room automatically. While you can edit the spaces by yourself and create forbidden zones, the robot also updates its map over time to continuously improve the cleaning process.
With this model, Roborock bets on its PreciSense LiDAR navigation to map the environment and determine the most optimal, efficient cleaning path while minimizing the number of areas it misses. It relies on structured-light sensors to detect and steer around typical clutter such as shoes, wires, and pet toys, and it also identifies tricky spots as ""No-Go Zones"" to prevent the robot from getting stuck — something that happened quite often with the Qrevo Curv.
Roborock's app is customizable, but I lean on the app's SmartPlan 3.0 technology, which automatically adapts suction and mopping settings per room, as I'd only set a specific cleaning routine if something extraordinary happened — like spilling coffee on the kitchen floor. Besides that, the SmartPlan and reliable voice commands with natural language are more than enough to keep my routine in check without me having to manually make many changes.
The Roborock Qrevo 2 Pro has a 5,200 mAh battery capacity, and while that's smaller than some premium models, Roborock says it offers a runtime of 253 minutes. With that, the robot can easily clean 35 square meters in 33 minutes while using just 10% of the battery. While I felt that Roborock's recent models were taking longer to clean my whole home, the Qrevo 2 Pro feels faster, and it hasn't presented any battery life issues. Though recharging to 100% takes time, the new vacuum always has enough battery to clean fully the following day.
As for the cleaning dock, which is where the robot charges, it features mop washing and drying capabilities, as it uses warm-air drying to keep the mops dry and prevent odors. There's also a 4-liter clean water tank that automatically refills the robot's water reservoir for continuous mopping and a self-emptying dust bag, which locks in pet hair, dust, and debris. That way, I never have to touch any of the gunk and grime the vacuum collects.
Everyone will have a slightly different experience, but I've noticed that it takes a few long cleaning sessions before I need to refill the clean water tank. For the dirty one, I'd rather remove it after each session. Another interesting feature is the auto mop detachment, meaning that whenever it needs to clean carpet, mats, or non-hardwood floors, it leaves the mop at the cleaning dock and only comes back for it when it actually needs to mop.
Roborock's Qrevo 2 Pro is in that ""sweet spot"" within the company's lineup, as it borrows some of the flagship tier features and offers them at a much lower price point. Among the premium features that could make you pull the trigger are the auto mop detachment for carpet, suction power you usually only find in higher-end models, high-temperature mop washing, an anti-tangle brush, and Roborock's FlexiArm tech that extends outward to clean edges around furniture legs.
All of that combined with the competitive price tag makes this robot vacuum one of the best values of 2026. Maintenance-wise, users only have to refill the tank every once in a while, and since the robot can also clean itself, you don't need to worry about getting down on the ground to clean it yourself, even after an intense cleaning session.
Finally, the Roborock app is also always worth mentioning since it's easy to use, highly customizable, and the AI capabilities actually work. A few omissions that aren't available at this price tag are more refined sensors and a detergent compartment. That said, if you're not looking to spend over $1,000, you'll be impressed with what the Qrevo 2 Pro has to offer for the price."
18,2026-10-31,"Anthropic conquered coding. Now it's setting its sights on the human body.
Anthropic's S-1 filing, which outlines its ambitions and financials ahead of a massive initial public offering next month, reads as a life-sciences manifesto. The maker of Claude sees itself as a crucial cog in the next generation of biological and medicinal research — and is selling that lucrative vision to its would-be investors.
Anthropic is hoping for a wave of new discoveries that will support its do-gooder mission and convince the public of AI's power and worth. Its life-science focus could also support financial growth beyond its AI software engineering tools. It would promote the use of Anthropic's most advanced, expensive models in an industry already accustomed to spending billions on research and development.
The S-1 filing is scattered with references to biotech ambition. Its second line says AI ""could rapidly accelerate scientific innovation"" and ""bring cures for major diseases within reach."" A page later, biological progress tops the company's list of ways it hopes AI will improve lives.
Anthropic also references its life sciences business in its ""growth strategy"" section and writes that nine of the 10 largest pharmaceutical companies and eight of the 10 largest healthcare companies in the Fortune 500 spent at least $100,000 with Anthropic over a recent yearlong period.
Read more about Anthropic's S-1 filing
""In the life sciences sector, where annual global pharmaceutical spend is expected to exceed $2 trillion in 2026, Claude is used in drug discovery and development by our customers, our biotechnology partners, and our own researchers,"" the company writes in the filing.
Anthropic declined to comment.
Anthropic has already gotten to work investing in life sciences. The company's Claude Science product, which is in beta, came out in June. OpenAI, Anthropic's erstwhile rival, has a competing offering dubbed Rosalind, while Google spinoff Isomorphic Labs plans to use AI to create cancer-beating drugs — promising a struggle between companies for research customers.
Besides that, Anthropic started a molecular biology lab in the Bay Area this year and spent $400 million in stock in April to buy the biotech startup Coefficient Bio. Vas Narasimhan, the CEO of the pharmaceutical company Novartis, also joined Anthropic's board in April. In August, Anthropic announced a new robotics standard that allows AI to control lab equipment. The company's careers page shows it's on a hiring spree for life sciences roles across chemistry, experimental biology, research engineering, and more. Two new roles opened late this week, offering $320,000 salaries to researchers capable of designing and manufacturing antibodies.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
In its S-1, Anthropic touted its work with pharma giant Lilly, saying the $1.3 trillion company has 50,000 employees with access to a Claude enterprise account and that more than 85% of its staff actively use the tool.
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei, a former theoretical physicist, has repeatedly touted AI's potential in the life sciences as a reason to advance the tech's capabilities.
In a September essay about ""pacing"" AI research, Amodei said he had survived a cancer that wouldn't have been treatable in the 1970s, and his father had died of a disease cured shortly after his death.
He previously elaborated on the themes in a 2024 essay dubbed ""Machines of Loving Grace.""
""Biology is probably the area where scientific progress has the greatest potential to directly and unambiguously improve the quality of human life,"" Amodei wrote, adding that he hopes AI-enabled research will help prevent and treat all infectious diseases, eliminate most cancers, and double the human lifespan.
The general public is less optimistic. Anthropic recently published survey results from August, finding that 91% of staff think AI will make a Nobel-level discovery by 2030, compared with 39% of US adults. No AI-designed drugs have ever received FDA approval.
Ben Mann, the co-leader of Anthropic's Labs organization, which builds new products and created Claude Code, told Business Insider this August that his small team is also focusing more on life sciences.
""Labs itself has shifted its focus from sort of more standard software engineering things to things like what Dario has described as curing all diseases like, Machines of Loving Grace-style,"" Mann said.

20,2026-10-31,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Paul Burke, 42, the CEO and cofounder of Vantory, an AI startup that streamlines vacation rental operations for property managers. He previously built a 64-home rental portfolio in Colorado and sold it in 2026 after two years. The following has been edited for length and clarity.
If you'd asked me two years ago if I'd ever be running a property management company, I probably would've told you you're crazy.
One of my sisters lives in Winter Park, Colorado. She's a real estate agent, and I was visiting her and met her boss, who owns the brokerage.
He invited me into his office that weekend, and he gave me this whole pitch on Winter Park and how billions of dollars are being invested in it, and there's all this opportunity. I thought he was trying to sell me a house, and he was like, ""We've got this brokerage, but we've wanted a property management company for five years, but we haven't found the right partner to build it with. We think you're that guy.""
Those opportunities don't just fall into your lap. I'd sold my previous tech company and was trying to figure out what was next, so I jumped at it.
Winter Park is not like Aspen. It was known for a very long time as the economical ski resort. People who were getting priced out or sick of the insane prices of the Vails and the Aspens were starting to come to Winter Park. You could get the same thing or something nicer for a third of the price.
But it was not very easy to convince people to move from existing property management companies to some brand new one with a guy who had no experience. So I had to take them all for lunch, look them in the eye, shake their hands, and say, ""I will do everything it takes to take care of your property, the best it can possibly be taken care of, and have it generate as much money as possible.""
We got a handful of clients, and then once we had a handful, it was really just about execution. It's always been my approach to business: get a small handful of customers, absolutely crush it for them, and then use those case studies to get more customers. And that's exactly what we did.
We performed extremely well. In some cases, the homes we were managing were making double what their next-door neighbor with the exact same home was making.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
I listened to the things that they needed and then executed on it, but what it really came down to was the numbers. We were able to show, ""Hey, your home's averaging $500 a night; literally, your next-door neighbor is averaging $1,100.""
People who start a property management company are generally pretty passionate about hospitality. Most of the big companies out there started off as a husband-and-wife team who got on a friend's property and another friend's property; next thing they know, the company is 20 years old, they've got 200 properties, and the business kind of gets in the way of the hospitality.
Being personable helps, but it doesn't necessarily generate more dollars in clients' accounts per month.
I was a derivatives trader for a decade, so I spent my 20s in Chicago, trading currency derivatives. I love diving deep into data, deep into numbers, and understanding the ins and outs of how to extract the highest price possible for something.
I did a deep dive into the data early on, and I saw things like the way pricing is done for short-term rentals; it's pretty standard, but it's priced by the number of bedrooms a home has.
A four-bedroom, $1 million home was charging the same as a four-bedroom, $4 million home. If someone's willing to pay 4 times more to purchase it, then you'd imagine there's got to be some correlation in what they'd pay to rent it.
Both my software and trading backgrounds helped in this, because I took a much more institutional approach to pricing, the same way airlines do with flights, and the same way hotels have been doing it.
I may have a unique skillset, but there are a lot of skills I don't have.
I don't know how to fix a furnace, but one time, the Sunday after Christmas, a furnace went out at this home, and being in a small town, I couldn't get a hold of anyone. I knew I couldn't fix it, but I showed up at 9 p.m. on a Sunday night and sat in the furnace room for an hour on YouTube trying to figure out if I could do anything to help.
I knew at the end of the day, even if I couldn't actually get it done and make it work, the CEO of the company showing up to show that he cares and to listen to the frustration would go a long way, and it did.
I drove that home to my team over and over and over again. You may not know the answer, you may not be able to fix the problem, but what so many people want is to have someone truly hear them and show that they care. However you can do that in a genuine way, however you can express that you care, I think, is key.
Most of the companies I've built have actually been pretty white glove in terms of service, but it's a whole new level with property management.
You're basically running a hotel that's scattered around a 10-mile radius. It was intense, and it was draining. I'm the youngest of eight kids, so I like being around people, but it definitely wore me down.
I exited the company for a combination of reasons: private equity is aggressively rolling up the short-term rental market, so the opportunity to exit existed — I was getting a call a week from interested buyers. I also wasn't feeling particularly challenged in the way I like to be.
I felt like we very quickly had owned the Winter Park market and we were on a really amazing trajectory. We had 64 homes live and had 74 signed.
When I took calls early on about selling our portfolio, I figured I would just start building relationships with some of the acquirers. I told them I wasn't interested in selling — ""We've got a 10-year plan"" — and we did, but last winter, there were a couple of moments where I was like, ""I don't want to do this anymore. I don't like people anymore.""
I think what I've learned about myself is that I am better suited for business operations than I am for hospitality operations.
I gave up a lot to start my new company, Vantory — most of my money was going to be made from an earnout. I got several offers from private equity firms to make me an operating partner because they basically wanted to take my playbook for my company.
I turned down that money, because I don't want to work for someone else. I like building companies, and I think I can help more people by doing it this way.
Vantory is exactly what I wished I had as a property manager. We've built something that not just executes the things that we did well, but I took my pricing playbook, automated it, and made it 10 times better than what I was even capable of doing.
Right now, I'm really excited and passionate about Vantory and just want to make it as big and helpful to as many people as possible. 
There are so many people who care so deeply about the guest experience — honestly, more so than I did. I want to give them their time back so they can focus on the thing that energizes them and they're best at, and let Vantory do all of the business stuff better than they could.

21,2026-10-31,"This as-told-to essay is based on a conversation with Anne Beiler, 77, who lives in Texas. The following has been edited for length and clarity.
I started Auntie Anne's because my husband and I weren't making any money.
He'd become a counselor, providing free support to people in need of help. I wanted to support him, so in 1988, I opened a store at a farmer's market, selling pretzels and lemonade.
One store become two, which later became twelve, and it continued to grow until we became an international franchise. It was a miracle that unfolded in front of us.
In 2005, when I was 56 years old and had spent more than 15 years in the pretzel business, we sold Auntie Anne's. I never imagined starting a successful company and then selling it one day.
I grew up in the Amish community and always saw myself as a little Amish girl. Then I became ""Auntie Anne."" Announcing the sale of Auntie Anne's was one of the hardest things I've ever done in my life, and selling the company meant I had to change my mindset. My identity couldn't be ""the founder of Auntie Anne's.""
I hadn't thought too much about what I'd do once we sold the company, but my husband and I bought a farm in our area in Pennsylvania, and we wanted to develop it into a medical center and community center. It became our mission.
I realized I had more time to write and speak. Writing books helped me see that there is life beyond selling your company. At the moment, I probably do two or three speaking engagements per month. Whenever the phone rings, I want to say yes to telling my story.
At my age, if I'm not relevant, I don't want to be out there as just another speaker. But when I tell my story, I feel alive.
I thought retirement would be less busy, but I'm still very busy.
When I was a little girl, I wanted to make a difference. Now that I'm older, I can't stop and do nothing. I don't think it's healthy for our bodies. I love going out into my town and seeing retired people working at grocery stores and doing things. We've been created to keep moving.
Since I turned 70, things that felt like a breeze to accomplish in my 50s and 60s, from working on the computer, writing a speech, or going out to speak, take a lot more mental energy to do now.
Everything I do seems bigger and more challenging. It's important for me to live in reality, not in denial. People always say, 77 is just a number. It may be a number, but reality tells me it's different to being 55 or 65.
A great benefit of retirement is that my daily routines are pretty simple. My phone doesn't ring at 7 a.m. — I don't want to do professional engagements before 9 a.m.
When I get up, I love to go outdoors. I love the quietness of the morning. I enjoy walking around my house, humming a tune, talking to God, or listening to the birds.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Then, I go to my emails to check who I need to respond to. Sometimes it takes a few hours and sometimes, it takes no time at all.
Most days, my husband and I go out in the afternoons. Community is very important to us, so we love going to local restaurants and bars in Texas, where we moved roughly 12 years ago, to get to know people. I've met wonderful people, and I feel satisfied.
I don't have any physical issues, and I love working outside doing gardening. During the Auntie Anne's days, my husband and I both had motorcycles we'd use to go and visit our stores. Motorcycle trips were always a part of our lives, and we carried that into our retirement. My husband had to sell his roughly five years ago for health reasons. I still have mine and get out on it occasionally on a beautiful day. I've ridden motorcycles for 40 years, so I'd rather be on one than in a car.
In this season of our lives, it's important to us to make sure we have everything in place for our children when we pass on. We talk about it quite often. We try to be healthy, avoiding processed foods and staying up to date with doctor's appointments. We aren't planning on going into a nursing home. Our daughter, who lives nearby, wants to take care of us with any outside help if needed.
Life is like the seasons. Spring, in your childhood and adolescence, is a time to dream. Summer is a time to plant so your dream can be fulfilled. Fall is a time to harvest — for me, this was selling Auntie Anne's and being able to step into a new chapter.
Winter is a time to enjoy. I love sitting on the couch and watching my husband sitting in his recliner. He's not the young man, full of energy, that I married, but the person he is on the inside is still the person I love.
When you retire, you aren't leaving yourself behind. You're bringing yourself into a new place.
Make this season of your life count, because it's the last season you have.

See how AI, virtual reality and predictive analytics are changing travel planning, search and advisor value.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
Technology has always shaped how we travel. What’s different now is how quickly it’s influencing nearly every stage of the experience.
Artificial intelligence is changing how travelers search for and evaluate their options. Virtual reality is helping people visualize destinations before they arrive. Predictive analytics is helping travel companies anticipate what customers may need before they ask.
As chief information officer of Cruise Planners, I spend a lot of time thinking about what these changes mean for travelers and advisors. The biggest opportunity isn’t removing people from the travel experience. It’s using technology to make them more informed, efficient and valuable.
One of the biggest shifts happening now is the move from traditional search to conversational discovery.
Travelers once searched phrases like “travel agent near me.” Now they can ask, “Who is the best travel advisor near me for a family cruise?” or “Which cruise works best for three generations traveling together?”
AI is being asked not just to find information but to understand intent and identify options that fit.
For travel businesses, that changes what digital visibility means. The question is no longer only, “Do I rank for the right keywords?” It’s also: Does the internet clearly understand who I am, what I specialize in and who I serve?
Before worrying about every new platform, start by making your business clear. Travel advisors should be able to answer three questions: Who are you? What do you specialize in? Who do you serve?
If you specialize in luxury European cruises, your digital presence should reinforce that expertise. If you serve families, groups or travelers in a specific market, that should be clear too.
Your website, Google Business Profile, blog and social media should all tell the same story. One of the first things I tell travel advisors is to maintain a Google Business Profile, but a profile alone isn’t enough. Your website and content should also clearly demonstrate your expertise.
The goal isn’t to create content for machines. It’s to become clearer to people and technology alike.
AI can help travelers compare destinations, organize information and narrow hundreds of possibilities into a manageable list.
But travel isn’t always a simple transaction. A family may be planning a major vacation, or someone may be traveling with an elderly parent who has mobility needs. Those situations require more than information. They require context, judgment and accountability.
That’s why I don’t see the future of AI in travel as a choice between technology and travel advisors. The stronger model is technology plus expertise.
AI can help advisors process information faster and reduce repetitive work, freeing them to spend more time applying judgment where it matters most.
If AI helps travelers understand their options, virtual reality can help them visualize those options.
Think about the difference between reading about a cruise ship cabin and virtually walking through it, or between looking at resort photos and exploring the property before booking.
I don’t think every traveler will plan a vacation through a VR headset. The broader opportunity is immersive visualization. Virtual tours, 3D environments and augmented experiences can help travelers better understand a ship, resort or destination before they buy.
For travel advisors, this becomes another consultation tool. Instead of simply telling clients what an experience looks like, advisors can increasingly help them see it.
Predictive analytics may not get the same attention as generative AI, but its impact could be just as important.
Travel companies generate enormous amounts of data. Predictive analytics can use those patterns to help anticipate what may happen next. For advisors, that could mean recognizing when a client may be ready to plan another vacation or identifying travelers who may be interested in a new itinerary.
Technology can surface the opportunity. The advisor still brings the context.
Travel advisors don’t need to become AI engineers, but they should focus on three things:
Establish a credible digital presence. Maintain an accurate Google Business Profile, and make sure your website clearly explains who you are and what you do.
Define what you want to be known for. Identify your specialties and the customers you most want to serve, then align your website, content and social presence around that.
Stay visible. Regularly publish useful content that demonstrates your expertise and keeps your business active online.
The goal isn’t to publish more content for its own sake. It’s to make your expertise easier to understand and easier to find.
Every major technology shift brings predictions about what will disappear. I think a better question for leaders is: What can this technology help people do better?
AI can process information at scale. Predictive analytics can identify patterns. Immersive technology can help travelers visualize experiences before they arrive.
But travel remains deeply personal. The future of travel technology shouldn’t be about removing the human element. It should be about giving the people who understand travelers better tools to serve them.

Technology has always shaped how we travel. What’s different now is how quickly it’s influencing nearly every stage of the experience.
Artificial intelligence is changing how travelers search for and evaluate their options. Virtual reality is helping people visualize destinations before they arrive. Predictive analytics is helping travel companies anticipate what customers may need before they ask.
As chief information officer of Cruise Planners, I spend a lot of time thinking about what these changes mean for travelers and advisors. The biggest opportunity isn’t removing people from the travel experience. It’s using technology to make them more informed, efficient and valuable.

I mean Cisco is not exactly known for high quality software.It can't get that much worse. For example, IOS famously left the TCP part of TCP/IP to be implemented by the process using it... and had no memory protection for quite some time.
Excluding TCP from a router's usual packet processing makes a lot of sense: it's job is to forward _packets_ not byte streams. The lack of memory protection makes sense when you consider the hardware software originally ran on. It did accumulate it to into large and unwieldy mass that prevented adopting memory isolation for some time. But considering contemporary software, Cisco did well at their core competency.

Cisco software and firmware is notoriously insecure. While LLMs will probably make that worse (and there is no way to do it with actually competent review in a few weeks), it may not be that much worse. Of course, ""Comprehension Debt"" (where nobody really knows the code anymore, and hence nobody can fix vulnerabilities) could still make this a total catastrophe.
While LLMs will probably make that worse
Why though? I'm not saying LLMs will not write security holes, they definitely will if you don't very explicitly tell them not to. But even the nerfed public ones are not bad at spotting security holes compared to people. Most people are notoriously bad at this, but there have also been enough that a huge number of CVEs have been well documented. These are all in the training data and LLMs can absolutely spot these patterns. And they can do it without getting bored on software that was written before those CVEs were found anyway.
Yes, and?
Even if you tell a human programmer to make no mistakes, they'll still write security holes. Yeah no shit LLMs aren't perfect. Neither are humans.
LLMs will cheerfully write egregiously bad ones unless you tell it not to. I find that a bit strange.

Six family plots sharing a courtyard is a modest starting point for an ambitious city plan. But I like that BIG’s Refugee Cities begins at that scale. Developed with to.org, the proposal addresses a difficult mismatch: emergency settlements can become places where people spend years, yet their housing and infrastructure aren’t necessarily planned to support the full lives unfolding there.
Announced on October 29, 2026, the initiative proposes a longer-term approach to housing, services, and employment. BIG, short for Bjarke Ingels Group, lists the architectural project as Refugee Future Cities, with a masterplan for Bidi Bidi in northern Uganda. It remains in design, an important distinction when the ambition extends well beyond building a better house.
Designer: BIG (Bjarke Ingels Group)






The plan grows outward from family plots into compounds, clusters, and neighborhoods. Six plots meet around a communal courtyard, giving the larger settlement a smaller social unit. That arrangement makes sense to me because a city needs intermediate spaces between a private home and a public street. A shared courtyard could offer room for conversation or small-scale work without requiring every activity to happen inside.
BIG identifies sun-dried earth brick, laterite stone, and bamboo among the proposed materials, developing local housing types rather than importing an entirely unfamiliar architectural language. The design also targets ventilation, weather protection, mosquito control, and solar power. These are the details I’d want prioritized over a spectacular silhouette. Keeping insects out and air moving deserves as much design attention as anything that photographs beautifully.

The renderings have visual appeal, though. Rounded, earth-colored walls gather around a large tree in one residential scene, while another image shows a market framed by branching structural supports and deep roof overhangs. The spaces look deliberately sociable. I read the tree-centered courtyard as an especially persuasive image because the gathering place feels as important to the composition as the buildings surrounding it.
Beyond housing, the proposal includes a food market, coworking space, technical workshops, training areas, and a health center. Arts, recreation, and gardens also have a place in the program. Including them feels like a reasonable acknowledgment that people need somewhere to spend time without every outing being an appointment, an obligation, or a transaction. I’m glad the brief makes room for ordinary pleasure.







The infrastructure ambitions are substantial. BIG proposes a solar-powered industrial hub with local energy generation, water treatment, and waste processing. Combining these functions could make resource management part of the settlement’s economic foundation. Still, I’d want to know who maintains the systems, how repairs are funded, and what happens when equipment needs replacing. Those questions belong alongside the appealing renderings.
The financing model is another important piece. The initiative seeks institutional investment, with commercial activity intended to support returns over decades rather than depending entirely on short-term philanthropy. Its proposed “Opportunity Zones” would include manufacturing and agriculture. The wider model also depends on willing host governments, land commitments, regulatory arrangements, and legal work rights for refugees. Those are prerequisites, not benefits architecture can deliver by itself.


I’m interested in that effort to connect housing with livelihoods, but I wouldn’t judge success by investment returns alone. My questions would start with residents: Can they shape their neighborhoods? Are jobs fairly paid? Who gets a say when commercial priorities and community needs diverge? A place can look generous on a site plan while leaving these decisions unresolved. The governance deserves the same scrutiny as the floor plans.
The first planned physical step is a single-family base prototype at the Bidi Bidi Performing Arts Centre. Starting with one home seems sensible if it gives residents a meaningful opportunity to test and influence what follows. For me, the proposal’s value will depend on that feedback reaching the larger plan. The courtyard is a promising beginning, provided the people who use it help decide what comes next.

Since your router is a gateway through which all of your internet browsing and usage passes, it's a device where security is of utmost concern. Using caution and taking extra measures to ensure your Wi-Fi network is secure makes sense, especially if you have an advanced threat model where you can't afford to put safety last. You can, in fact, gain better peace of mind by targeting specific brands as you shop for your next router. While worries about whether TP-Link routers are safe are overblown, other brands have better reputations among experts thanks to better critical flaws histories and free security software.
Many of the most secure router brands — including Peplink and GL.iNet — tend to market themselves to businesses and enthusiasts who aren't afraid to roll up their sleeves and learn some advanced networking skills. For those who want to avoid a technical challenge, you have a safe bet in Asus' consumer-friendly routers, and Netgear has its safety advantages if you don't mind shelling out. We based these recommendations on reading experts' reviews in publications like Consumer Reports and Tom's Guide, plus networking enthusiast websites like Router Security and SmallNetBuilder.
While some experts caution against consumer-oriented brands in general, since they prioritize convenience and user-friendliness over security, experts tend to agree that Asus is the best of popular brands in balancing convenience and security. That's thanks in part to the long-term router firmware updates that Asus is known to provide. Security holes in firmware are some of the worst vulnerabilities you can face, since they can allow the worst of the worst hacks, like remote code execution to your home network. Regular and continued firmware updates are important to mitigate those threats, and Asus is known for its long-term support.
Additionally, Asus doesn't lock safety features behind a subscription service like many of its competitors do. When exploring the most secure routers, HighSpeedInternet.com highlighted the Asus RT-AX68U as one of the most budget-friendly options, pointing out that it ""packs a lot of features for the money, including free security and parental controls."" PC Guide noted that the Asus GT-AXE16000 came with an active monitoring service called AiProtection Pro. They were impressed with its implementation of Trend Micro's malware database, and they went on to note that ""the best part is that this feature is free. You don't have to pay a yearly or monthly subscription to benefit from it; it's part of the package."" A built-in free security suite along with a record of long-term support and a user-friendly management system is hard to beat, as you'll see.
One recommendation you'll find among experts is a lesser-known brand of routers called Gl.iNet. PC Guide listed the GL.iNet GL-AXT1800 as its most budget-friendly secure router, and on the SmallNetBuilder forums, longtime member Tech9 recommended GL.iNet over Asus, saying, ""One of the best price/performance Wi-Fi 6 class routers is GL.iNet GL-MT6000 or Flint 2. It has OpenWRT-based stock firmware and vanilla OpenWRT support.""
While most routers put their own proprietary firmware on their routers, OpenWrt is open source, free, and not dependent on (or sending data back to) any specific manufacturer. Of course, installing OpenWrt is something you can do on any old router yourself (as long as it is supported), but that takes some technical know-how. That's where GL.iNet comes in, pre-flashing their routers with OpenWrt so you don't have to.
This is a double-whammy for safety and security. First, avoiding manufacturer firmware means you can generally trust that your browsing data isn't being transmitted back to the vendor for who-knows-what purpose. Second, as long as you're consistent about updating it, you can also expect to benefit from long-term security updates from OpenWrt. While learning to interact with OpenWrt to manage your network can be challenging, the safety that learning curve brings can make a GL.iNet purchase worth it.
For those who want a more absolute guarantee of safety and security, and those who aren't afraid of some technical work, Peplink comes highly recommended by professionals. Router Security recommends Peplink above all because of its lack of known security issues compared to most other brands. In their own words, ""Peplink has not had one critical security flaw. No headline grabbing bugs. Their software has bugs, of course, but never the critical security flaws that everyone else has.""
Technically, Peplink is targeted at enterprise customers, not the average home user. Still, there's nothing stopping you from buying a Peplink router and setting it up for your home. Be warned, though, that since Peplink designs its products for enterprises and high-tech users rather than the everyday internet user, the setup will be no simple task. Still, Router Security remarked, ""The Peplink user interface is, in my opinion, simpler than that of other techie oriented routers."" So a Peplink router is about as close as you can get to enterprise-level security without the enterprise-level configuration headaches.
While Netgear comes recommended with caveats, you can easily find it rated near the top in terms of browsing safety. Consumer Reports, in its 2026 router buying guide, rated Netgear's routers typically on the better side for data security and data privacy. The Netgear Nighthawk RAX70 and Orbi 750 mesh system were some of its top-recommended routers overall while getting good marks for safety. While Netgear's critical flaw record isn't immaculate, especially compared to Peplink; you're still making a reasonable choice by going for an easy-to-set-up Netgear system.
When reviewing the Netgear Nighthawk RAXE500, Tom's Guide praised Netgear's Bitdefender-infused security suite called Armor. However, unlike ASUS' AiProtection Pro system, Netgear Armor is not free, costing $99.99 every year. The review noted that extending the warranty added even more costs on top of that, explaining that ""Unfortunately, Netgear makes extended protection and additional security updates a subscription-based extra that could add nearly $200 to the already expensive price of the RAXE500."" So, while you can do worse for your internet safety in terms of router brands, Netgear is one that you'll have to be ready to open your wallet for.
We created our list of four recommended router brands for safety by reading discussion forums of networking enthusiasts and by reading professional reviews. The expert recommendations of fellow publications PC Guide, Tom's Guide, HighSpeedInternet.com, and Consumer Reports, plus trusted resources like SmallNetBuilder and Router Security, all informed our choices.
Since cybersecurity is a tricky topic to make broad recommendations on, we chose to include a range of options from consumer-friendly to enthusiast-targeted brands. We specifically provided both well-known brands that make it easy to set security configurations without much know-how and lesser-known brands that will cover the advanced security requirements of those doing highly sensitive work."
26,2026-10-31,"Given the lightweight nature of motorcycles, many of them can get away with using small single-cylinder engines that don't make much power — the majority of everyday bikes and scooters use this type of engine. Meanwhile, experienced riders with enough cash lying around can opt for cruisers, tourers, and superbikes with multi-cylinder engines developing triple-digit horsepower figures.
However, over the years, many enthusiasts, automakers, and boutique firms have pushed the limits of power in motorcycles to levels that defy belief. We're talking engines and power outputs that'd be considered impressive even by car standards, but are simply ludicrous in a motorcycle. Below, you'll find six of the most powerful engines ever fitted to a motorcycle — from V4s to a stonking V10 — with outputs ranging from 220 hp to over 600. We ranked them all from least to most powerful. It's quite an eclectic mix of motorcycles too, comprising production models that you can walk into a showroom and buy, track-only bikes that cost an arm and a leg, and one-off projects built by incredibly talented engineers.
If you're a fan of MotoGP, the Aprilia RSV4 Factory 1100 is possibly the closest you can get to a racing-bike experience while still being street-legal and relatively attainable. Nestled within the RSV4 Factory 1100's twin-spar aluminum frame is a 65-degree 1.1-liter V4 developing 220 hp at 13,000 rpm and 93.7 lb-ft of torque at 10,800 rpm, which is routed to the tarmac via a six-speed multi-plate wet-clutch gearbox.
While its engine is retained from the standard RSV4, the Factory 1100 adds a bunch of suspension and feature upgrades for better on-track performance. Specifically, it gets an Öhlins USD fork up front and an Öhlins monotube at the rear, both electronically controlled. It also comes with lighter forged alloy wheels, and additional features like launch control, GPS, a pit-speed limiter, slide control, and cruise control.
Thanks to its 450-pound wet weight, the RSV4 Factory 1100 carries an exceptional power-to-weight ratio of 0.49 hp-per-pound, allowing for a top speed of over 190 mph. And what do you pay for all this searing performance? Surprisingly, not that much, at least when compared to the other super-powerful motorcycles on this list. Aprilia has the RSV4 Factory 1100 listed at $26,749, excluding a $1,325 destination fee.
Unveiled earlier this year to commemorate Ducati's 100th anniversary, the Superleggera V4 Centenario immediately wowed everyone by becoming the Italian manufacturer's most powerful street-legal motorcycle yet. It achieves this thanks to a new engine, the Desmosedici Stradale R 1100, which is based on the Panigale V4 R's 1.0-liter V4. However, Ducati has increased the piston stroke, resulting in a slightly higher 1.1-liter displacement.
In its standard configuration, the Superleggera V4 Centenario produces 228 hp at 14,500 rpm and 86.7 lb-ft at 10,500 rpm, and if you opt for the Akrapovič racing exhaust and Ducati Corse Performance oil, that output rises to 247 hp at 14,750 rpm and 93.2 lb-ft at 12,500 rpm, channeled to the road via a six-speed racing gearbox. These figures not only make the Superleggera V4 Centenario the most powerful road-going Ducati, but also the most powerful street-legal naturally-aspirated superbike in the world. If you want one, however, you're out of luck. Ducati will build only 500 examples, each priced at $165,000 before options plus a $1,495 destination fee, and all of them are already spoken for.
Built purely for the racetrack1, the Kawasaki H2R is one of the fastest bikes in the world. It carries over the standard Kawasaki H2's 1.0-liter supercharged inline-4 engine, but with an uprated output of 322 hp at 14,000 rpm and 121.5 lb-ft of torque at 12,500 rpm. Kawasaki achieved this power bump by remapping the ECU along with installing more aggressive camshaft profiles, a stronger head gasket and clutch, an unrestricted carbon-fiber intake, and a super-loud titanium exhaust system.
Like the H2, the H2R comes fitted as standard with a MotoGP-style six-speed dog-ring gearbox, but unlike the H2, it's sold without headlights, turn signals, mirrors, or even a radiator cooling fan — all in the pursuit of shedding weight. Combined with the lightweight titanium exhaust and extensive use of carbon-fiber bodywork, the H2R tips the scales at 476.3 pounds, which is nearly 50 pounds lighter than the H2.
If these specifications tell us one thing, it's that the H2R is an absolute weapon, and Kawasaki proved this is true in the real world. In 2016, Turkish motorcycle racer Kenan Sofuoglu rocketed from a standstill to an incredible 248 mph in just 26 seconds on a stock H2R (fitted with specially-made slick tires and topped up with race-grade fuel) on Turkey's 1.67-mile-long Izmit Bay Bridge, also known as the Osman Gazi Bridge. If you find yourself itching to own a Kawasaki H2R, be prepared to part with at least $62,100, plus an $840 destination fee.
Yes, they put an aircraft-grade turbine engine in a bike. Yes, it's street-legal. And yes, you can buy one. Made by Louisiana-based Marine Turbine Technologies (MTT), the Y2K Turbine Superbike is among the most outrageous vehicles you can own. This motorcycle comes powered by a Rolls-Royce M250-C20B turbine engine that produces 420 hp and 500 lb-ft of torque, and is mated to a two-speed automatic transmission. If you're a helicopter buff, you may recognize this turbine engine from early units of the Bell 206L LongRanger.
Thanks to its gearing-limited top speed of 273 mph, the MTT Y2K Turbine Superbike ranks as the fastest street-legal production motorcycle on the planet. However, the word ""production"" does do a bit of heavy lifting here, because the Y2K Turbine Superbike isn't a mass-produced motorcycle like the Aprilia RSV4 Factory 1100 or Kawasaki H2R. Instead, it's a low-volume, custom-built bike that's priced from an eye-watering $275,000, before options. If you have that sort of money, though, you'd be joining a very exclusive owners' club, which even Jay Leno is part of. And as Leno famously told Popular Mechanics when asked about his Y2K Turbine Superbike, ""It's like the hand of God pushing you in the back... I've ridden a lot of fast bikes, but nothing pulls like this"" (via MTT).
If you thought the Dodge Viper was a wild car, imagine its V10 engine in a chassis that's a fraction of the size and routing all that power through one wheel. Dodge itself gave the idea a shot with the V10-powered non-street-legal Tomahawk concept over two decades ago, but the execution fell flat by way of a rather unexciting display at the 2003 Goodwood Festival of Speed. Couple that with Dodge's apparent aversion to independent testing of the Tomahawk's wild 400-mph top-speed claim, and it's no surprise that the concept drew widespread skepticism about its ridability.
British engineer Allen Millyard was among the folks who witnessed the Tomahawk at Goodwood, and took it upon himself to realize the crazy idea of a V10-powered street-legal motorcycle in 2007 (via MCN). He transplanted the 8.0-liter naturally aspirated V10 from a second-generation Dodge Viper GTS into a motorcycle chassis, and by 2009, the aptly-named Millyard Viper V10 was fully built.
The Millyard Viper churns out 500 hp and 525 lb-ft of torque, and doesn't even have a transmission, instead relying on a fixed-ratio direct-drive system to channel that grunt to the rear wheel. It's so powerful that even with its total weight of 1,389 pounds (750 pounds are from the engine alone), the Millyard Viper V10 set a Guinness World Record in 2023 for the fastest speed achieved on a tandem motorcycle: 183.5 mph. And despite being a custom one-off project, the Millyard Viper V10 wasn't a breakdown-prone garage queen. Allen Millyard kept the bike for 17 years, riding it all across the UK and logging over 9,000 miles before auctioning it off in July 2026 for £149,500 (roughly $200,000).
Let's take a quick break from the neck-snapping performance bikes and turn our attention to a long-legged cruiser, and undoubtedly the most American of them all: the Boss Hoss 496. Tennessee-based Boss Hoss has been hand-building V8-powered motorcycles (or rather, street rods) for decades, and the company's most powerful cruiser is fitted with a Chevrolet 8.1-liter big-block V8.
The Boss Hoss 496 doles out 600 hp and 568 lb-ft of torque and comes coupled to a two-speed semi-automatic gearbox with a reverse function. Buyers can even choose between a carburetor or an electronic fuel injection system. And while these numbers are very impressive even by sports car standards, the Boss Hoss 496 is not about tearing your face off in the corners. Instead, this 1226-lb street rod is about devouring miles on the highway and enjoying the feel of the open road, all while riding a veritable tsunami of power and torque. In many ways, it's one of the ultimate cruiser bikes you can buy."

We know that United Airlines CEO Scott Kirby thinks that one of the carrier’s biggest mistakes in the past was pulling out of New York Kennedy Airport (JFK). He’s committed to changing that, and the airline still seems to think that it’s on track to return to the airport in 2027. 
A lot has changed since this was initially a big topic of conversation, including United seemingly no longer being interested in any sort of merger with JetBlue. So with United potentially within a year of returning to JFK, I’d like to talk a bit more about what that could look like, as I’m still not sure I fully understand the strategy.
In this post:
United obviously has a huge hub at Newark Liberty Airport (EWR), and different airline executives have different takes as to the overlap in demand between Newark and Kennedy. 
Through late 2015, United had a relatively small presence at JFK, with a majority of the service consisting of premium transcontinental flights to Los Angeles (LAX) and San Francisco (SFO), with a product that was branded as p.s. The airline operated these flights with specially configured, three cabin Boeing 757-200s. 
However, in late 2015, the airline made the decision to end service at JFK, and instead consolidate flights at EWR. The airline was reportedly losing money on these flights, and just couldn’t compete with American and Delta, which both had major hubs there.
Shortly after that decision was made, Kirby joined United, before eventually being appointed CEO. For as long as he has been at United, Kirby has felt that leaving JFK was one of the carrier’s biggest strategic blunders. In 2021, United briefly returned to JFK, operating transcon routes with Boeing 767-300ERs. However, the airline pulled out by 2022, as it couldn’t get permanent slots, and couldn’t scale service in a way that made sense.
Ever since, United has been determined to return to JFK. We know that United wants up to 20 daily slot pairs at the airport, to offer very frequent coast-to-coast service. So, where do things stand? As flagged by JonNYC, United Chief Commercial Officer Andrew Nocella recently went on record to confirm that United is on track to return to JFK in 2027, which is just around the corner.
United pic.twitter.com/uH561yo8xL
So, how will United secure the slots needed for service?
United management is generally on top of things when it comes to strategy, and I can make sense of most things. However, I still struggle to fully rationalize the plans for returning to JFK. That’s not to say it doesn’t make sense, or that I’m right, but here’s how I think through this:
On the surface, entering a market as a fourth place player, with limited opportunity to grow, little connectivity on the JFK side, and limited ability to have a great ground experience, seems like it could create a challenge.
United Airlines executives still insist the airline will return to JFK in 2027. This seems to involve getting some number of slots from JetBlue (as part of their partnership), and then possibly acquiring slots from some other carriers. It’s still not entirely clear how this will play out, though last we heard, the goal is to get 20 slot pairs, and aggressively fly to LAX and SFO.
I know Kirby thinks it was a big mistake to leave JFK, though I remain a bit confused by how the airline offering high frequency service from JFK to the West Coast, when the markets are already served by three large carriers, will be lucrative.
How do you see United’s JFK strategy playing out?

Client platforms struggle to get memory anyway, right?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Although the consumer PC market is shrinking and will continue to shrink in the coming quarters, Intel is confident that the overwhelming demand from the AI industry for everything that can compute or store data will offset declines in the client space. To that end, the company is reallocating some of its capacity from client to data center CPUs, expanding its Fab 24 in Ireland to make more Xeons, has begun equipment installation into Fab 62 in Arizona ahead of schedule and vows to make 18A chips there, and is expanding its advanced packaging capacity in New Mexico and Malaysia.
 




""The industry continues to navigate one of the most significant supply shortages in history, driven by the unprecedented demand for Super Intelligence,"" said Lip-Bu Tan, chief executive of Intel, during the company's earnings call. ""Against this backdrop, I am pleased by the progress we made growing our server supply in Q3. I am mindful about the ongoing challenges we face in the future quarters to keep pace with server demand.""
Intel's manufacturing expansion comes as demand for server processors continues to exceed supply despite substantial production increases over the next several quarters. The company's Data Center and AI (DCAI) business generated $7.3 billion in revenue in the third quarter of 2026, up 17% sequentially and nearly 80% year-over-year. Intel expects demand for data center CPUs to remain strong in 2027 – 2028 as AI infrastructure operators deploy more CPU-intensive agentic and inference workloads.
To meet demand for Xeon CPUs in the coming years, one of Intel's immediate priorities is to increase output of Xeon 6 'Granite Rapids' processors, which are made using its Intel 3 process technology. The company recently began expanding production capacity at its Fab 24 manufacturing complex near Leixlip, Ireland, but this capacity will only come online by the end of 2027, unless the move can be accelerated.
To further increase its Intel 3 capacity, Intel plans to convert some manufacturing resources at its Oregon facilities from Intel 18A to Intel 3. These resources were originally intended to manufacture Panther Lake client processors, but weaker PC demand has allowed Intel to prioritize higher-demand data center products. The company expects the additional Intel 3 capacity in Oregon to enter production in the second half of 2027.
""What we are taking offline to put Intel 3 into Oregon is 18A wafers, which we are generally going to build Panther Lake products,"" said Dave Zinsner, chief financial officer of Intel. ""We see the opportunity with some of that capacity freed up to go produce more data center wafers.""
Granite Rapids has already become Intel's fastest ramping server processor family, but demand continues to exceed available supply, according to the company's management. Intel is also improving manufacturing yields and reducing production cycle times to extract more usable processors from its existing capacity. According to the company, Intel 3 manufacturing costs have declined by approximately 15% since the first quarter of 2026.
Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.
Intel is also accelerating the expansion of its leading-edge manufacturing operations in Arizona. The company is ramping Intel 18A production at Fab 52 and is accelerating equipment installation into the adjacent Fab 62, which is set to become the company's second high-volume manufacturing facility for 18A, 18A-P, and derivative process technologies.
These investments will support future Intel 18A/18A-P-based products, including server CPUs, although the company has not disclosed the precise allocation of manufacturing capacity between client and data center products at the facility. While the latter is natural, Intel said during the conference call that it expects to win some market share in the client space with its Core Ultra 400-series 'Nova Lake' CPUs, which will require the company to produce these parts in high volumes.
To support its capacity expansion, Intel aggressively procured new tools in the third quarter.
While expanding production of Intel 3 and Intel 18A/18A-P wafers is a must to increase output of data center CPUs, producing more wafers will not necessarily solve Intel's supply problems. The company also faces shortages of advanced organic substrates required to package its multi-chiplet processors (or rather system-in-packages, or SiPs?) that rely on EMIB and/or Foveros 3D packaging technologies. Intel has been working with substrate suppliers to expand their production capacity and expects approximately 40% additional substrate capacity to become available in the second half of 2027.
""We are not only constrained on the wafer side, we are also constrained on the substrate side,"" Zinsner said. ""So, it is important we get the substrate piece of this ramped up to be able to meet the demand.""
In addition, Intel is expanding its advanced packaging operations in New Mexico and Malaysia to support growing demand from internal product groups and external customers. The company already uses its EMIB technology to connect multiple compute dies in Granite Rapids processors, while its newer Clearwater Forest Xeons combine EMIB with Foveros Direct 3D and hybrid bonding to stack Intel 18A compute dies on Intel 3 base dies. Additional packaging capacity will be essential as Intel increases production of these processors and introduces more complex multi-die products as well as assembles advanced system-in-packages using EMIB, EMIB-T, Foveros 3D, and other technologies for external clients.
""On Advanced Packaging, we are pleased by the progress we are making with EMIB and EMIB-T for our internal products,"" Tan said. ""Both continue to see significant external customer traction as well. We continue to improve on execution, working with our supply chain to secure the capacity to meet the needs of our Advanced Packaging customers. We are also seeing robust progress in the readiness of Hybrid Bonding Interconnect (HBI), which is an enabler of 3D chip stacking for our upcoming server products.""
Intel's expansions will require substantial capital. Intel expects its capital expenditure (CapEx) budget to reach approximately $20 billion in 2026 and increase significantly in 2027 (though it did not reveal any firm numbers). The company said firm purchase orders to semiconductor equipment suppliers increased by nearly 40% sequentially as it secured tools for its manufacturing expansion. Intel is also evaluating opportunities to accelerate construction of its semiconductor manufacturing complex in Ohio, where the first major structural truss was recently installed.
""We have made good progress securing the equipment required to support our growth plans with firm purchase orders to equipment suppliers increasing nearly 40% sequentially,"" Zinsner said. ""We are also accelerating clean room build-outs, including Fab 62 in Arizona and exploring opportunities to accelerate in Ohio.
Yet Intel's capacity expansion will take time to produce results. New equipment must be installed and qualified, additional wafers must pass through lengthy manufacturing cycles, and substrate suppliers must increase their output (which will likely require them to procure new tools and install them) before Intel can ship more finished processors. Consequently, the company expects supply constraints to remain particularly severe during the first half of 2027, even as the client PC market is set to continue contracting.
Nevertheless, Intel expects its data center business to continue growing and believes that additional manufacturing capacity, higher yields, and improved substrate availability will enable it to capitalize on demand for AI infrastructure in the second half of 2027 and beyond.
