34,2026-10-31,"While it's far easier to cool components and manage airflow in a standard-size gaming PC tower, there's something about putting unlimited power into a small box, yet it still works. Is a mini PC worth buying? Of course it is, but there are some caveats to consider before making the switch. For day-to-day users who just browse the internet and create spreadsheets for work, there are a few key perks of switching to a mini PC, including saving on desk space. Smaller components that pack the same punch as bigger ones usually command a higher premium too. A mid-range Mini-ITX PC build from scratch can cost over $2,600, but we'll break down that cost later on.
But can gamers and tinkerers build their own custom mini PC from scratch in this day and age, and save money? Technically yes, but not in the obvious sense. Building something like a Mac Mini at home sadly isn't possible, thanks mostly to these ultra-compact computers running on System-on-a-Chip (SoC) architectures. This is where the processor and graphics share a single chip, with the memory built into the same package on a proprietary motherboard. And when you factor in custom cooling and power solutions, it's easy to understand why high-end mini PCs are so expensive right now.
But there's a solution for builders hell-bent on making the smallest PC they can – a Mini-ITX build — or users can simply try these popular budget mini PCs instead of a full-size desktop. Many of the latter also let users upgrade the RAM or storage down the line, instead of being locked into a soldered-in, ultra-slim solution.
While we can't build a true mini PC ourselves, we can take on an ultra-compact SFF (Small Form Factor) desktop build instead. Using the Mini-ITX motherboard standard, builders will be off to a great start thanks to it measuring 6.7 by 6.7 inches, giving a finished build similar dimensions to a current games console. In fact, mini PC builds are becoming more acceptable in PC gaming circles following the strong early demand for Valve's Steam Machine, and it's just a matter of time before pre-built PCs follow suit.
So what's the smallest form factor gaming PC available? For builders, cases such as the Fractal Design Terra or the modular Phanteks Evolv Shift XT have incredibly tight dimensions but still house desktop components. A case like the Fractal Terra has a capacity of 10.4 liters, which is around the size of a bread bin (can someone make a loaf-themed ITX build please?).
Despite the tiny footprint, modern SFF cases use smart PCIe riser cables that allow builders to ""sandwich"" a full-size, dedicated graphics card directly behind the motherboard. The riser itself doesn't cost any gaming performance, though heat can build up quickly in such a tight space. Stuffing high-end hardware into a 10-liter chassis also needs an SFX power supply and a low-profile CPU cooler like the Noctua NH-L12S to make sure the side panels actually close.
A mini-ITX build is going to be as cheap or as expensive as the components chosen, just like a desktop build, but the small form factor tax is definitely a first world problem. Mini-ITX motherboards are generally more expensive than standard ATX boards as manufacturers have to engineer complex, stacked thermal solutions to fit everything into a 6.7-inch square. 
Realistically in 2026, a premium case like the Fractal Terra is around $200, with a high-end Mini-ITX motherboard like the ROG Strix X870-I for around $430. That's $630 before considering a $850 mid-range graphics card, $340 for a CPU, a $160 M.2 SSD, $480 for 32GB DDR5 RAM,  specialized $70 low-profile cooling, and a $130 SFX power supply. All in all, a powerful Mini-ITX PC build will cost over $2,600.
For players who aren't interested in building, there's plenty of pre-build Mini-ITX gaming PCs that don't compromise on performance and will likely have a better price-to-performance ratio instead of buying SFF parts and components in today's market. It's almost always going to be cheaper to have an ATX motherboard-based build, but there's something about having a modular, powerful, and stunning gaming PC glowing in the corner of a battle station. For many, it's worth the premium."
35,2026-10-31,"We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.
Smart home devices can be pretty convenient, offering flexible control, remote management, and even power savings. More importantly, not all smart home upgrades need much elbow grease. Some are easy enough for beginners, while others can be done over a weekend. However, without a whole-home power backup solution, an outage can leave most, if not all, of your smart home gadgets inoperable. Fortunately, gadgets can help in these situations, keeping at least some crucial smart home products running. These include battery backup products that can power your router or modem, as well as other smart home devices.
However, it can be tricky to find the most useful gadgets for different smart home devices. Your smart home has so much going on, and figuring out where to start can be tough. So we have handpicked some of the best options to help you get started. We selected these gadgets based on buyer reviews and, when available, expert feedback.
Because many smart home devices rely on Wi-Fi, your Wi-Fi router and internet modem are two devices you need to keep running during an outage. APC is a reputable brand that offers a nifty UPS that can help with both. Known as the APC BE600M1, this UPS has a built-in 600 VA battery that can deliver up to 330 watts to connected devices.
APC says it can run for 29 minutes at 100 watts. However, since most routers and modems typically consume between 5 and 20 W, you'd average around 20 W, which would extend backup time fivefold to about 2.5 hours. While that should be enough in many situations, you can consider APC's larger battery UPS models, such as the APC BR1500MS2, which claim 73 minutes at 100 W (or 6 hours at 20 W).
You can also use the UPS to power any smart home hub for devices that use local protocols instead of Wi-Fi, such as Z-Wave and Zigbee, or a smart speaker. However, keep in mind that the more devices you add to a UPS, the less backup it will provide during an outage. The APC BE600M1 UPS costs $84, and Amazon shoppers have given it an average rating of 4.5 out of 5. Buyers are generally happy, with many saying it keeps their network up during outages and is ""perfect for home routers."" That said, a small number of buyers report receiving defective units that didn't work, or that their units died after months.
A small UPS can power your Wi-Fi router, but it's not ideal for more power-hungry devices. That is where a portable power station comes in. Bluetti's Elite 300 is a reasonably priced, well-rated option that packs enough power to run many crucial smart home devices. It has a built-in 3,014.4 watt-hour battery, which can deliver 2,400 watts of running power. This makes it a good option for powering your smart speaker, smart display, smart home hub, PoE switch, NVR systems, and more.
It houses four AC outlets, two USB-A ports, and two USB-C ports, including one that supports 140 W charging. More importantly, it can handle fast charging and multiple charging modes. You can charge it from 0% to 100% on AC power in just two hours. Another highlight of the Bluetti portable power station is its compact design, and the company claims it's the smallest 3 kWh option on the market. It also has built-in handles for carrying it around; however, it has no wheels. Moreover, its LFP battery is rated for over 6,000 cycles, which the company says makes it good for 17 years.
It lists at $1,599; however, it's often discounted and can be found for as low as $1,000. Experts at PC Mag, Reviewed, and Tech Radar have given the Bluetti Elite 300 favorable feedback. Experts say it has an excellent port selection, rugged build, good app control, and seamless UPS performance; however, they point out that it's very heavy and the fans can be loud. It has also earned a solid 4.7 out of 5 rating from Amazon shoppers, who appreciate its size and battery capacity. That said, some buyers report issues with customer support.
While you can optionally connect your Echo Dot smart speaker to a UPS or portable power station, a dedicated battery base offers a smoother, more integrated solution. Mission Cables, a brand best known for its Made for Amazon accessories, offers a dedicated battery base for the Echo Dot. It can keep your smart speaker running for up to five hours during an outage. Sadly, it's only compatible with the fourth and fifth generations of the Echo Dot. However, the company also offers similar gadgets for the Echo (4th generation), Echo Pop, and Apple HomePod Mini.
It's easy to install and designed to match the Echo Dot aesthetic, so it doesn't look out of place. It's also reasonably priced at $40, and if you time your purchase right, you might get it at a discount. Amazon shoppers are pretty happy with it, giving it an average buyer rating of 4.5 out of 5. Buyers find it valuable during power outages and like its seamless functionality. However, some buyers worry it can lose its connection to the Echo Dot if moved, affecting the Echo Dot's Wi-Fi connectivity. Some buyers also reported that their units died within weeks or months.
Even with a complete home power backup solution, it's still hard to keep all your smart home devices running during a power outage. However, many plug-in devices can run during a power loss with the right backup solution. Moreover, your battery-powered gadgets will continue running. So, when choosing gadgets that can keep your smart home devices running during an outage, we focused on products that offer battery backup to power one or more smart home products. We refined our selection by considering buyer reviews and, when available, expert feedback. As a result, all our recommendations carry an average buyer rating of at least 4.5 out of 5 on Amazon."
36,2026-10-31,"In a cavernous office in London's AI hub, a group of researchers is preparing to do battle with humanity's oldest enemy.
Isomorphic Labs, a spinoff of Google DeepMind and the brainchild of AI pioneer Demis Hassabis, has an ambitious mission even by the standards of other AI labs: using AI to create drugs that could cure cancer and make disease a thing of the past.
Max Jaderberg, Isomorphic's president and the person running day-to-day operations at the company under Hassabis' aegis as CEO, knows it's a lofty goal. Even so, he is unfazed.
""It's a ridiculous statement, but we really say it with a straight face,"" said Jaderberg, 36, who spoke to Business Insider in an exclusive interview in September. He added that the rate at which Isomorphic employees were coming to him with internal breakthroughs had accelerated from monthly to weekly in the past three years.
""There's no world in five years' time, where all drugs aren't designed by AI,"" he said.
Right now, that goal seems a long way away. AI-designed drugs have been in development for over a decade, but none have yet received FDA approval or completed the clinical trials process, a yearslong regulatory slog that around 90% of drug candidates fail.
Isomorphic has enjoyed an early-mover advantage thanks to AlphaFold, the AI model developed by DeepMind that won Hassabis a Nobel Prize and revolutionized protein mapping, opening the door to AI-designed medicines.
Jaderberg said Isomorphic planned to use its AI models to overcome many of the hurdles that typically trip up promising drug candidates. The company is working on a method of using its models — and its plentiful supply of compute — to predict how the drug molecule will interact with every other molecule in the body, a process that would be far too expensive to test by hand.
In February, Isomorphic unveiled a ""drug design engine"" that the company said improves on AlphaFold.
Isomorphic plans to use it to precisely engineer molecules so that patients can receive the minimum amount of drug needed, Jaderberg said. The hope is that this will avoid complications caused by high concentrations of the drug building up in their bloodstream.
The company's ultimate goal, Jaderberg said, is to be able to predict the interaction between molecules so well that its scientists will not even have to step inside a lab while designing a drug.
Jaderberg said the company's internal drug design efforts are focused on developing breakthrough drugs for cancer, conditions related to the immune system, and inflammatory diseases.
""There's a lot of companies that just say, 'Here's a medicine in the clinic, can we just make a small change and make an incremental improvement that will allow us to capture some of that market?'"" he said.
""I think we see a bigger opportunity, which is to go after medicines that haven't been made before,"" Jaderberg added.
Jaderberg, who was part of Isomorphic's founding team, was recruited by Hassabis to DeepMind straight out of university. He initially approached Hassabis to be an angel investor in the computer vision startup he founded as a student, but Hassabis instead offered to buy the company outright to bring Jaderberg to DeepMind.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
A former engineering student from a family of cancer researchers and doctors (his grandfather ran drug trials as a cardiologist in Sweden), Jaderberg was elevated to Isomorphic's president in January, having previously served as chief AI officer.
Now, he faces the challenge of actually shepherding Isomorphic's AI-designed drugs from prototypes to patients.
So far, Isomorphic has struck research partnerships with pharma giants Novartis, Johnson & Johnson, and Eli Lilly. Jaderberg said the AI startup's new drug design engine has also provided a foundation for the company to begin testing AI-designed drugs in preclinical trials, with human trials not far away.
""If we want to solve all disease, you need a drug design engine that doesn't just work for one disease area, one therapeutic area, one indication, one type of protein, even one modality. We need something that works for everything,"" said Jaderberg.
Isomorphic's president declined to provide a timeline for clinical trials, which the company has previously pushed back. However, Jaderberg said the company had not experienced any new delays and had been building up a clinical development team in Boston over the past year in preparation for the milestone.
Even with cutting-edge AI models and a healthy supply of compute, the barrier Isomorphic faces is daunting. Navigating the clinical trials gauntlet and taking drugs from concepts on a computer screen to real-life medicines is one of the most grueling challenges in biochemistry.
Researchers build molecules designed to target a specific protein, then subject them to an exhaustive trial-and-error process. Teams often end up going back to the drawing board when the drug fails to work on living cells as predicted or causes unexpected side effects.
Eventually, a few designs reach the stage where they can be tested on animals. After years of testing, a tiny selection might advance to human trials.
The entire process can take over a decade. Successful drug researchers can see just one or two of their drugs make it to patients across their entire career. Many more will have none.
Jaderberg said there were a few reasons for the 90% failure rate drug candidates face.
Traditional biotechs are often funding-constrained, he said, so they have to take a chance on molecules they know are not perfect. The drug molecule might hit ""off-target"" proteins that can cause unintended side effects and toxicity.
In Jaderberg's view, however, the underlying reasons for failure run deeper. Unsuccessful drug candidates usually fail because they are based on an incorrect understanding of biology, he said.
Building a truly revolutionary drug design engine requires a comprehensive model of biology that can understand how proteins interact, and the step-by-step chemical reactions that dictate how cells grow and die, he said.
""I think that points to a deeper and longer-term research goal that we're already underway on. Just how can you understand biology mechanistically better?"" said Jaderberg.
Despite Isomorphic's ambition, the road to the first AI-designed cancer drugs is still uncertain.
AI-first drug design startups like Recursion and Schrödinger have reported setbacks for their AI-designed medications, and some high-profile drug candidates have failed to move beyond Phase 1 trials.
Experts have urged caution. In a March essay, Emilia Javorsky, a director at the Future of Life Institute, warned that while AI excels at solving problems that are rules-based or data-rich, biology is neither. Javorsky criticized ""flippant promises by tech CEOs"" that their technologies will quickly usher in treatments for diseases that have tormented humanity for millennia.
Lennard Lee, an associate professor at the University of Oxford and project lead for the UK's cancer vaccine AI & supercomputing project, told Business Insider that AI has transformed scientists' ability to predict molecular properties and design new medicines. However, he warned that these medicines still face their ""ultimate test.""
""Drug development remains extraordinarily difficult, and promising predictions frequently fail when confronted with biological reality,"" Lee said.
Charlotte Deane, a professor of structural bioinformatics at the University of Oxford, said that many proteins within the body have a fluid structure that is hard for even the most advanced AI tools to model.
""We all aim for the stars and that's exactly what we should be doing. But it is still a long process to show that a drug works,"" Deane said.
The success of AlphaFold may have given Isomorphic an early lead in the race to discover cancer-beating drugs, but both Anthropic and OpenAI have unveiled their own drug-discovery efforts in recent months.
In April, OpenAI announced GPT‑Rosalind, a dedicated frontier AI model built to shorten the time from drug discovery to approval, and announced partnerships with pharma giants Novo Nordisk and Moderna. In October, the ChatGPT maker said it had acquired AI-powered drug development platform Orchestra Bio for an undisclosed sum.
Anthropic, which recently revealed plans for a blockbuster IPO, said this month it had partnered with six life sciences organizations to deploy AI throughout drug development and accelerate the clinical trial process. The AI lab has also set up its own internal drug discovery efforts that are targeting treatments for diseases, including rare childhood forms of muscular dystrophy, with Anthropic aiming to begin human trials as early as late 2028.
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei made his belief that AI could cure most major diseases in the next five to 10 years the opening salvo of his call to pace the frontier last month, revealing that he had survived an ""early-stage cancer"" himself.
In an X post in August, Amodei went further. He suggested that delivering real progress in the fight against diseases like cancer would be necessary to help the AI labs turn around the increasingly skeptical public view of the AI boom and justify the enormous cost in money and land.
""At this point, saying that AI will cure cancer is more a cliche than it is inspiring, and most people think it is deceptive. The thing that will work is actually curing cancer,"" Amodei wrote.
Jaderberg said that frontier labs like Anthropic and OpenAI, both of which are eyeing massive public listings, are more interested in ""building up hype"" and communicating results than Isomorphic was.
""We don't want to over hype what we're doing. We're not trying to sell software. We're not trying to license our models. We want to make medicines for patients,"" he added.
He said he ultimately envisioned Isomorphic as a vertically integrated drugmaker that designs, builds, and ships its medicines directly to patients; a ""SpaceX for drug discovery.""
Isomorphic boasts one huge advantage over its rivals: Hassabis, who remains the company's CEO after stepping away from his role as head of DeepMind in August to take on a broader research role at Alphabet.
In his post announcing his new role, Hassabis said he would be ""leaning into"" his work at Isomorphic ""to help cure disease."" Jaderberg said there had been no change in Isomorphic's organizational structure, but added that the company was already benefiting from Hassabis' new focus.
""He believes this is one of the most important missions we should be pushing. We have a ton of Demis time already to help push us on that vision, and having even more of his headspace is going to be positive for us,"" Jaderberg said.
Jaderberg added that he expects Isomorphic's singular goal to give it another advantage over Anthropic and OpenAI in the most hotly-contested part of the AI business: hiring.
Both Anthropic and OpenAI have been rocked by high-profile departures in recent months. Researchers from both companies have quit with dramatic warnings of catastrophic safety risks and AI-driven apocalypses.
Speaking before those departures, Jaderberg said that Isomorphic offered researchers a route to apply AI in a way that would have a positive real-world impact.
""I think there are a lot of AI scientists and engineers who actually want to see that final impact and want it to be something really tangible and different to what I think is being offered as impact from the current frontier labs,"" Jaderberg said.
""As a species, we have to be driving this technology toward the good applications as well,"" he added.
Jump to"

38,2026-10-31,"Sanket is a GNOME Shell extension that lets you rejig how the desktop’s date menu, calendar, notifications and Quick Settings look and behave – and then some.
It can put notifications beneath the Quick Settings menu in a floating island, or embedded inside of it. Quick Settings toggles can shrink to dots when space is tight, and expand again as and when needed, Android-style.
As well as shrinking Quick Settings pills to dots, other menu tweaks let you show up two rows of controls and choose if volume and brightness sliders remain visible in compact mode. An ‘edit’ mode lets you drag and drop the order of controls too.
With notifications no longer having to sit inside the calendar popover, it becomes more of dashboard than a catch-all. Forecasts via GNOME Weather compact to a single animated icon, and timezones in GNOME Clocks can be shown with analog icons.
Sanket does change how notifications are managed, making only the most recent visible. A bit jarring to me since expanding the list (wherever it’s place) is more effort than simply seeing them. Notifications can be pinned, which is neat. 
But what makes this more useful is that you don’t need to use everything it offers. 
There is a lot that you can change using Sanket – or not. You can keep the stock layout and just use it for other things, like hiding the calendar header or ‘upcoming events’ lists. 
I like that its settings includes visual previews of many layouts and options, so you know where elements shift without performing the ol’ ‘change something then work out what it did’ routine.
If you’ve never looked at GNOME Shell’s calendar or Quick Settings menus and thought ‘I wish I could change that bit’ then Sanket may not be that appealing.
But if you enjoy customising your desktop, or trying to make GNOME look more like other desktop environments or OSes – combined with Dash to Panel this can create a more ChromeOS-y inspired setup – then it’s well worth a look.
Sanket works on GNOME Shell 45 through 51. You can install it from the GNOME Extensions website (the desktop Extensions Manager app lets you browse and install extensions without needing a web browser).
Learn more about it on its GitHub page.

47,2026-10-31,"The Site
WordPress.com announced this past month that paid plans will be allowed to upload premium themes downloaded or purchased outside of the stock selection they maintain.  Or fail to maintain, which is probably the real reason for this.
I have suddenly gone from “All the supported themes kind of suck for my needs” to “There are too many choices!”  So I’ll have to see if I can wrestle with that problem.
Also in this month was the PHP2Shell critical vulnerabilities crisis that got a lot of site owners in a panic.  WP.com assured us that sites it hosted were all protected… and what would I be able to do about it in any case?
Then there was the Monday morning I woke up and found that the classic editor had once again disappeared from the hosting panel.  The block editor is probably fine for something, but writing isn’t it.  This turned out to be related to a changed WP.com made to the dashboard settings, where if you are set to “default” you get the classic editor and if you are set to “classic” you do NOT get the classic editor.  Somebody make sense of that.
War is Peace and Default is Classic
Anyway, I was back up and running by lunch.
And then, somewhere along the line in October, the site passed the 8,000 post mark.  I didn’t know you could stack crap that high, but here we are.
One Year Ago
The deal for the Saudis and Jared Kushner to buy out Electronic Arts had been finalized and approved and I was saying farewell to whatever the company meant at that point.
Amazon Games gave up on big online games, announcing New World would be closing and that their ever changing plans for something in Middle-earth would be changing again.  They were more interested in AI driven slop like their Snoop Dogg voiced Chaos in Court title.
There was also the big Pokemon Go update that boosted the level cap to 80… which was a bit of a let down actually.
Over on the Enad Global 7 front I was wondering if we were going to get any EverQuest 3 news.
With that not forthcoming, EverQuest did announce the Shattering of Ro expansion, while EverQuest II did the same for the Rage of Cthurath expansion.
Meanwhile, on the LOTRO front, SSG was giving people a chance to transfer characters off of the recently closed 32-bit worlds.
I was also wondering if we were going to see any more updates in No Man’s Sky to follow up Voyagers and expand on the corvette thing.  They seemed to lack for a landing pad, for starters.  The Breach expedition did give us some new corvette parts at least.  I went off to try that. Potshot and I also started to search for a paradise planet to settle on.
Over in New Eden CCP launched the annual Crimson Harvest event.  They also announced the candidate list for the CSM20 election, published the September 2025 economic report, announced the upcoming Catalyst expansion, kicked off Alliance Tournament XXI, began the CSM20 elections, and ramped up the final week of Crimson Harvest with new rewards.
In null sec Fraternity announced its invasion of The Initiative space, something they would never, ever come to regret, and PanFam pulled back their staging system, a portent to their coming collapse.
Also, the Reavers SIG turned eleven, though it existed mostly as a Discord chat channel by then.
In Palworld we were poaching in the No.1 Wildlife Sanctuary after which we were off to the mountain cold.  The it was on to a fiery mountainside climb.
We were also taking a look at the cozy title Palia.  We ran around and made ramen together.
In a Friday bullet points post I looked into Valheim coming to PlayStation, Microsoft laying people off and raising the price of XBox game pass, Sackbird studios being formed from layoff refugees, the demise of Greg Street’s Fantastic Pixel Castle plans, Embracer spinning off Coffee Stain while loading it with debt, and Ship of Heroes complaining about being review bombed.
And on the streaming front we watched Kaos with Jeff Goldblum, Rick & Morty season 8, and House of Guinness.  I wrote whole posts about each.
Five Years Ago
In a bit of irony I unleashed ads on the site in order to pay for the premium WordPress.com plan, otherwise they would be unleashing ads on the site.  But at least I was getting some of that ad money guess.  I kept that up every so often before finally deciding I had done that enough.
Facebook changes its name to Meta and Mark Zuckerberg announced they were going all-in on the idea of a VR based online metaverse.   John Carmack had some issues with the plan, while reality… made Zuck look like the man-boy dumbshit he is.  Nothing since has changed my view on that.
Then acting EG7 CEO Ji Ham spoke about the company and its plans.  Meanwhile EverQuest announced the Terror of Luclin expansion and EverQuest II announced the Visions of Vertovia expansion.  It was also Panda time in EQII.
Those expansions got me on to a bit of a comparison between the EQ expansion machine and WoW.
Blizzard announced the WoW Classic Season of Mastery plan, set to launch in November, an attempt to give a slightly different vanilla experience… and a temporary one at that as it closed down just a year later.
There would also be no BlizzCon 2022.  Covid and stuff.
We were getting into Diablo II Resurrected as a group.  We remade our group after a bit, working our way through the first act.  We got stuck into Act II pretty quickly, making it through to the end.
On my own I played a necromancer through the first difficultly level.  I was playing on Battle.net for some reason, where there were queues to get online.
Carbot and Honest Game Trailers had their own special D2R videos.
Amazon Games’ New World was live and I was trying to play… though there were issues.
In EVE Online there was a bit of a mining boom in the wake of World War Bee.  CCP was also going back to native MacOS support.  They also introduced CRAB beacons to the game and changed the tax structure.  And then there was the return of the Crimson Harvest event.
the Reavers SIG turned seven years old.
I also did an EVE Online Friday Bullet Points post about Quasar, Totality Day, Faction Warfare, mega skill point offers, monocles, and an exploration of the new user experience.
In my series on immersion I tackled Minecraft.
I wrote something about Words with Friends 2.
At home we were getting ready for No Time to Die by watching the previous four Daniel Craig Bond films… and rating them.  And then we went and saw it and I wrote a bit of a review.
Likewise, with the coming of the new Dune movie (part I) we watched the 1984 version of the tale.  The new film got a bit of a review here too.
Ten Years Ago
Amazon’s game studio announced some games including the alleged MMO New World.  Being given almost no information about New World did not stop some people from banging the hype drum, boosting expectations, and generally setting themselves up for disappointment.  I wonder how that turned out?
Also announced was the Nintendo Switch, which would allow millennials to play video games at roof-top parties, if the trailer was to be take literally.
Civilization VI launched and became the first Civ title I did not acquire at the soonest opportunity.  It just didn’t excite me.  Instead I was getting my strategy game fix with Stellaris.
I reviewed the Mineserver Kickstarter campaign a year after its launch.  The units were nine months late at that point, with no end to the wait in sight.  The whole thing eventually failed to get off the ground.  Another Kickstarter crash and burn.
I made it to level 20 in Pokemon Go.  There was also a Halloween event.
Tom Chilton, of unfortunate quote fame, let out another one when he said that World of Warcraft had over 10 million subscribers again after the Legion expansion launch.  Blizzard, having taken a vow of silence on subscription numbers the year before, denied everything and claimed he was mis-quoted.
Over at Daybreak they were discontinuing game cards and taking H1Z1: King of the Kill off of Station Cash as an RMT currency.  They did give us firm dates for the two Kurnak based expansions for EverQuest and EverQuest II.
In EVE Online, while we were fortifying Delve our foes in the Casino War began to turn on each other, with Pandemic Legion and their followers declaring their intention to take Tribute and Vale of the Silent away from Circle of Two and TEST.  All I could do was give a Nelson Muntz, “Haw-haw!” at that turn of events.
Still, that wasn’t half as much fun as CCP declaring casinos against the EULA with the coming of the Ascension expansion.  Our foes would have to actually earn ISK in-game.  I wondered what that would do to the economy.
We also got the YC118.9 update which, among other things, meant the death of the in-game browser.  We did get breast cancer awareness skins.  Yay, pink skins!
At our end of New Eden there was a lot to do.  I was fighting the Blood Raider menace with my Ishtar and looking for ways to help the coalition.  Reavers turned two years old and were blowing up citadels and fighting over timers in Querious.  The coalition itself was ranging up into Fountain with a new doctrine.
CCP was also busy on the VR front, launching EVE Valkyrie and EVE Gunjack, the first games in the VR offensive.  EVE Valkyrie was an Occulus Rift launch title FFS!  All of their VR titles would sputter and fail… along with VR itself, frankly… and later be shut down, with everybody involved laid off… unless they were Icelandic citizens.  They get lifetime employment.
And in World of Warcraft the Legion got its first post-launch content drop while my own enthusiasm for the expansion was starting to wane.
Finally, I was at EVE Vegas, but since it lasted through Halloween I didn’t post about it until November.  Also, a new version of my blogger feed was operating in the side bar.
Fifteen Years Ago
We all said farewell to Steve Jobs.
Tobold was predicting that CCP would go bankrupt in 2012.  I am NEVER EVER going to let that one go… at least not until Hilmar does the place in.
I summed up what I liked and disliked about Need for Speed: World.
I got Civilization II running on Windows 7 64-bit.  People still come here for that article.  I managed to get it to run on Windows 10 since then.  But it didn’t work back then with my original 16-bit copy, I had to get the 32-bit version, Civilization II Gold Edition.
I started another contest, this time for Azerothian travel posters.
I recalled the start of the alien invasion!
EA was planning to unify all of its free to play online games to use the same in-game currency… something that never actually happened… also, I think all the games on the list are dead now.
Nintendo was talking about augmented reality on their new 3DS console.  Five years later we got Pokemon Go… on iOS and Android.
The announcement of their Unity release got Gaff and I back to TorilMUD to mess around with the Tako Demon.  I had to work to remember some MUD command syntax to get his corpse afterwards.
Then there was BlizzCon.  The instance group was already bemused by Cataclysm, and the mention of Pandas did not revive our spirits.  Pet Battles seemed interesting at the time.  In coming to grips with the Pandaria announcements, I assumed that I would end up buying the expansion.
And then there were other WoW related things, like the announcement of the Guardian Cub, which some people predicted would be like PLEX in EVE Online.  Not quite, but it probably provided test data for the eventual WoW Token idea.
In EverQuest, on the Fippy Darkpaw server, after much complaining, SOE seemed to suddenly wake up and start doing things.  Problems were, you know, solved!  It was becoming a kinder, gentler server.  Sort of.
And then there was Rift.  We started scouting it as a potential home for in instance group after the disaster in EverQuest II.  The scouting went well enough that we all ended up in Telara.
Finally, I was sorting through many press releases and wondering where this so-called GDC Hall of Fame was.
Twenty Years Ago
The site got a mention in VirginWorlds Podcast episode 33 while Blessing of Kings was telling me how to be a raider in World of Warcraft.
I was defending instancing and game company forums as well as moaning about the fact that I couldn’t find a decent video card for my AGP motherboard.  Also, Blizzard’s WoW patcher was pissing me off as well while Lore was mocking WoW groups. [Damn, that was done in Flash, so I guess it is gone now.]
Our Saturday night World of Warcraft instance group completed our first full dungeon run in the Deadmines.  That meant my first dungeon run write up.  And my second.  But we got it on our third try.  Then it was off to The Wailing Caverns.  Did anybody have a five player dungeon adventure path like WoW back then?
I decided in EVE Online that covert ops would be a good career choice.  It certainly sounds cool.  However, the effort was cut short when I got to some of the pricier skills.
When it came to the then upcoming Lord of the Rings Online, I was up in arms about pointy ears and monster play, while the approaching Age of Conan made me think more about Barsoom.
I felt that fall urge to run around in EverQuest,  no doubt prompted by another EQ nostalgia post, plus I wanted to take some pictures of Faydwer in order to compare them to the upcoming EverQuest II expansion, Echoes of Faydwer.
I also jumped into EverQuest II and got all confused (nothing new there) about the trade skill changes that occurred during the 10 months I was away.
And I spent some time carping about MMO pricing plans and wondering what pen and paper games might make good MMOs.
Twenty-Five Years Ago
Dark Age of Camelot launched.  While I never played it… we were about to have a new baby in the house and we were having internet issues, and the world was falling apart due to 9/11… I had some friends who gave it a try.  I remember it being billed as “EverQuest without the suck” at some point.  Anyway, it is still alive, which is surprising because EA owned it… and still owns it, through Broadsword runs it for them.
Civilization III also showed up back in October of 2001, and I most definitely bought a copy of that.  I liked a lot of the ideas it brought, but it lost some of the raw simplicity of its predecessor.  I ended up playing a lot more Civ II even after Civ III was around.
Then there was Motor City Online, which was a bit of a precursor to Need for Speed: World.  I am kind of sad I missed it.  Of course, it was an EA MMO, so they scrapped it in less than two years, opting to go all in on The Sims Online.
Thirty Years Ago
Marathon Infinity, the last of the Marathon series of shooters from Bungie came out.  That was the era of the awkwardly shaped software boxes.  I owned a copy.
Most Viewed Posts in October
Out of the 20 posts on the list, only 19 and 20 were published this month.  But you can see the theme for what was popular; WoW nostalgia, Enshrouded, a bit of No Man’s Sky, Pokemon Go, and a couple old favorites.
Game Time by ManicTime and Nintendo
I had eight titles with time recorded in October.
EVE Online
I spent a good chunk of my gaming time in New Eden this month, mostly pursuing the Crimson Harvest event objectives and working on that achievement to visit all of the systems in Amarr space.  I finished the former… I did not get a Molok BPC sadly… and then kind of left off on the latter with 612 of 689 systems visited.  I’ll get to that later.
No Man’s Sky
The start of the month saw me finishing up the Our Journey Continues expedition.  But once that was done I moved on to other things… though we did get the Forsaken update just this week.  I did briefly peek in on that.
Over the Hill / Art of Rally
Somewhat unexpected, Over the Hill showed up and gave Potshot and I something to fiddle with while waiting for WoW Forever to arrive.  It is a simple yet fulfilling title which clearly ate up some time.   I also reinstalled Art of Rally just to take a look at the previous Funselektor driving game.  I am much better at off road objectives that don’t have a timer than rally racing, that is for sure.  But I’ll get to that.
Pokemon Go
You’d think I could have gotten three more max battles this month… and I would have been there had I been willing to use the cash shop to unlock the artificial limits imposed by the Saudis… why is it always them?  Anyway, that will be done in November for sure.
Stardew Valley 
My daughter and I have carried on, though we missed a weekend here and there due to travel, work, and school.  Still, we try to get together to play when we can.
Stars Reach
Playable Worlds has been pushing hard to get people to log in, holding events every weekend, though that hasn’t seemed to boost their numbers anywhere close to where things need to be for financial success.  I dropped in a couple of times, though not during events due to time conflicts, and found very little that interested me.  Builders have grabbed land around the portals on the few planets, outside of which is the desolation zone where they have strip mined, which was a predictable outcome.  I ended up uninstalling the game.  Maybe something interesting will arrive at some future date.
Valheim
I have been lightly playing Valheim since it hit 1.0 last month.  There was something wrong with the original world I started off with, so spawned a new world with a different seed where things seemed to be going better.  The exploration bug seemed to be satisfied more so with Over the Hill in the back half of the month, but I’ll get back to Valheim I am sure.
WoW Forever
I did not play as much of the beta as I thought I would, but I played enough to sell me on the whole idea.  It is fine.  We’ll see how it ends up going once it launches.
Coming Up
Kind of a big month coming up.
Probably the biggest video game news will be the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI.  Since it will be on consoles only initially, and since I barely started GTA V… 98 minutes played back in 2019… that will likely be a non-event for me.
And for Nintendo peeps, there is The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time coming in November as well.  I think this video sums up the tension between the two launches pretty well.

More on my front, we will see WoW Forever go live next week.  That should be comedy and chaos for at least a week as Blizz tries to deal with the onslaught of new players, old players, sight seers, bots, gold farmers, and RMT spammers trying to rush the gates.
Back in 2019 I declared that first day login issues and the like were a problem the industry was never going to solve.  We now have the most popular, well funded MMORPG studio poised to test that thesis.  They know what is coming.  Can they prove me wrong?  And will it really matter if it all settles down in a week or so anyway? We shall see!
EVE Online also has the Fractured Skies expansion coming out on November 10th.  Expect them to throw some free game time and have login rewards to entice people to try out their new stuff.  While PC MMOs probably don’t have much to fear from GTA VI, CCP/Fenris trying to soft launch, early access, paid alpha test EVE Vanguard within a week of that does feel like folly.  But there was never going to be a good time to do that, now was there?
Finally, the US has a big mid-term election on Tuesday where only voter fraud and blatant electoral interference should save the Republicans.  Can their evil malice overcome their stunning incompetence?  We shall see.
Is it really a decade since New World was announced? Feels like half that. There’s that thing about time going faster as you get older but it seems remarkably inconsistent to me. Some things, like that one, seem to have only just happened, while others that really did only just happen seem to have been around forever. I’m often looking at some game I feel like I’ve been playing for years and realizing it only came out a few months ago…

51,2026-10-31,"In 1955, rock and roll legends Bill Haley and His Comets recorded a 12-bar blues standard, written and first recorded earlier that year by Louisiana singer-songwriter Bobby Charles, called See You Later, Alligator. 
Haleyâs track was released early the following year, and although it didnât hit the top of the charts, See You Later, Alligator nonetheless proved successful enough to become his third and final million-selling record in the U.S. Its popularity was later cemented by Haley performing the song in his 1956 big-screen musical Rock Around the Clock (filmed to cash in on the success of his earlier 1954 hit) that likewise became a global phenomenonânot least thanks to reports of unruly teenagers dancing in cinema aisles and causing riots wherever it was shown. 
Given both the song and the tie-in movie's success, it might be tempting to presume that it is responsible for ""see you later, alligator"" becoming a snappy, slangy way of saying goodbye to someoneâand some etymological sources might even claim precisely that. But in fact, the opposite is true: Haley's song merely helped to circulate and further popularize a phrase that had already long been in use by the time his record was released. 
The Oxford English Dictionary's earliest record of this phrase dates from just a year before Haley and Charles' song, when ""see you later, alligator"" was explained as ""meaning goodbye"" in a guide to contemporary slang that appeared in a Nevada newspaper article. The linguist and etymologist Pascal TrÃ©guer has since unearthed an earlier record, from a newspaper in Hawaii in 1952, which likewise listed ""see you later, alligator"" as an example of ""the everyday 'slang' many high school students use."" But earlier still, the same phrase was used in an article in the Lincoln Heights Bulletin from Los Angeles, California, way back in December 1945: 
JINGLE BELLS!! JINGLE BELLS!! Christmas is just around the corner â¦ There are just two weeks shopping time till Xmas Day â¦ so don't forget all those luscious presents you are buying for the family and friends. See you later alligators!!
Tellingly, this article doesn't feel the need to explain the phrase's meaning, but simply uses it as a jokey sign-off. That suggests that way back in the mid 1940s ""see you later, alligator"" was already a familiar enough way of saying goodbye. So if it doesn't come from Haley's song, and it was already in established use a decade earlier, where did it come from? Well, until any further earlier evidence comes to light, the most likely theory here is that ""see you later, alligator"" is a hangover from a 19th and early 20th century fad for so-called rhyming slang. 
Thought to have emerged in London in the mid 1800s, rhyming slang was a faddish way of talking in which commonly used words were replaced with snappy rhyming equivalents. Stairs, for instance, became ""apples and pears."" A wife was a husband's ""trouble and strife."" To believe something was to ""Adam and Eve"" it. Later, during Britain's transportation era, when convicts were routinely shipped from the UK to see out their sentences elsewhere, rhyming slang spread to Australia too and quickly caught on there. And as any Brit or Aussie will doubtless tell you, many of these phrases are still well known (if not perhaps quite so well used) as they were almost two centuries ago. 
In the 20th century, however, a wave of postwar Australian immigration appears to have brought this technique to the western United States. ""See you later, alligator"" was presumably either imported from Australia or, as is more likely, coined in the U.S. around this time. Rhyming slang itself doesnât seem to have caught on for long in America, yet it was a fad just long enoughâespecially among students or teenagersâfor a handful of these phrases to fall into use. So, as well as ""See you later, alligator,"" and its stock reply ""In a while, crocodile,"" you might once have heard the likes of ""Do you know what I mean, jellybean?,"" ""Don't get tough, powder puff,"" and ""Whatâs buzzin', cousin?""
Not all of these have stood the test of time, but ""see you later, alligator"" did. And it's likely the success of Haley's record and the movie in which it appeared that helped to reinforce its use and the popularity it has had ever since.

We spoke to the experts

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's the largest cryptocurrency theft in history: hackers from North Korea allegedly swiped $6.36 billion in funds from Binance in the space of just 20 minutes, which has left customers reeling and the financial industry in shock.
The heist raises all kinds of questions about how this was allowed to happen, what might happen next, and whether Binance customers are going to get any of their stolen money back.
Even if you never owned any cryptocurrency before, there are bigger questions too — like what this means for global financial stability, and what the aftershocks might be for everyone outside the Binance world.Looking for a quick five-minute primer on the Binance hack? We've been speaking to industry experts to get some answers...
 



The basics are now pretty clear — $6.36 billion in cryptocurrency was stolen from the Binance exchange in the space of just 20 minutes. Security analysts are pointing the blame at North Korea's TraderTraitor hacking group, which has a history of this kind of heist. What's less clear is exactly how the hackers got access. Early reports identified a Binance employee's compromised laptop as a potential way in, though it now seems there's more to the story.
""A compromised laptop may explain where an attack started, but it does not, on its own, explain how billions of dollars could leave an exchange,"" Pieter Arntz, researcher at cybersecurity firm Malwarebytes, told TechRadar. ""The real question is what allowed that initial foothold to become access to withdrawal systems — and which safeguards failed to stop unauthorized transactions.""
""According to blockchain analysts, the attacker spent at least a day and a half preparing, including testing their tools on public blockchains where anyone could see,"" Ben Bernstein, manager of cybersecurity advisors at Huntress, told TechRadar. ""Then most of the $6.3 billion left Binance's wallets in about 20 minutes. That's common in attacks we investigate. The attacker does most of the work long before anything is stolen.""
 



Binance has promised to reimburse customer losses through its backup and emergency funds, but in the meantime we're seeing deposits and withdrawals frozen on the platform while the hack is investigated. It's a warning that even the largest and most sophisticated exchanges can still be vulnerable.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
""Binance says it'll cover every cent,"" Nigel Green, CEO of the deVere Group, said in a press statement. ""I hope so. The emergency fund is around $1.2 billion and the gap is several times bigger, so the rest has to come from the company’s own balance sheet. The market will want to see the numbers, and the new proof of reserves can’t come soon enough.""
""Long term holders have just been reminded, very expensively, why keys kept offline matter. Crypto has spent years pushing back against heavy regulation, and plenty of those arguments were fair. This one's harder to argue with. Platforms holding billions of other people's money need enforceable security standards. I expect regulators in the US, Europe and Asia will likely move faster after this week,"" he added.
In the meantime, the US Secret Service has said in a statement that it's ""working around the clock"" with partners in the private sector ""to identify, freeze, trace, and recover funds"" that have been stolen from Binance.
 



Whether you're a Binance customer or not, you need to keep your wits about you over the next few days. It's likely that scams trying to literally cash in on the hack will start appearing — perhaps warning you that you need to quickly claim refunds, lock your account against a particular threat, or go through a bogus verification process.
""Criminals routinely use major breaches as a hook for phishing campaigns,"" Dray Agha, senior manager for tactical response at Huntress, told TechRadar. ""Consumers should be highly suspicious of any emails or messages claiming to be from Binance offering urgent account recovery steps. You must only check your account status through the official application or website rather than clicking links in unsolicited messages.""
According to the team at Huntress, you should watch out for social media posts, emails, and text messages that use the Binance hack as a hook — perhaps a fake news story suggesting that now's the time to buy crypto, while prices are down.
As always, think twice about following links or entering sensitive information into online forms and portals you're not familiar with.
 



If you don't own any cryptocurrency, the good news is that you're largely going to be unaffected by what's happened here. There's not going to be a stock market crash, or a run on the banks, or a global recession — not because of this hack, at least. However, it's always worth weighing up your own personal privacy and security setup.
""If you don't own crypto, the direct impact on you is small,"" Ben Bernstein from Huntress told us. ""Your bank account and your card are not affected by this. The bigger takeaway for consumers is that attackers often get in by targeting people rather than technology, through a convincing email, a fake login page or a phone call from someone posing as IT support,"" he said.
""That makes security awareness everyone's job, at home and at work. If you pause before you click a link, approve a login or share a code, you're making it harder for an attacker, whether they're after your savings or your employer's systems,"" he added.
 



This clearly raises a lot of questions about the safety of cryptocurrency and the exchanges that it sits on — and of course it's far from the first crypto heist we've seen. In the longer term, it may lead to less confidence in crypto as a whole, but the experts we spoke to don't think it will bring the industry down. What will be important is making sure the risks of this happening again are minimized as much as possible.
""Despite the scale of the theft, this is not a threat to global financial stability,"" Feike Hacquebord, Principal Threat Researcher at TrendAI, told TechRadar. ""Around half of the stolen funds have already been frozen, while the regulated banking system is well equipped to absorb a shock of this size. This is a major crisis for Binance, not a crisis for the global financial system.""
""The change I’d like to see, for crypto firms, banks and every other business, is working on the assumption that someone will get in eventually,"" Ben Bernstein told us. ""Keeping attackers out matters, but it isn't enough on its own. Companies also need someone watching around the clock, especially for logins and account activity that don't look right, so they can find and remove an intruder before the money starts moving,"" he added.

Furious that her celebrity makeup mogul ex allegedly got away with hiding $15 million in a $150 million divorce battle, a jilted wife is demanding her own legal and forensic teams pay her more than $5.5 million for dropping the ball.
Yosi Cohen, 63, of Manhattan, the director and co-founder of the celebrity-favorite makeup brand Natasha Denona, filed for divorce against his wife Adi, 56, in Manhattan Supreme Court in 2019, according to court records. The divorce proceedings are ongoing.
The high-end cosmetics brand, founded in 2016, has been worn by stars including Kim Kardashian, Dua Lipa and Oprah Winfrey. Its namesake makeup artist, Natasha Denona, has worked with A-listers such as Gal Gadot and Bar Refaeli.
The divorce records are sealed, but in a separate lawsuit filed Oct. 25 in Manhattan Supreme Court, Adi accused her former divorce attorney, Daniel H. Stock, and forensic accounting firm KLG Business Valuators and Forensic Accountants of mishandling the case and undermining her efforts to uncover $15 million in real estate deals and secret bank accounts she claims her husband hid.
Yosi’s allegedly shady behavior included transferring money to a “post-commencement real estate entity” while falsely claiming it was acquired after the divorce proceeded, Adi claims in the lawsuit.
He also allegedly routed money through a web of companies, including Natasha Denona, which allegedly dropped $2 million into a real estate investment on Yosi’s behalf, according to the lawsuit. 
Adi blames Stock and KLG for missing critical deadlines, overbilling her and ultimately ruining her chances of recovering the money Yosi allegedly hid from the marital estate.
“[Adi] was deprived of the means to develop the facts concerning the array of marital assets in this high-stakes divorce,” the complaint reads. 
“[Her] husband had a documented history of falsifying records in the matrimonial action.”
Stock, who specializes in high-net-worth divorces, and KLG were hired to trace the allegedly hidden money, but Adi claims they failed to deliver.
She claimed Stock left several subpoenas with critical evidence about her husband’s alleged financial dishonesty unopened and that he missed a deadline in October 2023 to keep the “financial discovery” period open. 
Since they missed the deadline, Adi claims she has no way of being able to fight for her share of the money he allegedly tried to hide. 
“[Stock and KLG] engaged in self-dealing by billing [Adi] hundreds of thousands of dollars within five months, without producing any discernible results, or beneficial results for [her],” the complaint reads.

  By Connor Byrne |

Reliever Emilio Pagán has declined his $10MM player option with the Reds for 2027, Francys Romero of BeisbolFR reports. Unless he signs a new deal with the Reds by 4 p.m. CT on Nov. 2, the right-hander will head to the open market.
The 35-year-old Pagán, who has pitched for six teams since he debuted with the Mariners in 2017, spent the past three seasons in Cincinnati. He originally joined the club on a two-year, $16MM guarantee in December 2023, but the first season of the contract didn’t go particularly well. Pagán had separate stints on the injured list with triceps and lat issues, and he only delivered 38 innings of 4.50 ERA ball when healthy.
The past two years went far better for Pagán, who took over as the Reds’ closer in 2025. That year, he converted 32 of 38 save opportunities and recorded a stingy 2.88 ERA with quality strikeout and walk rates of 30.0% and 8.1%, respectively, over 68 2/3 frames. He was able to keep runs off the board despite inducing ground balls just 28.7% of the time. Pagán has walked that tightrope throughout his career, having logged a 29.3% ground-ball rate over 549 1/3 innings in the majors.
Pagán’s results in 2026 were similar, though he got off to a slow start and then went on the IL with a hamstring strain on May 6. He spent almost two months on the shelf and returned on July 1 looking to improve on a 6.43 ERA. Not only did Pagán do that, but he excelled over the last three months of the season. He finished with 25 saves in 28 chances, including 19 in a row after he came back from the IL, and a 3.38 ERA across 42 2/3 frames. The second-lowest walk rate of his career (6.8%) helped offset a lack of ground balls (25.7%) and a significant drop in strikeouts. His K rate fell to 25.9%, 2.2 points lower than his lifetime mark, and he benefited from a .243 batting average on balls in play and an 83.8% strand rate. Pagán has only given up a .252 BABIP in his career, but he has typically left runners on base at a 77% clip.
There are red flags with Pagán, who has put up an ERA well over 4.00 in five seasons, but he will nonetheless leave good money on the table with the Reds and seek a third straight multiyear pact. The deal he is opting out of was worth $20MM over two years. Even if Pagán doesn’t do that well this time around, he should approach or exceed the $10MM player option that he turned down. With that in mind, there doesn’t seem to be much downside in rejecting the option.

55,2026-10-31,"Siddiq Ander has accused RSF leader Hemedti of running the paramilitary group like a family business and suppressing dissent.
Save
Share
Sudan RSF commander defects from Hemedti’s forces
Senior commander Siddiq Ander says he has defected from Sudan‘s paramilitary Rapid Support Forces (RSF), according to exclusive video footage obtained by Al Jazeera.
The footage, published on Friday, shows Ander accusing relatives of RSF leader Mohamed Hamdan Dagalo, known as “Hemedti”, of tightly controlling the group’s finances, weapons, troop movements and military promotions.
Ander’s exit follows a string of high-profile defections this year, with some commanders leaving alongside hundreds of fighters.
“It has become clear to us beyond any doubt that we were serving a purely family-based project controlled by Hemedti’s relatives in every aspect, money, weapons, fighters’ movements and the granting of ranks,” Ander said.
“Anyone who speaks out and tells the whole truth faces expulsion, imprisonment or a hidden death.”
Hemedti’s brother, Abdul Rahim Hamdan Dagalo, serves as the RSF deputy commander.
A wave of defections has seen several senior RSF commanders leave the group this year, including Lieutenant Colonel Mohammed Azraq al-Fanjari, who announced on October 8 that he was leaving, along with 300 officers and soldiers and 30 military vehicles.
Al-Fanjari also urged other fighters to abandon the RSF, expressing regret over the suffering caused by the war.
His announcement came after the September 25 defection of Al-Tayeb al-Wahidu, who joined the enemy Sudanese Armed Forces (SAF) with 50 fighters in Omdurman, across the Nile from the capital, Khartoum, according to The Sudan Tribune.
Al-Wahidu cited abuses within the RSF as his reason for leaving.
Earlier, in May, Brigadier Ali Rizqallah, known as Savannah, announced his departure.
Yet another senior commander, Major General al-Nour Ahmed Adam, known as al-Qubba, joined the SAF in April and was publicly welcomed by Sudanese leader and military chief Abdel Fattah al-Burhan.
Al-Qubba’s departure followed disagreements over military appointments within the RSF leadership, the Tribune reported.
Sudan’s war erupted in April 2023 after a power struggle between the army and the RSF, including disagreements over plans to integrate the paramilitary group into the regular military.
The conflict has since displaced about 13 million people and pushed parts of the country into famine.
The latest defections come as both sides increasingly use drones in attacks that have caused mounting civilian casualties.
 

 
On October 26, strikes hit a market and residential area in Nyala, an RSF-controlled city in South Darfur.
Emergency Lawyers, a Sudanese rights group, reported more than 100 deaths, although other medical organisations recorded lower figures.
The United Nations says drone strikes have become the leading cause of civilian deaths in Sudan’s war.

56,2026-10-31,"
  By Mark Polishuk and AJ Eustace |
  at October 31, 2026 8:27am CDT  
Athletics right-hander Luis Severino will exercise his $22MM player option and forgo a return to the open market, as first reported by Mike Rodriguez of Rompiendo Sports. Severino will remain with the A’s in their final season in Sacramento before becoming a free agent in the 2027-28 offseason.
Although Severino previously expressed dissatisfaction with the facilities at Sutter Health Park, it’s no surprise that the veteran will stick around for the third and final year of his contract. Severino posted a 4.16 ERA over 12 starts and 62 2/3 innings before going on the 15-day injured list at the end of May with a strain of his shoulder capsule and subscapularis muscle. The injury proved to be season-ending, as Severino was able to resume throwing but didn’t get deep enough into his recovery process to even begin a minor league rehab assignment.
Between this shortened season and his so-so performance in 2025, Severino wouldn’t have made anywhere close to $22MM in free agency. Opting out of his deal also would’ve meant hitting the open market in offseason that is bound to be interrupted by a lockout, so it makes plenty of sense for Severino to take his guaranteed payday and focus entirely on returning to the mound in 2027 (Severino’s age-33 season).
Severino’s last stint in free agency saw him join the Athletics on a three-year, $67MM guarantee in December 2024 — the largest free agent contract in A’s franchise history. The first two years of the deal have seen Severino deliver a 4.43 ERA in 41 starts and 225 1/3 innings. He’s no longer a strikeout artist like he was at his peak with the Yankees, as Severino has struck out a below-average 19.4% of opponents with the A’s (24% in this season’s limited action). The righty’s 8.3% walk rate and 42% ground ball rate in 2025-26 are slightly better than the league average.
As one might expect, Severino’s metrics have been a lot better on the road than in Sacramento. In 20 home starts as an Athletic, he has a 5.85 ERA and has allowed 14.2% of fly balls to leave the yard. In 21 away starts, Severino has a 3.14 ERA and 6.0% HR/FB rate. He’s also held opponents to a .640 OPS on the road compared to an .823 OPS at Sutter Health Park.
With these splits in mind, it’s not out of the question that Severino could be traded this winter. The A’s explored dealing Severino at the 2025 trade deadline but were reportedly not eager to eat much of the remaining money on the right-hander’s contract. While $22MM isn’t a small sum, perhaps the A’s are more open to covering a portion of this remaining salary to help facilitate a deal. Alternatively, the A’s could deal Severino for another player on an unwanted contract.
Given how much the A’s need rotation help and how difficult it is to convince other pitchers to come to Sacramento, however, the likelier scenario is that Severino just returns as the veteran anchor of a young rotation. The A’s declined their club option on Jeffrey Springs yesterday, leaving Severino, J.T. Ginn, Jacob Lopez, and Gage Jump as the projected top four starters. Jack Perkins, Luis Morales, Luis Medina, and a host of other hurlers will be competing for the fifth spot, unless the A’s bring in another more experienced arm during the offseason.

A couple of weeks ago, an acquaintance told Peter that the stock market had reached ‘oversold’ levels and was unlikely to fall much further. He also advised Peter against panic-selling his holdings.
Curious to know more, Peter decided to discuss this with his friend Paul, a financial market analyst. When they met, Peter exchanged pleasantries before getting straight to the point.
Peter: Hey, is the stock market going to recover?
Paul: Someone appears to be worried about their portfolio.
Peter: Well, someone told me that the market is in some ‘oversold’ territory and so a recovery is bound to happen.
Paul: Well, that’s not a guarantee.
Peter: Why is that? What exactly does ‘oversold’ mean?
Paul: See, we call a market oversold when it drops too fast and too far due to heaving selling by market participants. It can be driven by fear or panic.
Peter: Is there is a way to track this?
Paul: Yes, it can be assessed using technical indicators, predominantly what we call oscillators. 
Peter: What are oscillators?
Paul: There are many indicators used in technical analysis. Each trader/analyst can have their own set of indicators and tools to assess the market. Oscillators are basically indicators that fluctuate around a reference level or within a defined range.
Peter: Can you give me an example?
Paul: Of course. Two commonly-used oscillators are the Relative Strength Index (RSI) and Moving Average Convergence Divergence (MACD).
Peter: Oh! I’ve heard these names. Can you explain a bit more?
Paul: Sure. Take RSI for example. It is based on Relative Strength (RS), which is calculated by dividing average gain by average loss.
Peter: What are those averages?
Paul: Average gain and average loss measure the magnitude of positive and negative price changes over a chosen period. The standard RSI uses 14 periods, usually 14 trading days on a daily chart. It also uses a smoothing method in its calculation.
Peter: Okay. How does RSI indicate whether a market is oversold?
Paul: RSI moves between 0 and 100. A reading below 30 is generally considered oversold. Similarly, a reading above 70 indicates overbought conditions.
Peter: So, if RSI falls below 30, does that mean the market will recover?
Paul: Not necessarily. An oversold market can remain oversold for an extended period. Prices can fall further, particularly during a strong downtrend.
Peter: Then what’s the point of tracking oversold conditions?
Paul: They alert us to the possibility of a recovery. But we should look for other signs, such as a reversal in price action, before drawing conclusions.
Peter: So, oversold doesn’t necessarily mean it’s time to buy?
Paul: Exactly! Oversold is not a guaranteed buy signal. It only suggests that selling pressure has been strong, and the downtrend might lose momentum.
Peter: Can you give an example?
Paul: Of course. Look at Nifty 50. RSI entered oversold territory in mid-September. But then, after a minor recovery, it fell further. RSI, too, inched up and slipped back into oversold region in early October. 
Peter: But it has recovered since then.
Paul: Yes, it has. But this time, it provided us with an additional indication for a possible recovery by forming a morning star candlestick pattern on the weekly chart.
Peter: Got it. So, I shouldn’t assume that a recovery is imminent just because the market is oversold. This can be used in conjunction with other factors to improve the odds of success. 
Paul: Absolutely. That’s why we shouldn’t rely on a single indicator to make investment decisions.

59,2026-10-31,"Coverage tells you the percentage of spoken words you know, not how many lines you can follow. Measured in Frieren and seven other anime.
Published 30 October 2026 by learnfromx
Say you want to watch Frieren, so you learn the 500 most common words in the show. A coverage calculator says that you now know 85.9% of the words spoken.
You make tea and press play. Here are three of the things you hear:
Blurred words fall outside the top 500. Hover or tap to reveal them.
By the third pause to look up a word, your tea has gone cold. With those 500 words, you know every word in 1,151 of the first 3,000 lines. That's 38.4% of them. The other 1,849 lines each contain at least one word you don't know.
Coverage is the number most learning sites quote. It's the percentage of spoken words that you know, so a particle spoken a thousand times counts a thousand times. The words at the very top of the Frieren list are all grammar pieces like の (of, 's) and は (topic marker), so the top 100 words alone give you 68.3% coverage. You'd follow only 8.5% of the lines.
Three figures describe the same 500 words:
manga-kotoba builds its coverage tables this way. jpdb and jiten show a coverage figure too, though their help pages don't give the exact formula.
The gap between coverage and lines comes from line length. A Frieren line has 7 words on average. You need to know all seven to follow it. At 85.9% coverage, your chance of knowing a whole line is about 35%. That's 0.859 multiplied by itself seven times. It's close to the 38.4% of lines we measured.
Thick line: lines understood in the chosen show. Dashed: coverage in that show. Pale lines: lines understood in the other seven. For each curve, we assumed you learn that show's most common words first.
All eight shows have this gap. Serial Experiments Lain reaches 95% coverage with the fewest words, 1,191. Even then, you'd follow only 77.5% of the lines.
Serial Experiments Lain needs the fewest words of the eight: about 400 gets you half the lines. Jujutsu Kaisen needs 980.
We capped every show at 3,000 lines so that a long show doesn't look harder for having more episodes. If a show gets harder later on, it looks easier here than it is.
Back to Frieren. You missed 1,849 lines. Of those, 1,042 are missing a single word, which is 56.4%. Across all the shows, that figure runs from 50.2% to 63.9%.
Going from 500 to 1,000 words helps you far more than coverage suggests. Coverage only gains 6.5 points, because these new words don't come up often. Lines understood gain 22.1 points, because one new word is often the last one you needed to follow a line.
You can't fill these one-word gaps with a handful of words. Those 1,042 lines are missing 804 different words between them. The most you get from one word is 4 lines, from words like 後悔 (regret), 約束 (promise) and 収集 (collecting).
As a rough comparison, we tried the 2,000 most common words of general Japanese, from the wordfreq list. Those got you 23.4% to 37.6% of lines. We then tried each show's own top 2,000 words. They got 74.3% to 93.3% of lines. Serial Experiments Lain has fewer than 2,000 different words, so it isn't in that second range.
A show's top words can be unusual. Number 304 on the Frieren list is 生臭坊主, a monk who doesn't keep his vows, which is what the characters keep calling Heiter. Number 21 is 魔法 (magic), four places ahead of 私 (I).
Say you learn a show's top words from one season, then carry on to the next. We tested something close to this on the four shows with enough lines. We built each list from the first 3,000 lines, then tested it on the next 3,000. On Frieren's next 3,000 lines, the old list gets 22.3%, while a list built from those lines themselves gets 33.0%. The word 試験 (exam) appears only once in those first lines and 51 times after them, because the characters take a mage exam later on. Across those shows, the drop is 5.5 to 14.0 points.
Many words have several meanings, which linguists call polysemy. We counted a word as known whichever meaning a line uses. At 500 words, both of these Frieren lines count as understood: 手が震えています (my hands are shaking) and 手に取るように分かる (I can read it like a book). You could know 手 (hand) perfectly from the first line and still be lost in the second. Word for word, it says ""as if taking it in your hand."" We didn't measure how many lines have this problem. On this count alone, the lines-understood figures are too high.
Run on our Frieren lines, this prints 85.9% and 38.4%. We don't store the unprocessed subtitle files. Your own subtitles and filler list will give you slightly different numbers.
Each cell shows coverage, then lines understood, at that many known words.
Known words needed to reach a given percentage of lines understood:
learnfromx is a flashcard app that teaches Japanese from anime. Each card uses a line where you know everything except the one new word, which is why we started counting lines. The figures above count each word once, however many meanings it has. That wouldn't work in the app: knowing あれ as ""that"" doesn't help when someone says あれ？ (huh?). These are two separate cards. It doesn't catch every such word yet, we're working on it. During early access it offers one show, Hunter x Hunter.
一歩から
Show names, characters, and trademarks belong to their respective owners. LearnFromX is not affiliated with, endorsed by, or sponsored by any of them."
