BERITA BAHASA INDONESIA
TOK PISIN
Topic:Crime
Convicted murderer Darryl Young has been accused of terrorising his community and stealing livestock. (ABC News: Sharon Gordon)
A triple murderer who gunned down a Queensland cattle grazing family had terrorised the community for decades, stealing livestock and threatening a police officer, according to his former neighbours and an ex-detective.
Darryl Young is serving three life sentences with a non-parole period of 40 years, a sentence believed to be the longest ever handed down in Queensland, for killing his neighbours Mervyn and Maree Schwarz, and her son Graham Tighe, after he demanded the family meet him at the gate of his Bogie property in 2022.
Young is appealing against his conviction and sentence.
Maree and Mervyn Schwarz (left) and Graham Tighe were found dead at the gate of the Bogie cattle station in 2022. (ABC News/Supplied)
The 63-year-old had wrongly accused the family of cattle theft before fatally shooting three of them and shooting Ross Tighe, the sole survivor.
But it was Young who was convicted of cattle theft in 2006 after he pleaded guilty to stealing 147 head of cattle with his mother, Janelle.
Fred Swift, a former detective in Queensland's stock squad, has never spoken publicly about his dealings with Young before now.
Mr Swift described meeting Young in 1994 and revealed he was the main suspect for cattle theft in the Bowen area in North Queensland.
""Darryl was one of the worst people I ever dealt with … I did call him the spawn of the devil, I said it to his face,"" Mr Swift told the ABC.
Fred Swift says Darryl Young was the main suspect for cattle theft in Bowen.  (Supplied)
Mr Swift said Young threatened him on multiple occasions.
""He was always very aggressive. He just told me, 'Watch what you're doing,'"" Mr Swift said.
Young was vindictive and would react when people reported him for cattle stealing, according to Mr Swift.
""It wasn't always him stealing cattle, but it often was.""
Mr Swift said after one particular complaint, Young pushed a man's cattle yards into a heap with a bulldozer and sat the machine on top of the wreckage as a warning sign.
Another time, Young drained a tank of molasses down the creek at the same man's property, Mr Swift said.
Also speaking publicly for the first time, Steve Norman, a former neighbour of the Young family's property in Bowen, said he was among the cattle owners whose stock was stolen by Young and his mother. 
Mr Norman revealed he had been trying to get a conviction since 1986.
""You wouldn't believe what the property next to me was called when his mother owned it. Sneaky Hills. It's a great name for cattle duffers, isn't it?"" Mr Norman told the ABC.
Mr Norman alleges Young cut his fences and threatened his daughters after he made reports to the stock squad.
""Every time we'd get the stock squad onto them, I'd have windmills broken, I'd have pumps pinched.""
Convicted murderer and cattle thief, Darryl Young, lived in prime cattle-breeding country in Bogie.  (ABC Tropical North: Danielle Jesser)
Another time, Mr Norman said a jet pump was cut out of a bore and the 6-metre hole filled with rocks, so it was unusable.
The stock squad had received multiple reports about Darryl Young over many years, but it did not have enough concrete evidence to charge him, Mr Swift said.
But one day in 2003, Mr Swift was wandering around the Gracemere cattle yards in Central Queensland, about a six and a half hour drive from Bowen, when he saw dodgy-looking brands on cattle and Darryl Young's name.
""That's when it all came tumbling down,"" the ex-detective said. 
""[He and his mother] were changing brands, registering brands very close to their neighbours' brands and putting their brand over the old brand.""
Fred Swift says Darryl Young and his mother were changing brands on cattle and registering similar ones to their neighbours'. (Supplied)
Later, Mr Swift was searching for a woman's missing cattle when he pulled into a paddock in Bowen.
""I was eating my ham and salad sandwich on wholemeal bread, and it suddenly dawned on me I was looking at stolen cattle,"" he said.
""They had been agisted to the farm that was there by Darryl Young. I don't think anyone else knew that they were stolen.
Young was sentenced to two years' imprisonment, to be released after serving six months, for the cattle theft conviction in 2006.
His mother Janelle Young's two-year sentence was wholly suspended.
During the 2006 case, Judge Bob Pack said the scheme was ""somewhat sophisticated"" because the Gracemere cattle yard was ""well away from the Bowen district"", meaning there were fewer chances of the cattle being identified by their real owners. 
""An under-resourced stock squad, which has been the position for many years, of course to some extent facilitates offending of this nature,"" Judge Pack said at the time.
The entrance to Shannonvale Station where Darryl Young lived. (ABC Tropical North: Danielle Jesser)
Mr Norman said things quietened down after Young's conviction, until the morning of the Bogie shooting in 2022. 
""The police rang me up wondering where I was. I said, 'I'm in Charters Towers' and they said, 'Thank Christ for that, because Darryl Young is up here shooting people,'"" Mr Norman said.
""[The police officer] was worried [about me].""
Young maintained his innocence throughout the trial this year, claiming he was never at the scene of the shooting.
Another of Young's former neighbours, Robert Kohler, told the ABC he never would have met him unarmed, for his own safety.
""I was fully aware of what Young was capable of,"" he said.
Mr Kohler lived at Sutherland Station in Bogie, a rural town about 55 kilometres from Bowen in Queensland's Whitsunday Region, from 2011 to 2018.
Robert Kohler (right), pictured with son Zac, says Darryl Young's reputation was as long as Queensland's Bruce Highway. (Supplied)
The Schwarz and Tighe family later bought the property and moved in, about a year before the 2022 tragedy.
Mr Kohler said he had near-daily issues with Young, whose home at Shannonvale Station bordered the property.
""When he moved here [in 2012], I had a cup of tea with him, and I told him he had a reputation as long as the Bruce Highway, but I'd treat him like he treated me,"" Mr Kohler said.
Mr Kohler said a few years later he called Queensland police's stock squad after Young was seen mustering with dogs on his property, and he later found horse and dog tracks.
But he said the stock squad did not take it seriously.
""The stock squad said, 'Oh, go up and have a look and see if you put any cattle through the gate,'"" he said. 
""I said, 'Isn't that your job?' and they said they were a bit busy.
""The whole system's broken mate, people take it for a joke.""
Mr Kohler said he also rang the stock squad after Young was seen driving with a tarp over his truck at 2am, which he suspected had stolen cattle inside.
""They could've gone down to his yard the next day and checked to see what he had in the yard, and DNA tested [the cattle],"" he said.
""He was just stealing blindly, and the police didn't want nothing to do with it at all.""
The Bogie murder scene was a property gate at Shannonvale Station where Darryl Young lived. (Supplied: Supreme Court of Queensland)
Mr Kohler wants stock theft to be taken more seriously.
""[When Darryl was jailed for stock theft], he was out in six months,"" he said. 
""All that led to where we are: three people losing their lives and one getting wounded.""
Katter's Australian Party leader Robbie Katter has called for better resourcing of the unit that deals with cattle theft, now known as the Rural and Stock Crime Squad.
Robbie Katter says the Bogie shooting should be a wake-up call to the government. (Supplied: Katter's Australian Party)
Mr Katter said investigations needed proper support with mustering costs, DNA testing, and specialist police who knew the bush.
""At Bogie … I think that's the wake-up call the government needed to demonstrate that this needs to be adequately resourced,"" Mr Katter told the ABC.
""It would certainly be good value for the government to nip these things in the bud before they blow up or signal to others that it's easy to get away with.""
Mr Swift joined the stock squad in 1984 and the Young investigation was his last job in the department. He was offered a promotion out of the unit, which he declined. 
""I saw people struggling and working hard. All their efforts to no avail,"" he said. 
""I always say, if you want to make money, you go into politics, start a religion, or steal cattle."" 
A Queensland Police Service (QPS) spokesperson did not comment on allegations that Young stole cattle or on how the stock squad handled the reports.
The spokesperson said Rural and Stock Crime Squad detectives had specialist expertise in rural and stock crime investigations, including major and organised crimes, drug offences, firearms theft and trafficking.
Investigations could be complex and protracted, often spanning large geographical areas, the QPS spokesperson said.
""The Rural and Stock Crime Squad encourages rural communities to remain vigilant by reporting suspicious activity or stock theft, securing firearms appropriately, and considering crime prevention measures such as security cameras, signage and secure property access.""
The ABC has contacted Young's lawyer for comment.

Australian Consumer Law (ACL): If you purchase a grey market Apple product from an Australian-based grey market retailer (like Kogan or online parallel importers), you still retain statutory consumer guarantees under the Australian Competition and Consumer Commission (ACCC), which means the seller must offer remedies (repair, replacement, or refund) for major failures. However, local Apple Stores in Australia may refuse direct warranty claims if the device is an unsupported regional import.

 -4,121.00
 Anthropic, the frontier AI lab, filed its most-anticipated IPO prospectus last week. It aims to go public in mid-November with the ticker ‘ANT’ on Nasdaq. While the issue size is not made public yet, the AI lab is reportedly seeking a valuation of $1.5-2 trillion or 75-100x trailing revenue.
AI, in its current form is a novel technology to the world — like the internal combustion engines, computers and the internet of the past — and the technology is evolving day by day. At this point in time, a layperson’s estimate of the extent of disruption that AI could cause is probably as good as a scholar’s. Therefore, it is really challenging to define an addressable market for Anthropic and the market share it could command in the future. However, the risk factors detailed here introduce enough uncertainty to cloud the investment case for the time being. Also, with interest rates elevated, the valuation leaves no margin for error.
Nevertheless, here’s a summary of Anthropic’s business, things going for it and those that are not.
Anthropic, founded in 2021, is an AI research lab that develops frontier AI models and lets customers use them for a consideration. Its products include the Claude family of large language models (LLMs) — Mythos, Fable, Opus, Sonnet and Haiku, and agentic AI platforms Claude Code for software developers and the more generic Claude Cowork meant for automating workflows. Anthropic’s customers range from individual users to large corporations.
Anthropic makes revenue in two ways depending on the customer’s usage pattern—consumption-based and subscription-based. Under the former model, the customer is billed according to their consumption of tokens. For example, assume a software developer builds a translation app that uses Claude. Every time someone uses the app, tokens are consumed for translation. Anthropic will then bill the software developer based on the number of tokens the app has consumed. Agentic use-cases, including Claude Code for coding, are also billed based on the number of tokens consumed. The latter revenue model is rather simple and more familiar. Users pay subscriptions to use Claude models for an agreed period of time. Table 1 contains the split between the two models, which is roughly 80:20.
Besides, Anthropic also works directly with companies requiring guidance on automating complex workflows. Anthropic engineers working with a pharma company on drug discovery, can be an example. Their recent offering is called ‘Ode with Anthropic’, which is an AI services company aimed at taking Claude’s capabilities to mid-sized companies and providing them support.
That said, what are Anthropic’s costs? Refer Table 1 for this part.
The first is compute and infrastructure cost — the most important cost-head. Note that Anthropic’s models are hosted on hyperscalers or large data centres operated by companies such as Google, Amazon and Microsoft. These data centres are equipped with powerful chips, including the likes of Nvidia’s GPUs (graphic processing units) that Anthropic uses — both for model development (training) and inference. Inference is the process by which an already developed/ trained AI model works on the user’s input to generate an output. Anthropic leases server/ compute capacity from these cloud companies, whom Anthropic calls as CSPs or cloud service providers, over long-periods of time. The annual lease expense is a direct cost and is captured under this cost-head. This was 55 per cent of revenue in H1 2026.
The second cost item is personnel expenses, which is self-explanatory. These accounted for 19 per cent of H1 2026 revenue. Interestingly, Anthropic appears to compensate their employees more using share-based payments (like stock options). Two-thirds of personnel cost in H1 2026 were share-based payments. Share-based payments do not entail cash outflow as in the case of salaries, but the trade-off lies in a future dilution for shareholders.
Next up is sales, marketing and partnerships. The sales, marketing part should be self-explanatory. We’ll talk more about partnerships. Anthropic refers to the CSPs as partners here. Here’s how it works. With the advent of cloud, enterprise software is now hosted on cloud platforms such as Amazon AWS, Microsoft Azure and Google Cloud. Anthropic uses them as distribution to reach the thousands of companies whose workflows take place on these platforms. In doing so, it promises to pay CSPs a sum, which is included in this cost-head. Overall along with marketing, these expenses were 19 per cent of H1 2026 revenue.
Finally, other operating and administrative costs. These work out to 11 per cent of H1 2026 revenue. However, this item generally may not be this high. It spikes in quarters where there are expenses linked to the company’s ‘donation matching program’. Under this program, if eligible employees pledge to donate to eligible non-profit organisations, the company will match that donation, by issuing shares (non-voting Class N common stock, to be precise). We’ll talk more on this in a moment.
Deducting the above costs from revenue gives operating income. Adjusting for the non-cash share-based payments and donation matching expense gives adjusted operating income of $1.6 billion, which is 10 per cent of H1 2026 revenue.
Between operating income and net income are interest income and taxes. However, there is also another item. Anthropic has issued convertible notes (or convertible bonds) and redeemable convertible preferred stock (RCPS) over various funding rounds. These are essentially equity waiting to be converted. Anthropic carries them at fair value and so, changes in their fair value must be recognised in P&L. If the value of these instruments increases between quarters, there will be a marked-to-market expense and vice-versa. This will continue until they are eventually converted to equity. Of Anthropic’s $81 billion of reported loss in H1 2026, almost all of that $81 billion had to do with fair value changes and thus are considered as non-cash adjustments to arrive at adjusted net income. Adjusted net income in H1 2026 works out to $2.7 billion which is roughly the sum of adjusted operating income of $1.6 billion and interest income of $1.1 billion.
Anthropic is constituted as a PBC or public benefit corporation. Though it may seem contradictory, this enables the company to balance three things at the same time: interest of the shareholders, interest of the stakeholders (who are affected by the company’s conduct; includes customers and the broader society because of AI’s pervasive nature) and its public benefit purpose stated in its charter — ‘the responsible development and maintenance of advanced AI for the long-term benefit of humanity’. The AI lab has a wholly-owned subsidiary called ‘Anthropic Public Benefit Fund LLC’ (fund) to carry out its public benefit initiatives.
Why is it contradictory? Take Meta Platforms’ case. Had it been a PBC, it may not have set up algorithms to keep engagement elevated among kids, keeping the interest of its users in mind. However, this may not be in the best interest of shareholders, as it would mean lower ad revenue. Back to Anthropic. Because AI is a generational technology and is too powerful to be taken lightly, perhaps it calls for such a structure. Its success and whether Anthropic is using it only as a façade to build goodwill or whether it is serious about it, remains to be seen.
Here are a few facts. Anthropic has already contributed 5 per cent of fully-diluted shares, as of June 30, 2026, to the fund. The ‘donation matching program’ also is meant for this purpose. The seven founders have separately pledged 80 per cent of their personal equity stake. Further, the company plans to issue about 2 per cent of fully-diluted shares every time the stock price grows  50 per cent, after going public.
While it is a fact that Anthropic is yet to become profitable, it cannot be denied that it has shown incredible growth in revenue — the kind tech start-ups can only dream of. Founded only in January 2021, its revenue has reached $19.7 billion on a trailing 12 months (TTM) basis. This is quite impressive, considering the time to develop the models from scratch and go-to-market. Alphabet reached $20 billion in revenue 10 years after inception, Microsoft in 24 years and Meta Platforms in 13 years.
The flywheel behind Anthropic’s growth is simple: Investment in more, better compute > Model improvement > Efficiencies for customers > Revenue growth. The number of large customers from whom Anthropic generated $100,000 or more in TTM revenue has grown from about 20, as of December 2023, 200 (in December 2024), 1,500 (in December 2025) to 11,900, as of September 2026. Customers from whom Anthropic generated $1 million and $10 million in TTM revenue were about 1,900 and 250, as of September 2026. Growth has not only come from customer additions but also in business with existing customers. This is captured in the net revenue retention of around 900 per cent, as of September 2026. This means that if Anthropic generated $1 of revenue from a customer in July-September 2025, it generated $9 from the same customer in July-September 2026.
Such rapid growth has resulted in operating leverage, with the AI lab turning profitable both at the operating level and net level, in Q2 2026, on an adjusted basis (see Chart 1). Revenue to compute and infrastructure cost ratio is on the rise — from $0.4 of revenue per $1 of compute cost in Q1 2025 to $2.1 of revenue per $1 spent on compute in Q2 2026. Operating cash flows, too, have turned positive in H1 2026.
The balance sheet is also flush with cash of about $131 billion, from recent funding rounds. For perspective, the entire balance-sheet size is only $147 billion.
If you are impressed with the company so far, hang on. The factors that investors need to be concerned about, outnumber the positives. We’ll deal with them one by one. But before proceeding, do bear in mind the company’s asking valuation of $1.5-2 trillion (per media reports), and evaluate the factors discussed.
Growth rate tapering?
First, the financials disclosed in the prospectus stop with H1 2026. This, while the company was comfortable with sharing data as of September 2026 for a select items—some of which have been discussed above. This adds fuel to the sceptic voices in the market that suggest this to be the company’s deliberate choice. Stopping financials as of H1 2026 may, in fact, be advantageous to Anthropic — because Q2 2026 was the quarter that broadly saw the end of token-maxxing. Token-maxxing was a trend among corporates that incentivised employees based on the number of tokens consumed rather than on the effectiveness of the outcomes. The trend even saw companies such as Ford fall back to hiring employees, after AI investments failed to live up to expectations. Per media reports, sequential revenue growth in Q3 2026 appears to have declined from 147 per cent in Q2 2026 to 45 per cent (see Chart 1). In fact, this could be the reason why the rumoured asking price may have come down to $1.5 trillion, which was maintained at $2 trillion sometime ago.
A 45-per cent sequential growth is still good, but perhaps in isolation. When building on a low base (TTM revenue of $19.7 billion), as compared to the prospectus indicated addressable market of $22.5 trillion by 2031 and given an asking multiple of up to 100x trailing revenue, 45 per cent growth does not make the cut. Also, makes one question, ‘Is AI adoption slowing?’
A problem only growth can solve
A slow growth rate comes across as a bigger problem considering this. Look at Table 2. Forecasting a demand surge for its products, Anthropic has struck massive long-term deals (some extending to 2036) with CSPs for leasing compute, that work out to a total of $827 billion. The leases are yet to commence, and so, the liabilities in relation to them are yet to show up on the balance sheet. While payments for some of them are based on actual consumption of compute, at least 62 per cent or $512 billion is on non-cancellable basis. Meaning, the lease obligations in these deals must be honoured, irrespective of whether Anthropic manages to garner demand for its products — a problem only sustained growth can solve. In this context, a weakening growth rate does not help Anthropic’s case.
The prospectus also suggests that Anthropic may pivot from leasing to building its own compute infrastructure. Even in that case, the problem does not go away, as the lease payments morph into upfront capital commitments. How Anthropic might source the cash for this is in the dark.
Can decline in training cost help?
Unlikely. Anthropic’s compute and infrastructure cost includes both cost of model development and inference. Model development costs are not capitalised. Given the AI lab operates in the frontier of AI technology and is a strong believer in scaling laws, it is unlikely to stop training new, more powerful and more autonomous models. Scaling laws suggest that when a model’s size, training data and compute are scaled up, there is a predictable rise in the model’s performance. Training cost can thus be reasonably expected to continue.
This is in contrast to the business models of traditional software and internet companies, where the spend on developing intellectual property is largely upfront, leaving the company to reap the benefits of operating leverage later.
Compute up, tokens down
Anthropic’s token prices are now just one-fourth of what they were in January 2025 (see Chart 3). This is as per Ramp’s AI index, which tracks the AI spending of its clients. Ramp is a fintech company that sells corporate expense management software. Token prices have now fallen to $0.62 per million tokens versus $2.56 per million tokens in early January 2025.
This raises a question: If Anthropic’s moat is real and demand for its models is so strong, why should token prices fall so drastically? Token prices of OpenAI are also on the decline. With a clutch of cheaper Chinese open-weight models having entered the fray, it raises concerns whether AI models are getting commoditised. Customers, too, are getting smarter and using tools that choose the model according to the complexity of the user’s prompt. Basic prompts need not be sent to the most sophisticated models, thus saving tokens.
On the other hand, data from BCA Research indicate that compute costs are rising (Chart 2). Constraints in the supply of power to data centres under construction make it difficult to assume a drop in compute costs in the near term.
Concentration risk
While no customer represented more than 10 per cent of 9M 2026 revenue, top five customers account for about 25 per cent of 9M 2026 revenue. The company is also becoming increasingly dependent on the distribution of CSPs. Share of revenue from CSP marketplaces is up from 11 per cent in 2023, 32 per cent in 2024, 47 per cent in 2025 to 48 per cent in H1 2026. This is a risk in the sense that the same CSPs also distribute their own AI offerings, such as Gemini and Copilot, as well as rival models like ChatGPT, whose developers could pay more for promotion than Anthropic.
Circular financing, dilution
Over the years, Anthropic has frequently raised funds using convertible instruments such as convertible notes and RCPS. These are often circular financing deals where CSPs such as Google and Amazon subscribe to these instruments, with the apparent intent that some of the money will flow back to them when Anthropic pays for compute. They also have the optionality of capital appreciation when they are converted to equity or when Anthropic undergoes a successful listing. For instance, look at the Broadcom deal in Table 2. Out of the $161 billion in total lease obligations, Broadcom allows Anthropic to settle up to $42 billion by issuing convertible notes (notes yet to be issued).
While these arrangements may be convenient, they might result in significant dilution for equity holders. These instruments are carried at their fair values (their values had they been converted to equity) as said above, and together they add up to $266 billion, as of June 30, 2026. For context, balance-sheet total on this date was $147 billion, after considering $133 billion of accumulated losses.
The dilution does not stop there. Employees are largely paid in stock options and RSUs. About 64 per cent of personnel cost over 2023-25 and in H1 2026 was met using share-based compensations. Equity holders also need to factor in the dilution from issue of shares in relation with the ‘donation matching program’ and other contributions to the public benefit fund.
Legal, regulatory hurdles
Given AI’s powerful capabilities and the potential misuse through deepfakes, cybercrime and hacking, investors should expect greater regulatory scrutiny of the AI ecosystem, which could constrain growth or may even result in liability for companies like Anthropic, if something goes wrong.
Further, while Anthropic apparently harbours noble intentions with its public benefit initiatives, it faces allegations of copyright infringement in multiple lawsuits. There is a high likelihood that Anthropic may not be able to defend itself in these lawsuits, forcing it to settle off court. In August 2024, a group of authors sued Anthropic, alleging that it had infringed their copyrights by using their literary works to train LLMs. The company later agreed to a $1.5-billion settlement (known as Bartz settlement).
Today, much of the US equity market has become one AI trade, betting on the AI investment cycle, from chips and cloud infrastructure to software and power companies. Therefore, Anthropic’s IPO is more than just a listing. For AI bulls, it is a referendum on whether the market believes AI labs can evolve into high-quality businesses. For AI bears, it’s about vindication if the bubble breaks. What unfolds in the first few months to a year after Anthropic’s listing could go down as one of the most defining episodes in the history of stock markets.

Michael Carrick’s stay of execution as Manchester United boss may not last long after his team imploded in a 2-0 Premier League loss at Chelsea.
The visitors wasted a string of chances to take the lead at Stamford Bridge on Saturday before Morgan Rogers waltzed through the heart of the United defence to open the scoring on 50 minutes.
Lisandro Martinez then ruined any chance of a revival from Carrick’s men with two quickfire yellow cards, the second of which also conceded a penalty converted by Cole Palmer.
Winless in nine games, their defeat leaves the 20-time English champions still just one point above the relegation zone and in danger of ending the weekend in the bottom three.
Many expected Carrick to be axed following last weekend’s 3-0 home defeat to Bournemouth.
The former United midfielder was instead given the chance to turn his side’s fortunes around and will lament a lack of ruthlessness during the first half.
Carrick recalled captain Bruno Fernandes after he was left out last weekend in the wake of criticising his manager’s tactics during the 4-1 thrashing at Como in the Champions League.
Fernandes should have marked his return with the opener when he blazed over from Bryan Mbeumo’s inviting cross.
The roles were reversed when Mbeumo could not stretch to meet Fernandes’s delivery at the back post before Amad Diallo was twice denied by Chelsea’s stand-in goalkeeper Mike Penders.
United did finally get the ball in the net when Matheus Cunha turned in Harry Maguire’s header from a corner, but the Brazilian had strayed just offside.
Carrick could not hide his frustration in a half-time interview with broadcasters and his mood soured further with a disastrous start to the second period.
Chelsea’s Rogers credits Carrick for revitalising his career during a spell together at Middlesbrough, but dealt a potentially decisive blow to his former boss.
The England international collected the ball inside the centre circle but was allowed to drive directly towards the United goal before dinking the ball over Senne Lammens.
Martinez also spoke out publicly in recent weeks to criticise United’s lack of spending in the transfer market to bolster Carrick’s squad.
But on this season’s evidence, Martinez is one of those most in need of replacing.
The Argentine international could not cope with Rogers’s strength moments later as he was penalised for hauling back the Chelsea forward.
From the resulting free-kick, Martinez then needlessly crashed into Wesley Fofana with a raised forearm.
After a VAR review, referee Peter Banks awarded a penalty and showed Martinez a second yellow card.
Lammens got hands to Palmer’s spot-kick but still allowed the ball to trickle into the bottom corner.
On a rare start since Xabi Alonso took charge of the Blues, Estevao had a third goal disallowed for offside when he crashed a pass from Palmer into the top corner.
Cries of “sacked in the morning” rained down from the Chelsea fans as their side toyed with United for the final quarter without adding to their lead.
Another big decision awaits the United hierarchy before the massive Champions League clash at home to Roma on Tuesday.
Beaten in two of their three European games so far, United face both the embarrassment and financial shock of an early exit should they fall to another defeat against the Italians at Old Trafford.

68,2026-10-31,"Strive CEO Matt Cole says the Binance hack has not changed his long-term case for Bitcoin, but cautions it is too early to call a market bottom.
Bitcoin has held up better than expected following the $6.3 billion Binance hack, according to Strive CEO Matt Cole, who said the breach does not undermine the cryptocurrency’s long-term investment thesis.
Speaking on Cointelegraph’s Chain Reaction on Friday, Cole said the breach had not changed his long-term investment case for Bitcoin. He compared the attack on Binance’s wallets to a bank robbery rather than a failure of Bitcoin’s network.
Bitcoin traded near $77,400 at 4:00 pm on Saturday, about 4% below its price shortly before the attack began Thursday, according to CoinGecko data.
Strive, a publicly traded company that holds Bitcoin on its balance sheet, reported owning 43,000 BTC as of Oct. 23. 
“I do have conviction that over any reasonable time frame, this does not change the investment thesis on Bitcoin,” Cole said.
Cole said Bitcoin’s decline was less severe than he expected given the scale of the breach and broader market weakness, although he acknowledged that it was too early to determine whether the cryptocurrency had reached a price bottom.
Cole described the market as being in an “early spring” recovery phase that could last another six to nine months, with prices gradually rising rather than surging to new highs.
Paul Chapman, financial crime director at Wirex, also distinguished the attack on Binance from a compromise of Bitcoin’s network. “It’s important to be clear that Bitcoin itself hasn’t been hacked — this was an attack on one company’s systems, not the network,” he told Cointelegraph.
Related: Strive closes $630M SATS IPO, leaves Bitcoin allocation unspecified
According to Glassnode, Thursday’s Binance breach was the largest cryptocurrency hack on record, with around $6.3 billion affected, exceeding the combined losses from the next nine largest hacks. The previous record was the $1.5 billion Bybit exploit in February 2025.
Glassnode said three of the 10 largest crypto hacks have occurred since the beginning of 2025, all involving exchanges.
Binance hack exceeds the next nine largest crypto exploits combined. Source: Glassnode
By comparison, following the Bybit hack in 2025, Bitcoin fell roughly 5% from nearly $100,000 to around $95,000 amid the breach and broader market volatility, according to a Bitfinex market report.
Anchorage Digital data cited by CNBC put Bitcoin’s average decline in the first 24 hours after previous crypto exchange exploits at 8.3%.
Blockaid CEO Ido Ben-Natan said the attackers appeared to have compromised Binance’s transaction signing process. Binance said Friday that it had found no evidence of leaked wallet private keys, though its investigation remained ongoing.
Blockaid’s analysis identified an Ethereum transaction containing 50 signed authorizations covering different transaction counts. A matching authorization allowed attacker-controlled code to operate through the wallet. Ben-Natan argued that checks before signing could have blocked the malicious authorization.
Magazine: Tampered Ledger reported in Singapore a month before news broke: Asia Express

69,2026-10-31,"Less-liquid tokens make up the remaining shortfall as Binance continues buying replacement assets through market and over-the-counter trades.
Binance said Saturday it had replenished 90% of the assets stolen in its $6.3 billion hack, up from 80% a day earlier, and remained on track to resume withdrawals Sunday.
The exchange is using a combination of its corporate treasury, a partial deployment of its Secure Asset Fund for Users (SAFU) and a portion of its futures insurance fund, a spokesperson told Cointelegraph.
“Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not users,” the spokesperson said, adding that the insurance fund would remain above required risk management levels.
The update follows Thursday’s theft, the largest cryptocurrency hack on record, which TRM Labs has since attributed to North Korea’s TraderTraitor hacker group. 
Following the hack, Binance said Friday it had set aside $6.34 billion to cover losses, including $5 billion in the affected cryptocurrencies. Industry partners like Tether and Circle froze about $3 billion in affected assets after the hack, but those funds are separate from Binance’s replacement of stolen tokens.
Still, CertiK told Cointelegraph on Friday that it needed more information to verify Binance’s claimed $5 billion in replacement cryptocurrencies. Binance has since published a new reserves snapshot, but says the full cryptographic proof is still being generated. “The full zero-knowledge proof takes approximately 40 to 50 hours to generate due to the computational workload,” the exchange’s update reads.
Binance said in its update on X that customer balances in Bitcoin (BTC), Ether (ETH) and eight other assets were fully backed. The remaining shortfall consists of less liquid tokens that it continues to buy through market and over-the-counter transactions, the update reads.
The spokesperson’s statement did not specify how much each funding source contributed or disclose the insurance fund’s minimum required balance. It also did not quantify how much stolen crypto had been recovered.  
Read Cointelegraph’s full coverage of the Binance hack here.
Magazine: Binance hackers raced to move billions before it could be frozen

70,2026-10-31,"I believe this is my third time writing about one of the best flea markets…Downtown Modernism. Because of that, I won’t bore you with all of the details; aside from that, it’s in Los Angeles, only happens twice a year, is the perfect size, and the vendors primarily sell mid-century-era goods. For me, this is total heaven. Here’s the current problem: I don’t need anything. The main spaces in my home are done, and since my boyfriend went out of town the morning of the market, I couldn’t even attempt to convince him to get things for his home. However, my dear friend Franchesca needed some art, and I clearly needed to be an investigative journalist and show off all of the things that caught my eye for this vintage-loving community (spoiler alert: I didn’t leave empty-handed. I’m sure “no one” saw that coming).
But there is one thing a professional investigative journalist does…they come prepared. I did not come prepared. Despite Franchesca and me being avid flea market lovers, we did not bring any cash. Not the biggest deal, as most markets have ATMs. But there is a $10 entrance fee, and their ATM had not arrived yet. We felt like idiots. Nothing like getting your butt up to get there at 7:45 (which was a little later than planned) to then get to the front to be understandably told, “Sorry, ladies. You can see if someone in line has extra cash, and then you can Venmo them.” As we were trying to figure out what to do, who else but EHD alum Mel Burstin sees me, says hello, and knowingly asks, “Do you need cash?” Needless to say, she was our angel, and in we went. So before we start looking at the amazing vintage, THANK YOU, MEL!
At the end of the first lane of the market, I saw these guys. Aren’t they outrageous in the best way? Yes. Will they be for everyone? No. And honestly, I wouldn’t necessarily say they are for me. But I could envision the face tables in a really cool garden as these incredible sculptures/tables/planter holders. I forgot to check the price, and to be honest, I’m not sure whether they are safe for the outdoors. But I think they are kind of a perfect introduction to the market as a message that this isn’t your average market. That cabinet is also something that I was drawn to but likely wouldn’t buy if I had the $1,200 to spend. It’s more 80s than I tend to go for, but I love the more organic wood grain with the ultra-modern shape. I could see it in a super high-end home where there’s only one “funky” piece that adds a fun contrast.
This vase was actually the first thing I took a photo of. I knew I didn’t need it (especially for the price), but I was so drawn to the colors, the shape, the hand-painted design. In the right space, this would look so cool.
Something that we noticed was the uptick in fun vintage patio furniture. The photo on the left is a company called Los Resort, and they restore old patio furniture! The shape of that set was so cool. The chair frames are the best. The yellow and white chairs were from a different vendor and cost $45 a chair. I don’t know why, but I feel like these styles could be making a comeback in the next couple of years…
This set had us swooning! This beauty was $3,500 and just unbelievable in person. Franchesca was scheming to try to take it home:) But it was meant for someone else (we hope!) who will give it a good home. The wicker table, while the coolest, I would imagine a tablecloth would be pretty necessary if any food were to be consumed on it. I’m normally not the most worried about “how to clean things” when I’m shopping, but the idea of semi-permanent crumbs in wicker would likely stress me out.
I’ve said it before, and I’ll say it again: I’m going to need a bigger home in the nearish future because there are too many incredible lamps that I need more space for. This one is a great example. MCM Italian (I think) from Modern Thrifter LA, with 2 lights and an ambient reflective plate. It’s a piece of functional art! $395 isn’t cheap, but it’s also not much more than what many major retailers are charging for non-vintage pieces.
Another Italian table lamp that I absolutely loved. It was perfectly small but not too mini. It was $600, which was more than I would have wanted to spend.
I know this one is pretty specific, but I thought it was insanely cool. It’s a late 1970s Vico Magistretti Nemea Lamp, and while $500 is a lot, when I looked online, they often sell for more than double that. 
Now, as I said in the beginning, art was Franchesca’s mission, and I was happy to support. Franchesca’s style tends to lean more classically romantic than mine, but we both love a little playfulness:) This will all make more sense when you see what she bought. I like that Downtown Modernism has more abstract/modern artwork than a typical market, but they also have nearly every style. 
As a reminder, Franchesca and her husband Ben were my friends who lived in the apartment above me for years (and I got to design their bedroom). Well, they were able to buy a home last year (bittersweet, but they are still close!), so they are now in a slow but intentional decorating mode.
There’s my flea buddy surrounded by pieces that really weren’t her style, ha. I really loved that red staircase photo. Should have asked for the price just to see:)
Ok, both of these spoke deeply to her heart. This first one was an original piece in the original frame for $350. The price was a bit more than she wanted to spend, so she passed. The piece on the right was much harder to pass up for her. It’s a piece by Argentine-Italian surrealist artist Leonor Fini. It was stunning, but at $600, Franchesca decided to wait. She got the vendor’s information if she changes her mind:) But please look up Leonor Fini. Her life and work seem pretty remarkable from the little bit I’ve read so far. 
These were just pieces I liked and wanted to share.
This one I really loved, but at $1,025 it was sadly a definite no. Also, I know I’m pointing out a lot of expensive things, but there are also many more affordable pieces there too!
And the prices aren’t getting lower yet. I stopped in my tracks when I saw this. The vendor wasn’t around to ask the price (well, I accidentally asked a shopper, thinking it was the vendor- whoops). Regardless of that, I just wanted you all to see it. The inset handles, the two-tone wood, the open and closed storage – perfect.
I did ask the price for this dining set, and it was $3,750. Honestly, for a table and four chairs, you’d likely spend close to that at a major retailer. But with this, it’s a 1957 by Glenn of California, which was a pioneer in MCM furniture manufacturing from the 40s to the 60s. The vendor told us that this set was a rarer design of theirs. I loved the chairs the most, and that leather seat is original. The lines are so beautiful.
This 1960s Danish teak and oak daybed for $1,875 (with new upholstery) was a great size and really comfortable! It’s from Modern for All.
This settee on the left is proof that there is a variety of styles at Downtown Modernism. We loved the arms and the two-tone look! I spotted those red velvet chairs while Franchesca was at another booth, and I had to snap a photo. The shape is perfectly exaggerated, and I feel like red velvet is a less common type of color/fabric combo for an MCM chair, right? If that’s correct, it needs to happen more. 
THAT BASE. No words. It’s perfect. 
Of course, I needed to stop by artist and vintage seller Melinda Forster‘s booth. It’s always my favorite. I’ve bought two of her ceramic pieces before, but her vintage finds are also so good. No one should be surprised that I love this chair with the circle cutouts 🙂 
Here’s a mix of her work and some vintage. It took a lot of strength not to buy another piece, but I knew I simply didn’t have the right place. Oh, and another great thing I loved about her booth this year was that she had a clearly marked (that’s important) table for items under $30. So nice and helpful!
This was also at Melinda’s booth, and I just thought it was so fun. It’s a 1970s Pierre Cardin Serving Set that was made in Japan out of plastic and mahogany. It has a serving tray, salt and pepper shakers with a caddy, and two pitchers. I didn’t see a price, but when I looked it up, one site is charging $675. I have a feeling hers was a lot less than that, but you never know!
I didn’t need it, but I loved it. It was $75 and would be a really fun napkin holder for a table, right?
I didn’t almost buy this, ha, but I was blown away by it. I’m not sure I’ve ever seen a more modern wood piano like this. Both Franchesca and I couldn’t stop looking at it. The vendor, Furniture Fosters, was selling it for $5,9995. According to them, it’s a Steen Nielsen Hammerspinet, which is a rare, mid-century Danish piano and harpsichord hybrid housed in a Brazilian rosewood case. 10/10.
Boy, do I also know how to find special but expensive pottery. I love that black and white ceramic box a lot. But at $400, I was going to skip. Then those little ceramic sculptures made me smile. The hands are adorable, right? They really weren’t right for me or my home, but at $900, I was sure. 
Now, before I knew the price, this was the first piece I thought about buying. The patina was great, the handle was cool, but that ball nozzle was everything. I mean, it’s so cute but kinda chic, right? Well, once I found out it was $400, it was out of budget, and my sad heart walked away. 
This is a brass “Nike” paperweight, and I loved it. I immediately thought, “Oh, maybe I can get it for my boyfriend! I think he’d like it.” A dangerous new purchasing excuse I need to keep a close eye on, ha. And had it been $20, I might have grabbed it with less consideration. But it was $60. Then I really thought about it and realized that while he would have appreciated me thinking of him, he would likely not have loved it like I did. So I passed…it was close one. 
I didn’t need them, but I hope someone did! SUCH a fun flatware set for $350. 
This was the first purchase of the day from Seoda. Franchesca bought a block print by N. Pollock for $45. It was the exact size she was looking for, and it was already framed in one she really liked!
Her second and final purchase was this sweet cat piece from an art show that celebrates autistic artists. This was near and dear to the vendor. She shared that her son is autistic and is an artist as well. This made it all the more meaningful. She paid $68 for it.
Onto me:) 
I also only made two purchases (which was two more than were on my list). But this book jumped out at me. I loved the cover, the patina, and the photos inside. When he said it was $20, I said, “done!” Did I totally need it? No. But will I need more design books in my future larger home? Yes.
This tray put a spell on me, I swear. I picked it up and fell in love. I love the asymmetrical placement of the opening, the carved lines, and the little feet you can’t really see from this angle. I did put it down, went around the market one more time, and then decided I needed it. I’m sure I could have haggled, but I didn’t and spent $125. It’s now on a nightstand, and I’m very happy.
Has anyone been to any good markets lately? Any favorites? Let us know! Happy thrifting, everyone.

A day after the $14,000 Nikkor Z 120-300mm f/2.8 TC VR S lens announcement, Nikon raised its full-year profit forecast, driven by semiconductor strength and reported an Imaging sales outlook cut (see previous financial reports):

Nikon raises full-year profit forecast by 50%, but trims Imaging Products sales outlook for the second half
Nikon today revised its consolidated financial forecast for the fiscal year ending March 31, 2027, significantly raising profit guidance while lowering its revenue outlook. For the full year, the company now expects:
This marks a major recovery from the prior year’s operating loss of ¥112.4 billion and net loss of ¥86.1 billion. The upward profit revision is driven by stronger-than-expected results in the Precision Equipment Business (especially semiconductor equipment service revenue amid favorable market conditions), along with improved outlooks for the Healthcare and Industry businesses. Additional factors include U.S. tariff refunds and lower selling, general, and administrative expenses from cost containment. Revenue is declining mainly because of a downward revision to the second-half sales plan in the Imaging Products Business (cameras and lenses). This continues the softness noted earlier in the year, including weaker demand (particularly in China) and prior cuts to camera and lens unit forecasts. A key driver of the improved outlook is Nikon’s stronger-than-expected first-half performance. Operating profit for the first half is now expected to reach ¥9 billion, compared with a previously forecast loss of ¥5.5 billion.
The group-wide upgrade does not mean Nikon’s camera business outlook has improved. Nikon actually lowered its second-half operating profit forecast, primarily because of a weaker outlook for the Imaging business. The stronger first-half forecast more than offsets that reduction, lifting the full-year total. In other words, Nikon expects to make more money overall despite forecasting lower sales, while its imaging business continues to face a more challenging outlook.
Nikon also shared a much stronger-than-expected performance for the first half of the fiscal year, swinging from a projected operating loss into solid profitability:
From Nikon:
Regarding the consolidated financial forecast for the first half, revenue is expected to be generally in line with the previous forecast. Operating profit is expected to exceed the previous forecast, driven by higher-than-expected service revenue in the Precision Equipment Business amid favorable semiconductor market conditions, tariff refunds in the United States, and lower selling, general and administrative expenses due to cost containment and the deferral of certain
expenditures. Profit before tax and profit attributable to owners of parent are expected to exceed the previous forecast, mainly due to the improvement in operating profit. Based on these factors, the financial forecast has been revised as described in 1. (1) above.
Regarding the consolidated financial forecast for the full year, revenue is expected to fall below the previous forecast, mainly due to a revision of the sales plan for the second half in the Imaging Products Business. Operating profit for the Group as a whole is expected to exceed the previous forecast. This reflects upward revisions to the profit outlooks for the Precision Equipment Business, the Healthcare Business, and the Industry Business based on the first-half results. Profit
before tax and profit attributable to owners of parent are expected to exceed the previous forecast, mainly due to the improvement in operating profit. Based on these factors, the financial forecast has been revised as described above.

74,2026-10-31,"The richest companies in the history of the world are spending so much on the infrastructure backing AI that they’re burning through billions in cash and threatening a recent AI-fueled stock surge as Wall Street wavers.
The latest financial disclosures by four of the biggest hyperscalers, Amazon, Alphabet, Microsoft, and Meta, as well as Elon Musk’s up-and-coming AI player, SpaceX, which includes xAI, revealed that the small group of tech heavyweights is set to invest as much as $828 billion on capital expenditures this year. That spending includes data centers, high-powered chips, and other infrastructure that fuels AI. 
Making up the $828 billion in capex spending is an expected spend for 2026 of $220 billion from Amazon, up to $205 billion from Alphabet, approximately $170 billion from Microsoft, up to $145 billion from Meta, and up to $88 billion from SpaceX, according to company earnings releases and calls.
At this point, the massive AI infrastructure bill has surpassed the market value of well-known businesses like energy giant ExxonMobil or payments company Mastercard and is even approaching the roughly $1 trillion America is spending on defense this fiscal year, itself the largest in the world. Several banks, including Evercore and Bank of America, see capital expenditure related to AI hitting $1 trillion in 2027.
Partly in response to this massive spending, stocks tumbled on Friday, leading both the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 to their worst one-day performances since June. The tech-heavy Nasdaq closed down 2.9%, while the S&P 500 fell 1.8%. Nasdaq 100 companies lost more than $1 trillion in market value. Sour guidance for the last three months of the year from U.S. chipmaker Intel also played a role.
Some of the market’s jitters can be drawn to concerns over free cash flow, which has long been a source of strength for Silicon Valley companies but has recently taken a turn. Four of the five tech giants spending hundreds of billions cumulatively on capex had negative free cash flow in the third quarter (Microsoft was the exception), meaning they spent more on buildings, equipment, and other capital investments than they generated in cash from their operations.
As companies that built their fortunes selling software and services increasingly pour money into physical infrastructure they are looking to borrow or raise more in some cases to pay for it. With a handful of technology stocks accounting for an outsized share of the S&P 500, the success or failure of these companies’ investments could have enormous implications for the market and the millions of Americans invested in it.
Aleksandar Tomic, an economist and the associate dean at Boston College, warned that these infrastructure investments could lead to consequences far beyond Silicon Valley if they don’t pan out, especially as companies increasingly rely on debt to finance their expansion. This increased borrowing could raise the cost of capital for other businesses, while a downturn among the tech giants could ripple throughout the broader economy.
“If these tech companies sneeze, the rest of the market would catch pneumonia,” Tomic told Fortune.
Microsoft’s recent financials show how keeping up in the AI race is affecting tech companies’ decisions overall.
For decades, the software giant has been one of the world’s biggest cash-generating businesses. It earned nearly $134 billion in profit during its latest fiscal year and generated almost $183 billion in cash from operations. Still, during the same period, its cash spending on property and equipment rose about 80%.
Microsoft announced a $25 billion bond offering Thursday, its first bond sale in more than nine years. The company said it would use the money for general corporate purposes, which it said could include capital expenditures.
Microsoft remained free cash flow positive last quarter unlike competitors, Amazon, Alphabet, Meta, and SpaceX, but as the costs of infrastructure increase, the company is looking to raise funds.
Meta reported negative free cash flow of $2.1 billion last quarter, compared with positive $10.6 billion during the same period a year earlier. On Monday, it announced it would issue new shares in a plan to raise up to $95 billion that will give it more funds it could put toward AI infrastructure.
Oracle, another major player in the AI infrastructure race, is also relying on borrowing and other avenues to support its expansion. The company told investors this week that it doesn’t expect to return to positive free cash flow until the second half of its next fiscal year, which would run from the six months prior to May 2028. After that, the company said, it predicts free cash flow shooting up to at least $30 billion for the fiscal year ending in May 2029.
For now, some big tech companies can point to the encouraging sign that customers are paying more than ever for computing power. Google’s cloud revenue more than doubled last quarter, while Microsoft’s Azure business grew 48% and Amazon Web Services expanded by about 39%.
Cloud providers make money partly by renting compute to AI providers. As AI demand is also projected to grow in the coming years, these operations could be a welcome source of revenue for hyperscalers. Still, not all cloud revenue comes from AI, and rising sales don’t necessarily validate the giant investments of today. 
In other positive news for the AI sector, Claude maker Anthropic filed paperwork for an initial public offering this week, helping to boost the Nasdaq 0.8% on Monday. The filing also drew attention to more than $265 billion in future minimum purchase commitments, mostly for cloud computing and other infrastructure services, which could be a boon for hyperscalers.
Still, overall, companies are betting huge sums of money today to build out the  computing capacity they expect to need years from now, a move that could be prescient but also comes with big risks in the short term.
For investors, the question is increasingly whether AI will become profitable enough to justify the enormous bill. If expensive data centers and chips don’t generate the profits that companies are expecting, the fallout could have consequences that extend well beyond Silicon Valley to the U.S. economy and beyond, said Tomic.
“We do need infrastructure, we do need this capital, but, can they sustain it? I think that’s the big big question,” he said.
Amazon CEO Andy Jassy, whose company on Thursday reported negative free cash flow of more than $42 billion over the past year, made it clear on the company’s third quarter earnings call he sees little choice but to keep spending.
“Would it be more convenient if this type of acceleration and opportunity happened over 25 years and you could spread the CapEx over 25 years,” Jassy asked an analyst during the call. “Yeah, it might be more convenient, but that’s not the world that we live in, and that’s not the opportunity.”
Role: Reporter
Marco Quiroz-Gutierrez is a reporter for Fortune covering general business news.

										Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI) Chairman Tuhin Kanta Pandey addresses the gathering during a session on ‘Building India's World Class Capital Market’, as part of the 125th anniversary celebrations of the Bharat Chamber of Commerce, in Kolkata
																															| Photo Credit:
											Swapan Mahapatra
																			
SEBI Chairman Tuhin Kanta Pandey on Saturday said India’s existing exchange infrastructure is “sufficient” to handle larger volumes. Pandey emphasised that it is now difficult for location-based exchanges to operate, as location is now much less relevant because everything has to be electronically connected.
“I think even the existing exchange infrastructure is sufficient to handle larger volumes also,” Pandey replied to a question from the media about whether the country needs more exchanges, regional or national.
To a set of questions related to the proposed revival of the century-old Calcutta Stock Exchange (CSE), he said the bourse needs to meet conditions such as a net worth of  ₹100 crore and an average annual turnover of  ₹1000 crore, infrastructure for orderly execution of trades, an online screen-based trading system, an online surveillance capability and cyber security controls, among others.
The SEBI Chief said CSE will have to settle outstanding broker dues and establish a separate clearing corporation or tie up with trading corporations for settlement of trade before restarting its operations, as trading has remained frozen since 2013. Also, a business plan has to be there.
“All these conditions are there which need to be met. You need to understand that a market infrastructure institution is one of the most important institutions for market integrity. Today the systems are extremely high-tech. You have to invest a lot in technology. So, any investment of that order obviously will require that the money be recovered in the business plan,” Pandey said, adding an exchange will need to generate sufficient turnover.
“Now, the turnover will depend upon people who volunteer to come and trade (on the exchange). Traders will want liquidity, because if you do not have adequate liquidity, then your impact cost will be very high. So, it is a catch-22 situation,” he pointed out.
Notably, the West Bengal government has proposed to support the revival of the 118-year-old Calcutta Stock Exchange, which is on the verge of closure due to several hurdles, to reclaim Kolkata’s place as a financial capital.
“It is difficult for location-based exchanges. Location is now much less relevant today because everything has to be electronically connected. You not only trade within the country; there are traders who are trading from outside the country. So, in a way, new technologies have made the location much less relevant than earlier when they used to be physical trades,” the SEBI Chairperson said during the media interaction in Kolkata.
He said the capital market regulator is likely to issue the proposed consultation paper within weeks on compliance requirements for foreign portfolio investors (FPIs).
Earlier, in a Bharat Chamber of Commerce event, Pandey said SEBI has taken several steps to make fundraising processes simpler and more efficient as businesses seeking access to capital, time, cost and regulatory certainty matter.
“We have streamlined IPO processes and shortened timelines for rights issues. Listing requirements have been rationalised for large issuers. We have also addressed practical concerns of founders by permitting them, subject to prescribed conditions, to retain certain employee stock options after listing,” he said.
Pandey informed that SEBI is exploring the creation of a capacity-building network for independent directors.
“We feel that capacity development of independent directors is an important issue we should deal with. So, we are working now, and very soon we will have a wider consultation on that. Tentatively, we have an idea to develop a capacity-building network for independent directors, where all the important institutions, professional bodies, academia, and chambers and industry come together,” he said.
To a question, Pandey said SEBI is monitoring the developments at the leadership and governance differences between Tata Sons and Tata Trusts.
“At the holding company level, there are some issues, as that’s not really a listed company. The rest of the companies, those which are listed, follow all the LODR (Listing Obligations and Disclosure Requirements) norms,” he added.

The Waldorf Astoria brand has been on an impressive growth trajectory in recent times, and has opened several new flagship properties. This Hilton luxury brand has just opened its latest hotel, in Kuala Lumpur. It looks beautiful, but I can’t help but wonder if it will succeed. Let me explain…
In this post:
The Waldorf Astoria Kuala Lumpur has opened as of late October 2026. The residential-style, all-suite property features 268 accommodations, ranging in size from 80 square meters (861 square feet) to 745 square meters (8,019 square feet).
The property is set within Kuala Lumpur’s “Golden Triangle” district, and as you’d expect, it’s intended to be an urban retreat with both extensive dining and wellness facilities.
Dining options are headlined by Jean-Georges Vongerichten’s signature market-to-table concepts at JG KL, abc kitchens KL, and The Bar by JG. Then there’s also The Istana (where breakfast is served), Yaari (serving Indian cuisine), and Peacock Alley (the signature Waldorf Astoria bar concept).
The Waldorf Astoria Spa is 2,275 square meters (24,488 square feet), and features La Prairie treatments. Then there’s a fitness center, along with an outdoor pool.
Based on the renderings that have been shared so far, the property looks beautiful, in my opinion.
Kuala Lumpur is one of my favorite hotel markets in the world, in terms of how reasonably priced luxury hotels are, at least by international standards (of course I understand that doesn’t factor in local buying power). The city’s current top hotels, like the Four Seasons and Park Hyatt, can be had for around $300 per night.
With that in mind, what are rates like at the Waldorf Astoria Kuala Lumpur? Well, the hotel is trying to set a new pricing record in the city, and not just by a small margin. I see the absolute lowest rate currently being $479 per night, though seasonally rates are $600+. That’s before taxes and fees, for a base suite.
If you’re booking a cash rate, I’d recommend doing so through the Hilton for Luxury program, which offers extra perks when booking through select travel advisors, including breakfast, a hotel credit, an upgrade subject to availability, and more.
For those redeeming Hilton Honors points, the rate seems to be 100,000 points per night, assuming a standard suite is available. At my valuation of 0.5 cents per Hilton point, that’s like “paying” $500 per night to stay here, inclusive of taxes and fees.
When you consider that standard suites are over 860 square feet, that price probably doesn’t seem unreasonable. You’re getting more than twice the space you’d ordinarily get at the city’s top luxury hotels, so it’s not illogical that the pricing would be higher.
If this hotel had 60 accommodations, I’d say there’s probably a market for that, by just chasing the very top end of consumers who value space. But 268 accommodations?! That’s an absolutely massive number of suites to fill at record prices. I mean, this hotel will have more keys than the Park Hyatt Kuala Lumpur.
I just can’t imagine the hotel will be able to run any sort of decent occupancy with that pricing, and so many suites to fill. Either I’m wrong, or the hotel will have to lower prices, or will consistently run low occupancy.
The other thing is that if you’re not specifically looking for a suite, it’s really hard to compete with the Park Hyatt Kuala Lumpur, which is also new, and a really stellar property. It also creates intrigue among people, given that it’s one of the world’s highest up hotels.
The Waldorf Astoria Kuala Lumpur is now open. The 268-suite property is setting new records in the city, both for entry level room size, and also for pricing. The property looks beautiful, and the dining options also look top notch. However, filling 268 rooms at rates that are 50%+ higher than any existing option will be a challenge. 
What do you make of the Waldorf Astoria Kuala Lumpur? Do you think they’ll be able to get these rates while running decent occupancy?

78,2026-10-31,"
					By Melanie Goodfellow 
Senior International Film Correspondent

EXCLUSIVE: Here’s a nicely timed company announcement for Halloween.

	Dubai-based indie director and writer Faisal Hashmi has officially launched genre-focused production company Spine Drop Pictures and unveiled its inaugural slate of three horror features.

	Hashmi has won fans over his 15-year career with a collection of 10 contained, high-concept genre shorts with chilling twists, including as Amber, Wicken and Skipped, which have screened at scores of film festivals and generated more than three million combined views on YouTube.





	
	
		
					Related Stories		
	













	News
	



	
		
					Dark Sky Films Takes U.S. Rights To Prize-Winning Emirati Horror 'The Vile'		
			















	Festivals
	



	
		
					Dubai Film Festival To Return In December 2027, As 2026 Edition Of Saudi Arabia's Red Sea Film Festival Looks Uncertain		
			







	Alongside directing, he also recently took co-writing credits on Emirati director Ali F. Mostafa’s long-awaited feature City of Life Continuum, a sequel to his breakout 2009 debut City of Life.

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	Hashmi is also attached to write and direct English-language, time-loop horror-thriller Fractal, starring Jason Flemyng and Gabriela Vargas, which is being produced by Vienna-based Pont Pictures and is targeting a 2027 shoot in Serbia.

	Hashmi’s strategy for Spine Drop Pictures is to focus on high concept, commercially oriented genre pictures, budgeted at below $1 million and tapping into his experience of working on a tight budget with his short films.

	“I’ve always looked at how can I tell an amazing story with less locations, fewer resources, but still make the concept so strong that it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to make an expensive movie for cheap, but rather a movie that feels contained and is made in the best way to make it in the first place,” he told Deadline.

	The inaugural slate spans Off Plan, Cinder and Latching, which have been written and will be directed by Hashmi.

Off Plan is an interconnected horror anthology featuring six stories unfolding across Dubai over the course of a single night.

	Hashmi has designed a modular shooting approach so that the six segments can be filmed as separate short-form productions that can be scheduled independently, allowing for greater flexibility and cost efficiency through shared crew and production resources.

	










Cinder is billed as a gritty supernatural thriller set against the unforgiving remote mountain landscapes of the UAE. It expands on Hashmi’s award-winning 2020 short film Amber, in which a barber in a remote UAE highway town finds himself up against a vicious kidnapper who has abducted a young girl. The short won best film at the UAE’s Al Ain Film Festival.

	Hashmi describes Latching as a feminist possession-revenge horror film unfolding over the course of a single harrowing night in Karachi, Pakistan, where it is being developed for production. Longtime UAE resident Hashmi is of Pakistani descent. 

	“We’ve seen what’s possible internationally with the success of low-budget, high-concept horror films like Obsession, Undertone and Good Boy. In an emerging independent film market like the UAE, I think that approach is especially important. We need films that aren’t just creatively ambitious, but commercially viable, with a realistic potential for profitability and international appeal,” continued Hashmi on his strategy. 

	“A sustainable film industry isn’t built on getting one expensive film financed. It’s built on making films that can find audiences, recover their investments and help finance the next one. That’s the thinking behind Spine Drop Pictures.”

	Hashimi says Spine Drop Pictures is exploring opportunities for slate investment, international co-productions, strategic financing and distribution partnerships as it advances its inaugural features.

80,2026-10-31,"
  By Mark Polishuk |
  at October 31, 2026 10:43am CDT  
The Hanwha Eagles will be posting third baseman Si Hwan Roh for Major League teams this offseason, according to multiple reports out of South Korea (including Jeeho Yoo of Yonhap News).  The Eagles have asked the Korean Baseball Organization to post Roh, and the KBO League has subsequently told MLB teams about Roh’s availability.
It isn’t clear if Roh’s 30-day posting window is now open, or if an official posting announcement will come in the next few days.  Once Roh is posted, he’ll have 30 days (shorter than the 45-day posting period for NPB players) to reach an agreement with a Major League team.  If a deal is reached, the Eagles will receive a posting fee worth 20% of the first $25MM of Roh’s contract, 17.5% of the next $25MM, and $15% for anything beyond $50MM.
The timing of Roh’s posting is particularly noteworthy this offseason since the collective bargaining agreement expires on December 1, and a lockout is expected to follow.  If Roh and other KBO/NPB players posted this winter haven’t signed with a big league team by December 1, they’ll be caught up in the lockout transactions freeze just like their MLB counterparts.  In Roh’s case, the shorter 30-day window probably allows for a greater understanding of his market, as most or even all of his posting period (depending on when it begins) could be up by December 1.
Should a posted player not find an acceptable deal before or after their window expires, he’ll return to his former club for the 2027 season.  There’s an added wrinkle in Roh’s situation, as he would be returning to the Eagles to begin an 11-year contract extension signed last February.  That extension begins with the 2027 season, though it included a provision that Roh will first be posted for MLB teams this offseason.  If Roh does indeed move to the majors, he is also contractually required to return to the Eagles whenever he should return to KBO upon the conclusion of his MLB tenure.
The 11-year contract will pay Roh $30.7 billion won, or a little over $23MM as per the current conversion rate beween the won and the U.S. dollar.  With some significant financial security already lined up, Roh has some leverage in fielding offers from Major League teams, though an MLB club could conceivably offer him a lower sum than $23MM but in a shorter timeframe.
The KBO League is generally considered to be a rung below Nippon Professional Baseball, and as such there is less of a track record of players (particularly hitters) making the successful transition from South Korea to the majors.  Roh’s market will hinge on how many big league clubs feel he has the tools to produce against higher-level pitching, though there’s some intrigue given how well Roh has hit in his home country.
Roh made his KBO debut in 2019 at age 18, and he’ll enter free agency in advance of his age-26 season.  He put up decent numbers (particularly for a player of his young age) in his first four seasons, and then broke out as a star over his last four years of action.  His career-best 32 homers in 2025 helped carry the Eagles to the Korean Series, and the 2026 season saw Roh play on South Korea’s World Baseball Classic team, and hit .283/.360/.505 with 29 homers over 571 PA for the Eagles.
In the field, Roh has been a full-time third baseman for the Eagles over the last four seasons, though he has played first base in the past and during the WBC.  If MLB evaluators feel Roh can provide at least decent glovework at the hot corner, it’ll boost his value in a free agent market that is pretty thin on regular third base options.
