81,2026-10-31,"The lock held. The money left through the side door.
On Thursday, someone stole $6.3 billion from Binance, the world’s largest crypto exchange. Most of it left in about 20 minutes.
It’s the largest crypto theft ever and roughly four times the previous record, the $1.5 billion stolen from Bybit in 2025. The BBC’s cyber correspondent called it probably the biggest single theft of any kind.
Ten days before the hack, I published the last chapter of Zero Knowledge, my novel about three founders whose algorithm breaks the cryptography that crypto (and almost everything else) runs on. Insert whatever “truth is stranger than fiction” or “life imitates art” cliché comes to mind.
In chapter 9 of Zero Knowledge, a Bitcoin wallet from 2010 moves for the first time in twenty years, and a fictional exchange called Tessera starts posting to its status page. The first update says “All customer funds are safe and accounted for.” Twenty-one minutes later, Tessera pauses Bitcoin withdrawals. Forty-seven minutes after that, it pauses deposits and withdrawals on everything.
On Thursday, Binance paused withdrawals and deposits. It then turned deposits back on about four hours later.
On Friday, Binance’s co-CEO Richard Teng said:
We never use any customer funds so they are not impacted.
By chapter 36, Tessera has halted trading. A customer who sold all her Bitcoin months ago can’t get the $38,000 in cash she needs to close on a house. She waits six hours in the support queue to reach an agent named Ruth L., who keeps repeating the same line about customer funds. Then:
Customer: What did you do with yours.
Ruth L.: I moved mine on Tuesday.
As I write this, withdrawals are still paused. Binance’s latest update says they’ll start coming back in phases “within approximately 34 hours, subject to the completion of security checks,” and that the results of its proof of reserves will be out by Monday, November 2.
Based on what Binance and the people tracking the money have said so far, here is how the hack worked.
Binance says it started with a compromised laptop belonging to a DevOps engineer, and that it has since found “additional points of breach.” The attackers used an Ethereum feature called EIP-7702, which lets an ordinary wallet hand control of its behavior to a piece of code by signing an authorization. Binance’s own hot wallet - the wallet an exchange keeps online to pay out withdrawals - signed 50 of those authorizations in a single transaction, each one pointing at a contract the attacker controlled. Teng said the transfers “did not pass through our normal approval controls.”
None of it was improvised. Ari Redbord of TRM Labs told the BBC that the attacker funded an operator wallet 36 hours before the theft and had the signatures prepared in advance. Teng said the attackers used a “self-destruct mechanism” that destroyed their tools and traces as they went.
TRM Labs and Chainalysis both say they have high confidence it was North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, also known as Lazarus. Edward Howell, an Oxford expert on North Korea, told the BBC that 40 to 50% of North Korea’s weapons of mass destruction programs are funded by cyber and crypto theft.
One antagonist in Zero Knowledge is Sable, a patient foreign operative working for a government I never name. Sable prepares for a long time and then moves fast. While I made Sable up, TraderTraitor is real, is associated with North Korea, and appears to have just stolen $6.3 billion.
While we don’t know with confidence exactly what happened or how it will resolve, it appears no one broke the math, which is a central theme in Zero Knowledge. One of the main protagonists, Samantha, who is the co-founder and CTO of Basalt Labs, invents an algorithm using a quantum computer that breaks the public-key cryptography under Bitcoin, banking, and software updates. In the book’s appendix, How It Actually Works, I wrote that the real damage from a break isn’t that secrets become readable. The damage is that “the world can no longer tell a true thing from a lie that’s signed correctly.” A forged signature is “a real signature now, just made by the wrong person.”
That’s what happened at Binance, without a quantum computer. Binance’s hot wallet produced valid signatures, and every one of them checked out. The BBC’s explainer pointed out that “the blockchain wasn’t hacked,” only the exchange was. That’s true, and Vlad (another of the protagonists in Zero Knowledge and the invisible co-founder of Basalt Labs) would have something to say about that. Late in the book, he learns the hard way that the absence of a break is not the presence of safety.
Thirty-seven minutes after the main sweep, Tether froze about $971 million of its USDT stablecoin sitting in the attacker’s main wallet. Three minutes later, Circle froze $823 million of USDC in the same wallet, “in coordination with law enforcement.” Ripple, World Liberty Financial, and Arbitrum’s Security Council froze more. Within a day, about $3 billion was frozen. So about half of the money got saved by companies with a button that freezes your money. The other half - more than 420,000 ETH and 18,270 BTC, roughly $2.4 billion according to TRM - is gone, because native Bitcoin and Ethereum don’t have an issuer, and nobody can freeze them.
One of the key pitches of crypto is that nobody can freeze your money. Go reread the last paragraph. Hmmm…
In chapter 36, a Bitcoin developer named Petra proposes a patch that would make 4.1 million exposed coins unspendable so nobody can steal them. Another developer shoots it down, telling her she’s proposing to “confiscate from the dead as a safety measure.” In real life, Tether didn’t need consensus. It needed 37 minutes.
And then there’s SAFU.
Binance has an emergency fund called SAFU, the Secure Asset Fund for Users. It’s about $1 billion. The hack was $6.3 billion. Binance says it has set aside $6.34 billion of its own money to cover the loss, and by Friday night it said it had already replaced about 80% of the stolen coins with the same coins and was buying the rest on the market. Its co-CEO Yi He pointed out that $3 billion is only about 2% of Binance’s user assets and reserves. In case anyone was still worried, Binance’s founder, CZ, told Elon Musk, who was mocking the hack on X, that it was a “bad time to troll” and that Binance’s SpaceX shares are worth more than $1 billion. Yi He then replied to CZ in public: “Reporting to the major shareholder: Our company’s revenue for the year is sufficient to cover the losses, so we do not yet need to sell the SpaceX shares you are bullish on.”
Isn’t this kind of insane? A company lost more than six billion dollars on a Thursday and announced on Friday that it would cover the whole thing out of its own pocket. According to CNBC, Binance was holding about $145 billion the day before the hack, so this was about 4% of it. There’s no FDIC behind SAFU. There’s Binance, which is the insurer, the insured, and the one publishing the proof of reserves on Monday.
Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor, put it well in that CNBC piece: “A pledge to make customers whole is only as strong as the resources behind it.”
In chapter 54, a credit union IT person in Greeley spends a Friday calling 63 banks, trying to get 412 paychecks of about $1,400 each accepted. A woman whose paycheck disappeared posts “is money real” and gets four million views. One reply quotes a Fed statement promising that deposit insurance is unaffected and asks “why would you say that unless one was about to.” I wrote that because reassurance is often what scares people. This week, Binance’s reassurance was $6.34 billion, and Bitcoin slid from about $80,000 to a six-week low of about $76,400. The BBC called that a sign the market has matured. Or maybe it is numbness. Or suspension of disbelief.
In 2023, I wrote a post titled I Don’t Hate Crypto. I still don’t, and it’s been economically good to me.
But I think crypto might be fucked.
The industry made two promises. The math makes it secure, and nobody in the middle can take your money or stop it from moving. This week the math held, and it didn’t matter, because the keys live on computers on networks administered by people with laptops. The decentralized part worked exactly as designed, which is why $2.4 billion of North Korea’s money can’t be touched. What saved Binance’s customers was the opposite of crypto: centralized companies freezing tokens, and a centralized company with an enormous balance sheet writing a check.
Now add the thing Zero Knowledge is about. This hack needed a compromised laptop, 36 hours of preparation, and a gap in one company’s controls. A quantum break needs none of those. Something like a quarter to a third of all Bitcoin sits in addresses whose public keys are already written on the chain for anyone to read. The fix, BIP-360, was accepted into Bitcoin’s proposal repository earlier this year, isn’t live, and even when it is, it can’t force old coins to move. If the largest exchange in the world can’t keep its own hot wallet’s signing authority away from one engineer’s laptop, I’m not optimistic that the world finishes migrating to new signatures before someone like Sable shows up.
My entire shitty first draft, all 137 chapters, is free at zeroknowledge.ink. I’m about to start working on my second draft (in public) once I get a few more pieces of AuthorMagic finished so I can do this using my own software.
I wonder what is going to happen next week.
Subscribe via RSS or visit the subscribe page."

2026 was the year of peak camera — and that camera was the A7R VI

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Against a backdrop of component shortages and price hikes, 2026 has been a challenging year for camera companies, and it's been a quieter year for new gear as a result. That being said, we were still treated to some cracking cameras — but standing tall above them all was the Sony A7R VI, with a new 66.8MP full-frame stacked sensor, Bionz XR 2 processor, Sony's latest subject-detection autofocus, and its first new battery for 10 years.It's a formidable camera whatever your genre of photography, and won both our Best Professional Camera and overall Camera of the Year awards for 2026 at the TechRadar Product of the Year Awards 2026. Here's what makes the A7R VI so special, in my opinion.
 



It's possible that I got carried away slightly when I said the A7R VI “had no weaknesses”, but after weeks testing the full-frame mirrorless camera across landscape, portrait and wildlife photography, I had no doubt in my mind that it deserved a perfect 5-star rating.
I’ve been reviewing cameras for almost two decades, and the A7R VI genuinely feels like peak camera. In my A7R VI review, I was even compelled to say “Sony can stop making cameras now.”
The A7R VI is a powerful all-rounder, as opposed to a pure pixel monster
First up, I must point to the new sensor, which had an admittedly modest bump in pixels from the already class-leading A7R V’s 61MP sensor, to 66.8MP. But that’s not the exciting part about this sensor — it’s also a ‘stacked’ type with a faster read-out speed.
Faster read-out speeds can improve a camera’s performance in multiple ways, not least by reducing rolling shutter (an ugly distortion effect that appears when recording video and taking photos of high-speed subjects).
For me, I preferred having the smaller bump in resolution and a greater emphasis on speed, as opposed to an even bigger bump in resolution but no speed improvement. The A7R VI is a powerful all-rounder, as opposed to a pure pixel monster.
The camera packs Sony’s Bionz XR 2 processor, which combines two chips, including one that’s tasked with subject-detection autofocus. Whether I was photographing small birds or large mammals in a nature reserve, or taking portraits, the A7R VI never skipped a beat.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
 



Then there’s the camera’s handling. Historically, for me Sony's design and user experience haven't matched those of other leading mirrorless camera brands — I’m a sucker for Nikon in particular. But the A7R VI is spectacularly well designed.
There’s a tilt-and-flip touchscreen, meaning the screen can be pulled out and used as a tilt screen for waist-level shooting, or flipped out and rotated for selfies — traditionally you’d have to pick either / or (or have neither). It’s the most versatile design on the market, and one which you can also find on the latest Lumix S1 II cameras. If I had one wish, it would be that Sony increased the size of its 3-inch display in the future, even if only to 3.2-inches like Nikon.
The viewfinder is big, bright and highly detailed class-leading 9.36m-dot display, while the button layout makes perfect sense, and the camera’s grip is nice and deep and comfortable to hold.
Housed inside the grip is an all-new battery — Sony’s first for 10 years since the legendary NP-FZ100. The new unit has a higher capacity, and increases shot life by 20%; not bad, considering the previous battery already delivered class-leading power.
The downside to the new battery is that it’s not forwards / backwards compatible with the NP-FZ100, so upgraders are going to have to splash out on additional batteries should they need them.
 



Video specs are decent, though arguably not as strong as those of some lower-resolution rival cameras. For example, Sony again omitted open-gate recording (this would finally appear later in the yet-to-go-on-sale FX5 cinema camera).
But if photography is your beat, and you need a high-resolution sensor with huge scope for cropping — the A7R VI’s Super 35 (APS-C / 1.5x crop) mode is still a healthy 28MP — and don’t need the absolute top speed and buffer performance of cameras like the A9 III and A1 II, there really is no better camera.
The A7R VI was so good, in fact, that it put the (much) pricier A1 II’s position in doubt, given that it can do 90% of what that camera can, while boasting a higher-resolution sensor.
I reviewed some fabulous cameras in 2026, but my absolute favorite was without doubt the A7R VI — it’s the camera I wish I owned, along with some of Sony’s spectacular recent pro GM lenses. It was the crowning achievement for Sony in a year in which it also scooped our Brand of the Year award, and comfortably maintained its #2 market position behind Canon.

He also started a media team in Nairobi, Kenya, where he lived for a few years volunteering for a faith-based organisation. 85,2026-10-31,It is understood the young adult was in critical condition on life support in a metropolitan Adelaide hospital as of Saturday morning.
86,2026-10-31,"David Murphy
Economics and Public Affairs Editor
Over the past few months, there have been positive noises about the housing market.
Unfortunately, the housing crisis is far from over. That is still years away.
Like the banking crisis, problems built up over a long period and will take years to resolve.
There is a shift under way, however it is a number of relatively small moves in the right direction. Here are some of them:
The number of new homes built between July and September of this year is up 10.4% on last year.
So far in 2026 there have been almost 27,000 dwellings finished.
Analysts predict between 39,000 and 40,000 will be completed by the end of the year, compared to a total of 36,000 in 2025.
It's an indication of progress - but still well short of the 50,000 to 60,000 needed to meet pent-up demand estimated at 250,000 to 300,000 homes.
The Government scrapped annual targets and instead said it wanted to see 300,000 homes built between 2025 and 2030.
To achieve that, a substantial increase in annual output is needed.
Figures from the banks show the number of first-time buyer mortgages is up 9% year on year.
There have been 22,600 home loans taken by first-time buyers so far this year.
There is a 7% rise year on year in the number of people drawing down mortgages to move from one home to another.
That indicates that there is more liquidity in the market.
Home buyers are not just buying new houses, the market for older homes is important too.
There is slightly more positive news here as well. In September, 14,600 properties were listed for sale on MyHome, up 12.5% year on year, as the supply of new listings finally improves.
The market has been contracting over recent years as potential sellers hesitated due to concerns about finding another home.
Soaring property prices is one of the symptoms of huge demand and lack of supply.
This time two years ago prices were rising by 10% per annum. That increase has now slowed to 5%. Economists say it will decelerate further to 3% or 4%.
Nobody is forecasting a drop in property prices because demand remains strong and Central Bank rules have prevented banks from over-lending.
The market may be cooling off for a number of reasons. Higher interest rates usually dampen house price growth.
The European Central Bank hiked rates from 2% to 2.5% over recent months with another rate rise expected in December.
But for many buyers it has reached the point where affordability is now at or beyond their limit.
The Government has introduced significant policy changes to encourage investors by altering building standards, reducing VAT on new apartments and relaxing rent controls.
Opposition parties have been critical of these steps. They point out that tenants have been affected by the new rules.
It comes as homeless numbers hit a record of 17,885 people in emergency accommodation.
Unsurprisingly, the new measures have been welcomed by the construction industry.
There is a lot the Coalition needs to do to lift the output of new housing above the critical 50,000 threshold.
One of the dangers is that progress could stall because of changes in the wider economic environment.
The construction industry is facing higher inflation, rising interest rates and geopolitical uncertainty which may make investors hesitant before financing new projects.
Goodbody Stockbrokers chief economist Dermot O'Leary, who was a member of the State's Housing Commission, said several policy levers need to be pulled by the Government to step up progress.
He said these include ""continuing fiscal supports for the social housing sector and home ownership"" and ""faster progress on rezoning of residential land"".
Mr O'Leary also calls for a ""coordinated response"" on the provision of utilities such as water and electricity connections to ensure sites can be developed.
In addition, he said there needs to be further work to reform the planning system.

Economics and Public Affairs Editor

Follow@davidmurphyRTE

OpenRouter has also started prioritizing speed over cost.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Darkbloom, a decentralized AI inference network that aggregates and sells spare compute capacity from Macs, told its members to expect revenues to fall as token prices continue to drop. According to The Register, the price per million tokens for Anthropic dropped by 77% in under five months, falling to $0.62 in October 2026 from $2.65 in May.
 




“Data centers have become cheaper. Output per NVIDIA cluster is up ~20x this year, and their margins are wide enough to cut prices 90 percent and still profit at 50 percent,” said Kydo, a researcher from Eigen Labs, the developer of the compute platform. “The market caught up to our price. We could go lower, but then most of you would earn even less [than] nothing.”
It’s estimated that someone running an M5 Pro Mac Mini with 64GB of Unified Memory could earn as much as $192 per month. This is the best-case scenario, though; the median earnings of the top 50 operators on Darkbloom’s leaderboard are about $159.86 per month. This translates to about $5.26 daily or $1,918.50 annually. However, if we account for Anthropic’s 77% token price drop, earnings will fall to a measly $36.77 per month, or about $1.23 per day.
AI token prices are falling so fast that they've even outpaced Moore’s Law, with some reports saying AI is becoming 50% cheaper every quarter and up to 13 times more affordable every year. News of companies blowing through their annual AI budgets in months has less to do with per-token price and more with the number of tokens, as agentic AI use outpaces token price drops. Aside from that, OpenRouter, Darkbloom’s primary client, has started prioritizing decode speed, quantization, and prefill speed, putting users without a top-of-the-line Max or Ultra Apple chip at a disadvantage.
Despite that, Darkbloom says it will not sit idly by and let some users lose out because of these changes. The network is analyzing model demand to advise members on which models are in higher demand right now. It’s also looking at other kinds of workload beyond inference, including distributed CI/CD jobs, improving network performance to better compete against other computer providers/aggregators, and finding other clients outside of OpenRouter.

88,2026-10-31,"Most Widely Read Newspaper
Dangote Petroleum Refinery. Photo: Dangote
Kindly share this story:
The Dangote Petroleum Refinery exported an average of 54.63 million litres of petrol, diesel and aviation fuel daily in September 2026, exceeding the 39.98 million litres supplied to the Nigerian market.
Findings showed that the refinery’s export volumes overtook domestic receipts for the first time in the two months under review.
The development came as the refinery operated at 105.98 per cent of its installed capacity, its highest utilisation rate in the January–September period, highlighting the growing role of the $20bn facility in the international petroleum products market even as Nigeria continues to grapple with concerns over domestic fuel availability.
Data from the September 2026 fact sheet of the Nigerian Midstream and Downstream Petroleum Regulatory Authority analysed by our correspondent on Saturday showed that combined exports of the three products increased by 37.33 per cent to 54.63 million litres per day from 39.78 million litres per day in August. 
In contrast, domestic receipts declined by 22.08 per cent to 39.98 million litres per day from 51.31 million litres per day in the preceding month.
The figures mean that the refinery exported 14.65 million litres more than it supplied to the domestic market daily in September.
This reversed the situation in August, when domestic receipts exceeded exports by 11.53 million litres per day.        
The shift occurred despite an increase in production, with the refinery raising its combined output of Premium Motor Spirit, Automotive Gas Oil and aviation turbine kerosene by 15.68 per cent to 97.67 million litres per day from 84.43 million litres.
The latest figures also showed that the refinery had operated at or above 100 per cent of its installed capacity in four months in 2026, namely May, June, August and September.
Its utilisation rate stood at 101.25 per cent in May and 101.36 per cent in June before dropping sharply to 71.09 per cent in July following maintenance on its crude distillation and residue fluid catalytic cracking units.
Operations recovered to 105.21 per cent in August and climbed further to 105.98 per cent in September.
The refinery had operated at 99.12 per cent in April, after recording 61.27 per cent in January, 78.13 per cent in February and about 93.62 per cent in March.Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources
The September utilisation rate implies that the facility processed approximately 689,000 barrels of crude oil daily, above its installed capacity of 650,000 barrels per day.
However, the refinery management says its capacity has been upgraded to 700,000 barrels per day. 
   
The NMDPRA data indicated that the refinery received about 618,000 barrels of crude daily during the month, meaning that the difference of approximately 71,000 barrels was sourced from existing refinery inventories.
A breakdown of the September figures showed that petrol production increased by 12.59 per cent to 47.22 million litres daily from 41.94 million litres in August.
However, petrol exports rose by 58.99 per cent to 15.47 million litres per day from 9.73 million litres, while domestic receipts fell by 20.55 per cent to 28.50 million litres from 35.87 million litres.
The refinery also increased diesel production by 35.04 per cent to 24.32 million litres daily from 18.01 million litres.
Diesel exports surged by 81.83 per cent to 15.91 million litres per day from 8.75 million litres, while domestic receipts dropped by 34.20 per cent to 8.14 million litres from 12.37 million litres.
Consequently, diesel exports were almost twice the quantity supplied to the domestic market.  Related News  Evening recap: Sowore disputes police account, FG begins N50,000 support, other top stories  Nigeria, Russia move to replace 59-year-old aviation deal  NSCDC busts illegal diesel refining site in Kano, arrests five suspects   
Aviation fuel exports also remained significant, rising by 9.15 per cent to 23.25 million litres daily from 21.30 million litres in August.        
Domestic receipts increased slightly to 3.34 million litres from 3.07 million litres.
Exports accounted for 87.44 per cent of the refinery’s aviation fuel evacuation in September, making the product the biggest contributor to the overall export dominance.
Although domestic receipts of petrol and diesel combined remained higher than their exports, the difference narrowed considerably.
The refinery supplied 36.64 million litres of the two products daily to the domestic market, against exports of 31.38 million litres.
The gap fell to 5.26 million litres daily in September from 29.76 million litres in August.
The export surge has raised fresh questions about the relationship between rising domestic refining capacity, petroleum products exports and the availability of fuel in the local market.
Notably, the refinery’s daily petrol production of 47.22 million litres exceeded Nigeria’s average daily petrol consumption of 44.4 million litres in September. 
   
However, only 28.50 million litres of its output entered the domestic market, while 15.47 million litres were exported and 3.25 million litres added to refinery stocks.
The regulator’s figures showed that domestic refining met about 65 per cent of national petrol consumption during the month.
Nigeria’s average daily petrol receipts from domestic refineries and imports fell by 23.37 per cent to 38.7 million litres in September from 50.5 million litres in August.
Petrol imports also declined by 30.14 per cent to 10.2 million litres daily, which the NMDPRA attributed to the exhaustion of approved import permit volumes.
Diesel receipts recorded a sharper decline, falling by 40 per cent to 8.7 million litres daily, the lowest level in the 13 months covered by the fact sheet.
This was below average daily consumption of 14.5 million litres, with the regulator attributing the decline to low imports and domestic evacuation amid high prices.
The country’s petrol stock sufficiency stood at 23.9 days in September, below the NMDPRA’s 30-day benchmark.        
Diesel stock sufficiency also declined to 49.7 days from 51.6 days in August.
The figures highlight the complexities of Nigeria’s downstream petroleum market following the removal of petrol subsidies and the emergence of the Dangote refinery as a major supplier.
The 700,000-barrel-per-day refinery, which began producing petrol in September 2024, was expected to reduce Nigeria’s dependence on imported petroleum products, conserve foreign exchange and improve domestic supply.
However, the September data show that increased production does not necessarily translate into equivalent domestic deliveries, as exports, inventory management and market pricing also determine how much of the refinery’s output reaches Nigerian consumers.
The challenge for the government and petroleum regulators is to ensure that the expansion of domestic refining capacity translates into reliable local supply, adequate stock levels and competitive prices while allowing refiners to participate in international markets.

The 700,000-barrel-per-day refinery, which began producing petrol in September 2024, was expected to reduce Nigeria’s dependence on imported petroleum products, conserve foreign exchange and improve domestic supply.
However, the September data show that increased production does not necessarily translate into equivalent domestic deliveries, as exports, inventory management and market pricing also determine how much of the refinery’s output reaches Nigerian consumers.
The challenge for the government and petroleum regulators is to ensure that the expansion of domestic refining capacity translates into reliable local supply, adequate stock levels and competitive prices while allowing refiners to participate in international markets.
                    
In contrast, domestic receipts declined by 22.08 per cent to 39.98 million litres per day from 51.31 million litres per day in the preceding month.
The figures mean that the refinery exported 14.65 million litres more than it supplied to the domestic market daily in September.
This reversed the situation in August, when domestic receipts exceeded exports by 11.53 million litres per day.        
The shift occurred despite an increase in production, with the refinery raising its combined output of Premium Motor Spirit, Automotive Gas Oil and aviation turbine kerosene by 15.68 per cent to 97.67 million litres per day from 84.43 million litres.
The latest figures also showed that the refinery had operated at or above 100 per cent of its installed capacity in four months in 2026, namely May, June, August and September.
Its utilisation rate stood at 101.25 per cent in May and 101.36 per cent in June before dropping sharply to 71.09 per cent in July following maintenance on its crude distillation and residue fluid catalytic cracking units.
Operations recovered to 105.21 per cent in August and climbed further to 105.98 per cent in September.
The refinery had operated at 99.12 per cent in April, after recording 61.27 per cent in January, 78.13 per cent in February and about 93.62 per cent in March.Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources
The September utilisation rate implies that the facility processed approximately 689,000 barrels of crude oil daily, above its installed capacity of 650,000 barrels per day.
However, the refinery management says its capacity has been upgraded to 700,000 barrels per day. 
   
The NMDPRA data indicated that the refinery received about 618,000 barrels of crude daily during the month, meaning that the difference of approximately 71,000 barrels was sourced from existing refinery inventories.
A breakdown of the September figures showed that petrol production increased by 12.59 per cent to 47.22 million litres daily from 41.94 million litres in August.
However, petrol exports rose by 58.99 per cent to 15.47 million litres per day from 9.73 million litres, while domestic receipts fell by 20.55 per cent to 28.50 million litres from 35.87 million litres.
The refinery also increased diesel production by 35.04 per cent to 24.32 million litres daily from 18.01 million litres.
Diesel exports surged by 81.83 per cent to 15.91 million litres per day from 8.75 million litres, while domestic receipts dropped by 34.20 per cent to 8.14 million litres from 12.37 million litres.
Consequently, diesel exports were almost twice the quantity supplied to the domestic market.  Related News  Evening recap: Sowore disputes police account, FG begins N50,000 support, other top stories  Nigeria, Russia move to replace 59-year-old aviation deal  NSCDC busts illegal diesel refining site in Kano, arrests five suspects   
Aviation fuel exports also remained significant, rising by 9.15 per cent to 23.25 million litres daily from 21.30 million litres in August.        
Domestic receipts increased slightly to 3.34 million litres from 3.07 million litres.
Exports accounted for 87.44 per cent of the refinery’s aviation fuel evacuation in September, making the product the biggest contributor to the overall export dominance.
Although domestic receipts of petrol and diesel combined remained higher than their exports, the difference narrowed considerably.
The refinery supplied 36.64 million litres of the two products daily to the domestic market, against exports of 31.38 million litres.
The gap fell to 5.26 million litres daily in September from 29.76 million litres in August.
The export surge has raised fresh questions about the relationship between rising domestic refining capacity, petroleum products exports and the availability of fuel in the local market.
Notably, the refinery’s daily petrol production of 47.22 million litres exceeded Nigeria’s average daily petrol consumption of 44.4 million litres in September. 
   
However, only 28.50 million litres of its output entered the domestic market, while 15.47 million litres were exported and 3.25 million litres added to refinery stocks.
The regulator’s figures showed that domestic refining met about 65 per cent of national petrol consumption during the month.
Nigeria’s average daily petrol receipts from domestic refineries and imports fell by 23.37 per cent to 38.7 million litres in September from 50.5 million litres in August.
Petrol imports also declined by 30.14 per cent to 10.2 million litres daily, which the NMDPRA attributed to the exhaustion of approved import permit volumes.
Diesel receipts recorded a sharper decline, falling by 40 per cent to 8.7 million litres daily, the lowest level in the 13 months covered by the fact sheet.
This was below average daily consumption of 14.5 million litres, with the regulator attributing the decline to low imports and domestic evacuation amid high prices.
The country’s petrol stock sufficiency stood at 23.9 days in September, below the NMDPRA’s 30-day benchmark.        
Diesel stock sufficiency also declined to 49.7 days from 51.6 days in August.

Exports accounted for 87.44 per cent of the refinery’s aviation fuel evacuation in September, making the product the biggest contributor to the overall export dominance.
Although domestic receipts of petrol and diesel combined remained higher than their exports, the difference narrowed considerably.
The refinery supplied 36.64 million litres of the two products daily to the domestic market, against exports of 31.38 million litres.
The gap fell to 5.26 million litres daily in September from 29.76 million litres in August.
The export surge has raised fresh questions about the relationship between rising domestic refining capacity, petroleum products exports and the availability of fuel in the local market.
Notably, the refinery’s daily petrol production of 47.22 million litres exceeded Nigeria’s average daily petrol consumption of 44.4 million litres in September. 
   
However, only 28.50 million litres of its output entered the domestic market, while 15.47 million litres were exported and 3.25 million litres added to refinery stocks.
The regulator’s figures showed that domestic refining met about 65 per cent of national petrol consumption during the month.
Nigeria’s average daily petrol receipts from domestic refineries and imports fell by 23.37 per cent to 38.7 million litres in September from 50.5 million litres in August.
Petrol imports also declined by 30.14 per cent to 10.2 million litres daily, which the NMDPRA attributed to the exhaustion of approved import permit volumes.
Diesel receipts recorded a sharper decline, falling by 40 per cent to 8.7 million litres daily, the lowest level in the 13 months covered by the fact sheet.
This was below average daily consumption of 14.5 million litres, with the regulator attributing the decline to low imports and domestic evacuation amid high prices.
The country’s petrol stock sufficiency stood at 23.9 days in September, below the NMDPRA’s 30-day benchmark.        
Diesel stock sufficiency also declined to 49.7 days from 51.6 days in August.
The figures highlight the complexities of Nigeria’s downstream petroleum market following the removal of petrol subsidies and the emergence of the Dangote refinery as a major supplier.
The 700,000-barrel-per-day refinery, which began producing petrol in September 2024, was expected to reduce Nigeria’s dependence on imported petroleum products, conserve foreign exchange and improve domestic supply.
However, the September data show that increased production does not necessarily translate into equivalent domestic deliveries, as exports, inventory management and market pricing also determine how much of the refinery’s output reaches Nigerian consumers.
The challenge for the government and petroleum regulators is to ensure that the expansion of domestic refining capacity translates into reliable local supply, adequate stock levels and competitive prices while allowing refiners to participate in international markets.
                    
The refinery had operated at 99.12 per cent in April, after recording 61.27 per cent in January, 78.13 per cent in February and about 93.62 per cent in March.Make PUNCH part of your everyday experience.Add us to your Preferred Sources
The September utilisation rate implies that the facility processed approximately 689,000 barrels of crude oil daily, above its installed capacity of 650,000 barrels per day.
However, the refinery management says its capacity has been upgraded to 700,000 barrels per day. 
   
The NMDPRA data indicated that the refinery received about 618,000 barrels of crude daily during the month, meaning that the difference of approximately 71,000 barrels was sourced from existing refinery inventories.
A breakdown of the September figures showed that petrol production increased by 12.59 per cent to 47.22 million litres daily from 41.94 million litres in August.
However, petrol exports rose by 58.99 per cent to 15.47 million litres per day from 9.73 million litres, while domestic receipts fell by 20.55 per cent to 28.50 million litres from 35.87 million litres.
The refinery also increased diesel production by 35.04 per cent to 24.32 million litres daily from 18.01 million litres.
Diesel exports surged by 81.83 per cent to 15.91 million litres per day from 8.75 million litres, while domestic receipts dropped by 34.20 per cent to 8.14 million litres from 12.37 million litres.
Consequently, diesel exports were almost twice the quantity supplied to the domestic market. Related News  Evening recap: Sowore disputes police account, FG begins N50,000 support, other top stories  Nigeria, Russia move to replace 59-year-old aviation deal  NSCDC busts illegal diesel refining site in Kano, arrests five suspects   
Aviation fuel exports also remained significant, rising by 9.15 per cent to 23.25 million litres daily from 21.30 million litres in August.        
Domestic receipts increased slightly to 3.34 million litres from 3.07 million litres.
Exports accounted for 87.44 per cent of the refinery’s aviation fuel evacuation in September, making the product the biggest contributor to the overall export dominance.
Although domestic receipts of petrol and diesel combined remained higher than their exports, the difference narrowed considerably.
The refinery supplied 36.64 million litres of the two products daily to the domestic market, against exports of 31.38 million litres.
The gap fell to 5.26 million litres daily in September from 29.76 million litres in August.
The export surge has raised fresh questions about the relationship between rising domestic refining capacity, petroleum products exports and the availability of fuel in the local market.
Notably, the refinery’s daily petrol production of 47.22 million litres exceeded Nigeria’s average daily petrol consumption of 44.4 million litres in September. 
   
However, only 28.50 million litres of its output entered the domestic market, while 15.47 million litres were exported and 3.25 million litres added to refinery stocks.
The regulator’s figures showed that domestic refining met about 65 per cent of national petrol consumption during the month.
Nigeria’s average daily petrol receipts from domestic refineries and imports fell by 23.37 per cent to 38.7 million litres in September from 50.5 million litres in August.
Petrol imports also declined by 30.14 per cent to 10.2 million litres daily, which the NMDPRA attributed to the exhaustion of approved import permit volumes.
Diesel receipts recorded a sharper decline, falling by 40 per cent to 8.7 million litres daily, the lowest level in the 13 months covered by the fact sheet.
This was below average daily consumption of 14.5 million litres, with the regulator attributing the decline to low imports and domestic evacuation amid high prices.
The country’s petrol stock sufficiency stood at 23.9 days in September, below the NMDPRA’s 30-day benchmark.        
Diesel stock sufficiency also declined to 49.7 days from 51.6 days in August.
The figures highlight the complexities of Nigeria’s downstream petroleum market following the removal of petrol subsidies and the emergence of the Dangote refinery as a major supplier.
The 700,000-barrel-per-day refinery, which began producing petrol in September 2024, was expected to reduce Nigeria’s dependence on imported petroleum products, conserve foreign exchange and improve domestic supply.
However, the September data show that increased production does not necessarily translate into equivalent domestic deliveries, as exports, inventory management and market pricing also determine how much of the refinery’s output reaches Nigerian consumers.
The challenge for the government and petroleum regulators is to ensure that the expansion of domestic refining capacity translates into reliable local supply, adequate stock levels and competitive prices while allowing refiners to participate in international markets.

Damilola Aina is a journalist at Punch Newspapers with over five years of experience covering energy, business, investment, infrastructure, and property sectors. He specializes in producing well-researched and insightful reports that inform readers and provide clarity on complex topics. All three of the Rangers’ mutual options will be bought out, reports Shawn McFarland of The Dallas Morning News. ESPN’s Jesse Rogers first reported this afternoon that designated hitter Joc Pederson passed on his end of an $18.5MM option. Catcher Kyle Higashioka will receive a $1MM buyout in lieu of a $7MM salary; reliever Jakob Junis is paid a $1.25MM buyout on a $5MM option.
McFarland’s report doesn’t specify which side formally declined the Higashioka/Junis options, yet it’s safe to assume the team passed on both. Neither player was much of a factor down the stretch, and Texas is clearly working against payroll limitations.
Higashioka wraps up a two-year, $13.5MM free agent deal. He combined for a .233/.296/.391 slash line with 18 homers across 513 plate appearances. That’s perfectly serviceable for a part-time catcher. Higashioka has a solid reputation as a receiver but has been below average at throwing out baserunners. He didn’t play after July 20 due to a flexor strain in his forearm.
It’ll almost certainly be a one-year deal this time around, as Higashioka hits the market in advance of his age-37 season. The Phillies, Yankees, Diamondbacks, Cubs, Brewers, Marlins, Reds, Red Sox, Twins and Tigers could all be in the market for a backup catcher. Texas will also need to address the position. Elias Díaz is a free agent, leaving Danny Jansen and Logan O’Hoppe as an underwhelming catching tandem.
Junis could look for a sixth different team in the past five seasons. The 34-year-old righty has bounced around the league on a series of one-year contracts. Junis typically puts up solid results behind plus control and a heavy diet of sweepers. He sits in the low-90s with below-average strikeout stuff but has managed four consecutive seasons with a sub-4.00 earned run average.
That includes 54 1/3 innings of 3.64 ERA ball for Texas this year. Junis led the club with 18 holds and even recorded six saves, briefly holding the ninth inning before Jacob Latz seized the job with an All-Star season. It was a nice overall return on investment for a $4MM free agent pickup. The production was heavily frontloaded, however. Junis took a 2.80 ERA across 35 1/3 frames into the All-Star Break. He battled left hip and adductor issues in July and August and wasn’t effective upon returning, giving up 11 runs with more walks than strikeouts in 19 second-half innings.
I really think people are sleeping on O’Hoppe. With all due respect to LAA, they rushed him to the big leagues. He had the chance to go to AAA and work on things after the trade and he was crushing the ball.
Agreed, O’Hoppe was a rising star in Anaheim. I was shocked when he was traded. I’d go with an O’Hoppe/Jansen tandem.
Why would the Tigers need Higashioka? That doesn’t make sense with their current catching tandem and Briceno waiting in the wings.
I wonder if a junis/Giants reunion is in the cards. Send like the kind of guy they need and at a good price point
Someone paying Pederson more than that?
I doubt anyone will per season but if he can go out and sign for 2/$30M or 3/$42M or something he’s better off opting out. He’s 34, it’s probably his last multi-year contract opportunity.
Ofc if he signs for 1/$10M two-weeks into Spring Training, which at least feels like it’s in the realm of realistic outcomes, it’ll be a mistake, but I get it. He’s basically coming off the 3rd best season at the plate he’s ever had and that’s after a slow March/April. From May 1 on, he hit .238/.327/.525 with 24 HR and a 136 wRC+. He had a 137 wRC+ in the second half.  He’s in the same tier of hitter as Suzuki but will be a lot cheaper. More flaws, ofc, being a platoon bat that can’t play defense, but he’ll have plenty of suitors this offseason, and should be able to snag 2/$30 from someone who misses out on the top hitters of the class.
And he’ll most likely pull a Jorge Polanco/Harrison Bader and be much worse in 2027. Teams never learn though.
With the uncertainty surrounding the CBA, this was not a wise move on Pederson’s part.

It wasn’t a player option. It was a mutual option. Texas would not have picked up their end. He lost nothing by declining his end.
It was a mutual option. The team wouldn’t pick it up.
Mutual options are accounting technicalities, it doesn’t really matter who declines it. The whole point is to shift the buyout onto 2027’s books (like business books, not payroll circumvention).
Of course it matters who declines it. Players don’t decline their end to spare the team from having to decline it. And teams don’t decline their end to spare the player from having to decline it.
It really doesn’t though, the Red Sox declined Sonny’s but you can’t tell me they would not have taken him back at that price. A mutual option hasn’t been accepted in 13 years, it’s a formality.
Just bc some writers on MLBTR say it’s a formality and they never get picked up doesn’t make that true. Every offseason there are one or two that are possible options that will get picked up by both sides. They are rarely picked up. They aren’t never picked up. 
The last option excercised by both sides was Aramis Ramirez in 2014.

The idea that they’re automatically declined and nothing more than a formality is a dumb narrative pushed by this site for a decade, but it’s just not true. You can be certain it’s a narrative with an agenda being pushed by the fact that this site has been saying it for that long, essentially since right after the last mutual option was picked up. 
And Sonny Gray aside, that’s one example and it’s the wrong direction – idk if it’s what happened but a team might decline their side if they know the player will anyways bc they have 5-days to exclusively negotiate a new contract with that player and they decline it as part of their extension talks, which is a scenario that doesn’t apply to a player declining their side – many, MANY players excercise their option only to have the team decline their side. Players never decline their side to spare the team. Find ONE mention from Pederson saying that’s what he did.

I’m sure you can’t. But try. And when you can’t, maybe start thinking for yourself instead of letting some rando writers on MLBTR tell you how and what to think.

The short-term outlook turned negative for the stock of Cochin Shipyard (₹1,114.20) as it slipped below the crucial support level.
It now finds support at ₹1,096. A conclusive close below this will trigger a fresh fall on the stock. The next major support is at ₹892.
On the other hand, the stock finds an immediate resistance at ₹1,200 and a major one at ₹1,320. Only a close above the latter will alter the current bearish trend on Cochin Shipyard.
Overall, we expect the stock to move downward with occasional spikes.
F&O pointers: The counter witnessed a rollover of 90 per cent to November series, which is in line with previous months. Its November futures closed at ₹1,116.60 against the spot price of ₹1,114.20.
The counter shed open interests on Friday when the stock fell sharply by over 3 per cent. This signals unwinding of long positions. Currently, open interest stands at 54.24 lakh shares for Cochin Shipyard, a decline of over 2 lakh shares last week.
Option trading indicates that the stock could move between ₹1,100 and ₹1,200. 
Strategy: Traders can consider going short on Cochin Shipyard futures now. We advise traders to keep a stop loss at ₹1,123 initially and can trail it down as the price drops. Traders can aim for a target of ₹1,096.
As the market is volatile and Cochin Shipyard being a high beta stock, we advise traders to use stop loss judiciously.
Stay away from this strategy if the stock opens above ₹1,135 or below ₹1,100 on Monday.
This strategy is strictly for traders who can understand the risk involved in trading futures and also meet margin commitments in case of wild price swings. So, risk-averse traders can skip this.

91,2026-10-31,"Yves here. Rob Urie examines both the geopolitical debate and much more pressing, the issues in play in the upcoming US midterms. He highlights the long-running shifts in Western capitalism, as in the successful effort to tamp down labor bargaining power, and the resulting rise of oligarchs. Rob highlights that neither party has any interest in reducing their hold on power, which would be necessary to reverse societally-destructive policies.
By Rob Urie. Originally published at The Journal of Belligerent Pontification
The essay follows this quick note:
Dear Readers, I’ve received inquiries both friendly and hostile over shifting from writing about geopolitics to US politics and AI. The problems that I am trying to address in doing so are 1) information that used to be available in the US regarding geopolitics is either no longer available or increasingly unreliable, 2) articles on geopolitics published exclusively on Substack were being censored to the point where they are invisible and 3) if the politics in the US don’t get fixed, the entire world is in trouble.
For reasons that I will not speculate about here, the Western Left’s take on the Russia / Ukraine conflict reads like it comes straight from CIA press releases. I do not believe that this is the actual source. But the determination behind the historical amnesia that supports the Left view hasn’t been moved one inch in the five years of my trying. An analogy would be reporting on the Vietnam War that treated it as a contest between North and South Vietnam. That framing is so irrelevant as to appear insincere.
I am still paying close attention to the geopolitics and will resume writing about international affairs in the near future. But the emergency at present is getting the US sorted out. Thank you for bearing with me here.
________________________________________________________________________
With current polling suggesting that the Democrats will win the House and possibly the Senate in the upcoming midterms, the longer trajectory of American politics is brought into focus. With the US currently engaged in two wars and one genocide; the wholesale destruction of the institutions that make life livable in the US, and the economic empowerment of a predatory oligarch class, most of what Americans call politics isn’t on the ballot.
As has been the practice for more than a few decades, Donald Trump ran as a political outsider, threatening to blow the system up if elected. Ordinary DC chair warmers, as the collectors of political donations to support oligarchic control over the rest of us, represent the institutionally-mandated mode of ‘governing’ in the US. Trump came to the job with a list of long-discredited ideas that less intrepid souls would have understood to be both idiotic and self-destructive.
One possible way to consider this contest is as between the political parties. One party supports one flavor of policies and the other party favors another. As should be evident at this point in history, that system put in place a tiny group of oligarchs making really bad decisions for the rest of us. Crypto grifts and AI? This is what happens when a ‘heads, they win, tails we lose,’ power dynamic is in place. The good news for the few is bad news for the rest of us.
Should the pollsters have the election predictions right, the first order of business will be securing the results. Up through about Bush v. Gore this impulse would have been bipartisan. At the time the myth of American democracy had been well sold. That the US was founded as a Constitutional Republic explains quite a bit about why it is today an oligarchy. Giving power to those pursuing their own interests leads to power being used in pursuit of those interests.
That the representatives of both parties appear in public surrounded by ‘their’ oligarchs should provide a clue as to who wields power in the US. That the fortunes of these oligarchs came through inheritance and / or dubious business endeavors should provide a clue as well. One unifying factor has been CIA venture capital funding through In-Q-Tel. We, the people, have the CIA to thank for Oracle, Palantir, and a host of AI startups.
In 1925 US President Calvin Coolidge gave us “the chief business of the American people is business.” He was referring to the industry that built the heartland. While it is a mistake to romanticize the brutal labor relations of US industry, post-War prosperity made it possible to perceive a crude unity in a national interest. That war profiteering and the seizure of foreign economic resources became the point of US foreign policy required decades to re-instantiate.
‘Business’ hasn’t been the business of the American people for at least the last half a century. Just getting by has been. The question now is whether to blame politicians or economics for the shift? On the one hand, politicians make economic policies. On the other hand, the special circumstance of the US having the only intact industrial infrastructure following WWII was temporary. The ‘American Century’ was an artifact of history. That Americans were told that the advantage was permanent explains some fair portion of the political rage of today.
By the 1970s the writing was on the wall. The American infrastructure advantage began to wane. Imports from Japan were climbing the value chain from trinkets to consumer goods. American industrialists shifted from labor hoarding to searching abroad for poor, desperate workers who wouldn’t contest poverty wages with no benefits. The claim that brutal labor relations were the key to American prosperity led to the passage of NAFTA (1994) and the elevation of finance as a disciplining factor for labor.
The brutality towards labor emerges from the capitalist theory of capital accumulation. Capitalist economics posits that the social savings that fund capitalist investment produce a benefit for both capital and labor. But US economic evolution since the 1970s tells a different story. Capital understood that the post-War infrastructure advantage was over early on. The temporary truce between capital and labor was turned back into a zero-sum game where capital saw its future in treating labor as prey.
The wisdom of this move is a matter of perspective. The fast money crowd turned the US into a casino. Fortunes are made or lost making ‘bets’ on the direction of financial markets. That US markets have been openly manipulated since the 1987 stock market crash (‘Greenspan put’) is a matter of public record. That billionaires are minting their own currencies (‘crypto’) gives the power of the state to unaccountable individuals and institutions.
This shift of state power to oligarchs is why political resolution in the US won’t be found anytime soon. The likely 2028 candidates are differentiable in rhetoric but not in policy. The wars to be fought are the same. The genocides are the same. State violence against anti-genocide protesters began in 2023, not 2025. Within the current configuration of power, ‘give ‘em enough rope’ is the alternative to direct confrontation.

Graph: while readers are likely familiar with this graph, interpretation requires slight knowledge of statistics. Statistical averages (in orange) are prone to over-representing extreme concentrations (kurtosis) of wealth relative to what most people experience. In the graph this explains why the orange bars keep rising higher relative to the blue bars. Interpretation is that the concentration of wealth in the US has risen relative to what most people experience since the early 1980s. This is evidence that the US is an oligarchy. Source: inequality.org.
The current impediment to rebuilding a functioning economy is that China and other economic competitors have already built the ‘factories of the world.’ In order to compete the US needs a new entry point. The new factories that could have been located in the US and employed American workers were built by other nations. The ineffectiveness of recent US efforts to re-industrialize is a function of decades of underinvestment. As China learned decades ago, building roads that run from factories to export facilities is how they get built.
That the Americans perceived a ‘unipolar moment’ (1991) as the US economy was being systematically dismantled demonstrates the power of delusional thinking. Many of us at the time were incredulous that US military production was shifted to geopolitical rivals just as US belligerence and poorly considered militarism were rising. Surely, no one was so stupid as to expect that geopolitical rivals would export arms to the US as they were being attacked by it.
On this last point, I was wrong. Market ideology that had little to no explanatory power with respect to US economic success up to that point was put forward as uncontested fact. Having accepted a project in college where I traced the reach of the Western imperial powers into Africa, the neoliberal ‘free-market’ trade routes followed the old imperial trade routes quite closely. What this means is that US ‘free-market’ economics is imperialist dogma.
Donald Trump’s homicidal / suicidal resistance to regulating AI is a function of this ideology. Recall the neoliberal theory that corporate-friendly regulation is the secret to unleashing the animal spirits of capitalism? Today it means handing state-level destructive capacity to any psychopath with a computer that decides that s/he has had enough of this world. Why would would-be school shooters put themselves at risk when they can send AI agents to cut off the town’s water supply?
The US had the same opportunity as China and other economic competitors to invest and it chose to give the money to the oligarchs instead. While China was building an export-led economy the people running the US were arguing over the size of tax cuts for billionaires and how to ‘shrink the size of government.’ Lest this shock, this is the very same ‘abundance’ theory recently being touted by self-styled centrists in the US. That it is recycled Right-wing dogma from the 1980s locates it on the ideological spectrum.
That Reagan represented the hard-Right when he was in office but his policies are considered ‘the pragmatic center’ in the present illustrates the conundrum. Why would those of us who opposed these policies in the 1980s vote for them in 2026 unless we changed our minds about them? As both legacy parties bleed voters, the evidence has it that lots of people haven’t changed their minds. The question then is why self-styled centrists don’t just vote for the party that represents their politics? The answer back is that they do. It just doesn’t fit neatly into any coherent notion of a political center.

Graph: remarkably, nearly every name on this graph of billionaires whose wealth has ballooned in recent years is a recipient of Federal largesse, known colloquially as welfare. And ironically, almost every one of them claims to be a self-made person who thinks that welfare for the poor should be abolished. This absence of self-awareness would make them dangerous to the oligarchy if it didn’t define it. Source: inequality.org.
Part of what is perpetually odd about American politics is that we are constantly told what we care about but never asked. Iran having nuclear weapons? Israel is the state that never should have been allowed to have them. Iran is ruled by a rational government. Israel is populated by genocidal and apocalyptic religious zealots. To the ‘Israel controls the US crowd,’ Trump just forbade Netanyahu to attack Iran to save his (Netanyahu’s) election campaign. And the Israeli government then stepped in to prevent Netanyahu from doing so.
Regarding what Americans want from politicians and the state, this is always posed in terms of new costs rather than net benefit. Current ads claim that Medicare for All (an inadequate solution) would ‘cost taxpayers $400 billion’ when those who have looked into the matter offer that it would save $1 trillion per year. Equitable public schools would likely require Federal funding. But we would get in return an educated populace that possesses the tools needed to thrive in the world. And again, it is oligarchic welfare recipients upset that their looting might be hindered by public investment who object to these policies.
More broadly, Americans seem to expect absolutely nothing from the politicians that they vote for. This leaves us complaining about half-baked and wholly inadequate campaign promises that go unfulfilled instead of insisting that we get the policies that we need and have already paid for. The suggestion here is that a barely negotiable set of policies be proposed to get us out of this cycle of reacting to inadequate proposals
· Education: state-funded pre-K through post-Doc for all comers. End the Charter schools and redistribute the money to public schools. And use Federal money to raise and equalize the quality of education through equalizing the funding.
· Healthcare: a public system where healthcare is fully state funded for all humans living within the US. All healthcare will be free at the point of provision. End the health insurance industry and create jobs for the workers displaced.
· Childcare: create a childcare authority to manage a national network of free at the point of service childcare facilities whose purpose is to free parents to work. Hire local workers at a living wage with benefits. Put child safety and wellbeing first.
· National defense: end the network of 800+ US military bases handing the land back to the nations in which they are located. Dissolve the US standing army and cut the Pentagon budget by 85% – 95%. If a military emergency arises, draft people in order of their family wealth with the richest being drafted first. This isn’t punitive. It is to prevent the rich from starting wars from which they benefit economically.
The usual pushback at this stage is panic-mongering about the Federal budget deficit. How can ‘we’ possibly afford these luxuries when we have pointless and murderous wars to fight, banks and bankers to bail out, and tax cuts for billionaires to pay for?
What is missing in the budget hawk panic is that it was private debt that caused the GFC (Global Financial Crisis), not public (Federal). Non-financial private debt (to prevent double counting) is currently $45 trillion versus $40 trillion for the public debt. And the Federal government of the US issues its debt in USD which it can create at will. Private companies get charged with counterfeiting if they do this.
The tales of sovereign defaults under these conditions are far and few between. The best known, Russia in 1998, involved a pegged currency. The point is that it was the peg that was the problem, not the debt. The US has a floating rate currency. It isn’t pegged and hasn’t been since the US left the Gold Standard under Nixon.
FWIW, my Democrat friends are motivated in the current election and my Republican friends aren’t. This jibes with news reports on early voting. More Democrats than Republicans are showing up at the polls. But Trump has already put down legal markers for contesting the election results. He had his chance to govern pretty much unimpeded and he made a mess of it. The problem of ICE at polling places so far hasn’t materialized to my knowledge. I voted without incident. The larger challenge at present will be to get past Trump’s legal impediments.
The immediate problem of an AI induced financial crisis has been averted for now with the announcement that AI company Anthropic will IPO (Initial Public Offering) in late November. This should provide some breathing room until the next AI induced crisis appears. If we can get Trump out of the way a tiny vacation from the existential dread that he has been causing may be possible. But recall that he is delivering 40,000 2,000lb bombs to Israel of the sort that flattened Gaza. This won’t be over until it is over.
Maybe it’s simply what Jamie Galbraith calls “entropy economics” and the old order is wearing out and was already doing so when Reagan brought us the first MAGA in the 80s. And so our ever more bizarre hyper wealthy have to go to greater extremes to assert their dominance of a system that is cracking up. Some of them even think they can make themselves immortal or, in Trump’s case, carry on his narcissism beyond the grave by erecting monuments to himself.
Change will come, but with the planet itself under threat this time is different from all the other times. IMO we humans need to take a much closer look at ourselves–all of us. It’s time to get a grip.
Rob Urie great article especially cutting the military budget by 95 percent plus closing all of the foreign US military bases and if we go to war draft the rich first. These are common sense ideas not farfetched and once we do his plan then there is
Money for socialized medicine
Money for jobs programs
Money for education
Money for conservation mass transit
This would be New Deal Part Two or Henry Wallace as VP succeeding FDR as opposed to Dr. Strangelove Harry Truman
I think that at least in the Middle East,  Iran will be making the decision about our keeping open or abandoning our military bases in the region.
It seems any comprehensive look at the current state of things actually falls far short of comprehensiveness. There are no longer pockets of isolation from the global dynamics of humans vs. the planet we live on. We are all components of the stew being cooked in a pot we are all part of. Sorry for that analogy. My point is there are too many moving parts for even a very informed person to wrap its head around. And none of those parts are isolated. Everything is very linked currently. So many socioeconomic analysis carry no consideration of the accelerating environmental catastrophe we are feeding. We are literally throwing fuel on the fire that will consume us. I myself am using lots of concrete, which is environmental destructive, not to mention using lots of diesel and gasoline and installing solar to build a parachute for my heirs. At the same time I am a news junky and involve myself in the pursuit of solutions which involve the impossible task of educating my fellow humans to the conundrum we face. Maybe AI can save us by destroying us.
Poland should grasp the nettle. Pexit!!!

Quick comment before I dive into the body of the article:
For reasons that I will not speculate about here, the Western Left’s take on the Russia / Ukraine conflict reads like it comes straight from CIA press releases.

Although to be fair, Bernie Sanders has been mouthing off Dem Party tropes on the Ukraine issue, so maybe that’s what we are talking about? 
Again, not being snide (for once), I genuinely would like to know whom the accusative finger is being pointed at.

The Western Left would be the Left press and the institutions that are publicly proclaimed to represent the Left like the Democratic party. Whether you or I agree is irrelevant. Most readers of most US publications make that association.
I’ve written about Russia / Ukraine since 2013. The pushback that I got after February 2022 from publishers came straight from the CIA. The Ukrainian military wasn’t fascist because Zelensky was Jewish. And Russia committed a ‘full-scale’ invasion of Ukraine. This was the CIA’s line in the press and the line put forward in article after article in the Left press. Jacobin was particularly propagandistic in this regard.
If you really don’t know this, you have virtually everything written in the US press since 2022 as evidence of my claim.
I think the argument is that none of this is truly Left, that part of why the oligarchy has been so successful is that the Left voices have been suppressed and replaced by a bunch of lifestyle liberals. Meaning professional managerial class types who make bleating noises about wealth inequality and claims to want “Medicare for all” before they go back to parroting their CIA and billionaire-funded foundation scripts. In the fourth paragraph after his quick note, Urie writes,

The first sentence about the myth of American democracy is true. The second sentence is not true. The third sentence is an important clue to why Urie’s second sentence is wrong.
It is true that the US was founded as a Constitutional Republic. Urie does not really attempt to explain what it is about a Constitutional Republic that leads inevitably to oligarchy, which he clearly implies. The key here is to understand that the very core of the republican experiment is to create and sustain institutions that will constrain, limit, penalize and when needed neutralize and even eliminate “those pursuing their own interests” to the detriment of the General Welfare. This boils down to governmental institutions and capabilities able to overide, redirect, and sometimes eliminate economic forces. In other words, to regulate the economy. 
The richly funded (by the rich) creation of the conservative and libertarian movements over the past nine decades has been explicitly aimed at crippling and eliminating the governmental institutions and capabilities able to regulate economic actors. Basically, to allow the rich to further engross the nation’s wealth through economic activity that does not conduce to the General Welfare. Indeed, to “GIVE power to those pursuing their own interests so that they may pursue those interests unimpeded by considerations of the republic and the General Welfare. 
The core of conservative and libertarian ideology is the insistence that such unregulated economic activity, through the magic of the marketplace, will result in the greatest good for the greatest number, and this ought be how the General Welfare is defined. In short, the core of conservative and libertarian ideology is fundamentally hostile to the core of republican ideology.
There are many authors and researchers who have documented the history of this, focusing on the opposition to Franklin Roosevelt, the Mont Pelerin Society, the Powell memo, the corruption of American christianity, etc., etc.

“Taming Democracy: “The People,” the Founders, and the Troubled Ending of the American Revolution”
Terry Bouton
https://academic.oup.com/book/47710
Abstract
“Americans are fond of reflecting upon the Founding Fathers, the noble group of men who came together to force out the tyranny of the British and bring democracy to the land. Unfortunately, as Terry Bouton shows in this highly provocative first book, the Revolutionary elite often seemed as determined to squash democracy after the war as they were to support it before. Centering on Pennsylvania, the symbolic and logistical center of the Revolution, Bouton shows how this radical shift in ideology spelled tragedy for hundreds of common people. Leading up to the Revolution, Pennsylvanians were united in their opinion that “the people” (i.e. white men) should be given access to the political system, and that some degree of wealth equality (i.e. among white men) was required to ensure that political freedom prevailed. As the war ended, Pennsylvania’s elites began brushing aside these ideas, using their political power to pass laws to enrich their own estates and hinder political organization by their opponents. By the 1780s, they had reenacted many of the same laws that they had gone to war to abolish, returning Pennsylvania to a state of economic depression and political hegemony. This unhappy situation led directly to the Whiskey and Fries rebellions, popular uprisings both put down by federal armies. Bouton’s work reveals a unique perspective, showing intimately how the war and the events that followed affected poor farmers and working people. Bouton introduces us to unsung heroes from this time–farmers, weavers, and tailors who put their lives on hold to fight to save democracy from the forces of “united avarice.” We also get a starkly new look at some familiar characters from the Revolution, including Benjamin Franklin, Alexander Hamilton, and George Washington, who Bouton strives to make readers see as real, flawed people, blinded by their own sense of entitlement. Taming Democracy represents a turning point in how we view the outcomes of the Revolutionary War and the motivations of the powerful men who led it. Its eye-opening revelations and insights make it an essential read for all readers with a passion for uncovering the true history of America.

“But the emergency at the present is getting the US sorted out.”
I guess for me that type of real sorting out has to start with US history.
Most importantly, from my perspective, is the fact that the American Left and Right are not the
European Left and Right transplanted.  
The American Right had no aristocracy to defend and the American Left no ancient regime to overthrow.
Sombart’s famous question–why there is no socialism in America has a stack of standard answers (the frontier, mobility, ethnic fragmentation of the working class, winner take all elections and above all race–and every item on that list helped to shape what the Left could become.
The lineages of the US left seem to consist of three strands.  The oldest is the producerist-democratic strand consisting of Paine, the radical edge of Jeffersonianism, the Workingmen’s parties of the 1820s, the Jacksonian anti-bank politics, the Knights of Labor, Henry George’s land movement, the 1880s Populists, Debs’s Socialist Party at its 1912 peak, the CIO, and the Sanders revival.
The second lineage is the moral-evangelical strand: abolitionism, the social gospel, the settlement houses, and the civil rights movement, basically the Protestant conscience politicized.
The third strand is the managerial-progressive strand, the Progressive era experts, and the New Deal administrators, the stream that becomes the Progressive Managerial class.
The American Left’s recurring internal war is between the producerist-democratic strand and the managerial-progressive strand or populists vs. progressives.
It is also the case that every promising Lefty coalition–the Populists in the 1890s, the New Deal exclusions, the 1960s crack-up–was broken on the color line.

										OpenAI has cut its fundraising target by $10 billion as an AI stock selloff and rival Anthropic PBC’s impending public listing complicate its efforts to secure investor commitments.
																															| Photo Credit:
											Dado Ruvic
																			
OpenAI has cut its fundraising target by $10 billion as an AI stock selloff and rival Anthropic PBC’s impending public listing complicate its efforts to secure investor commitments, the Financial Times reported.
The ChatGPT maker is now seeking about $20 billion at a $1.25 trillion valuation, down from $30 billion at a $1.4 trillion valuation last month, the FT said, citing people with knowledge of the matter. 
Some investors have reduced their commitments to OpenAI’s funding round, according to the report. The scaled-back ambitions reflect growing investor unease over the enormous sums being poured into artificial intelligence. The Nasdaq 100 fell 3.5 per cent on Friday.
Bloomberg News reported last week that OpenAI was seeking to raise capital at a $1.25 trillion valuation during the final stages of negotiations.
A group of investors from the United Arab Emirates, including state-backed MGX, is expected to be the largest participant in the round, the FT said. SoftBank Group Corp. and venture capital firms including Sequoia Capital, Coatue Management, Founders Fund and Andreessen Horowitz also plan to participate, according to the report.
OpenAI, Sequoia, Founders Fund and SoftBank declined to comment, the report said. MGX, Coatue and Andreessen Horowitz did not immediately respond to a request for comment, according to the Financial Times.
The fundraising effort faces additional competition from Anthropic, which is preparing to go public next month at a valuation of at least $1.5 trillion, the newspaper said. OpenAI, which raised $122 billion in March, is seeking fresh capital to finance its vast computing ambitions as it prepares for a potential IPO of its own next year, according to the report.

A clever African grey parrot named Rocco went viral after being caught using Alexa to secretly order snacks and toys from Amazon while home alone.
Rocco, who lives in the United Kingdom with his owner Marion Wischnewski, mastered the smart assistant by mimicking human speech. He successfully added items like strawberries, broccoli, ice cream treats, and toys to shopping lists, and even attempted to place full orders.
His owner first noticed something unusual when random items started appearing in her Amazon cart. After some investigation, she discovered that Rocco had been chatting with Alexa whenever he was left unsupervised.
Originally rescued by the National Animal Welfare Trust, Rocco’s mischievous intelligence has captivated people worldwide, highlighting just how clever and playful African grey parrots can be.
